Chapter 2 The Clinical Interview, the Mental Status Examination and Case Formulation

In this chapter
  1. 2.1 The Clinical Interview: Purposes, Types and Structure: distinguish interviews by purpose and by degree of structure, and name the major diagnostic interview schedules and what each is for.
  2. 2.2 The Craft of Interviewing: recognise and produce each microskill, from attending and open questions to confrontation and interpretation, and adapt the interview to children, older adults, resistant or suicidal clients and other cultures.
  3. 2.3 The Case History: lay out a full psychiatric case history in its standard order and explain what each heading is meant to capture.
  4. 2.4 The Mental Status Examination I: Appearance, Behaviour, Speech, Mood and Affect: describe what the clinician observes before a single symptom is asked about.
  5. 2.5 The Mental Status Examination II: Thought and Perception: classify disorders of the stream, form, content and possession of thought, and of perception.
  6. 2.6 The Mental Status Examination III: Cognition, Judgement, Insight and the Written MSE: test the cognitive functions at the bedside, grade insight and write an MSE.
  7. 2.7 Risk Assessment: Suicide and Violence: weigh risk and protective factors, use and criticise the SAD PERSONS mnemonic, and assess violence risk.
  8. 2.8 Behavioural Assessment: carry out a functional analysis and choose among self-monitoring, observation and recording methods, knowing their errors.
  9. 2.9 Case Formulation: build a 5 Ps, biopsychosocial, cognitive-behavioural and psychodynamic formulation, and tell formulation from diagnosis.
  10. 2.10 Classification as a Clinical Tool: state what DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 are for, measure diagnostic reliability with kappa and weigh the costs of labelling.

Weightage. 15 of the 422 questions mapped in this book (3.6%) test this chapter, 8 of them from real past papers. 1 of its 10 sections is High yield.

In February 1969, a psychology professor then teaching at Swarthmore College, near Philadelphia, walked into the admissions office of a psychiatric hospital in Pennsylvania and complained that he had been hearing a voice. The voice was unclear, he said, but it seemed to say "empty", "hollow" and "thud". He gave a false name and a false occupation. Everything else he told the admitting clinician about his life, his parents, his marriage and his moods was, he later wrote, true. The professor was David L. Rosenhan (1929 to 2012), who moved to Stanford University in 1971 as professor of psychology and law, and he was perfectly well.

He was admitted. So, over the next few years, were seven other sane volunteers: a psychology graduate student in his twenties, two more psychologists, a paediatrician, a psychiatrist, a painter and a housewife. Between them they presented at 12 hospitals in five states on the east and west coasts of the United States, some shabby state institutions and one well-staffed private hospital. Every one of them was admitted. All but one were diagnosed with schizophrenia, and the exception received a diagnosis of manic-depressive psychosis. The moment they reached the ward, the "pseudopatients" stopped reporting the voice and behaved exactly as they normally did. Their hospital stays still ranged from 7 to 52 days (an average of 19), and those with schizophrenia were discharged not as "well" but as "schizophrenia, in remission".

What happened inside the wards is the part that students remember. The pseudopatients took notes openly; a nurse recorded that the "patient engages in writing behavior". When one paced the corridor out of sheer boredom, a nurse asked whether he was nervous. When a group of patients waited outside the cafeteria half an hour before lunch, a psychiatrist told his residents that this showed the "oral-acquisitive" nature of their illness. The ordinary, slightly mixed relationship one pseudopatient described with his parents was rewritten in his case summary as long-standing ambivalence in close relationships. The staff never detected an impostor. Several of the real patients did: during the first three admissions, 35 of the 118 patients on the admission wards voiced their suspicion, one of them saying, "You're not crazy. You're a journalist, or a professor."

Rosenhan published the study in the journal Science in 1973 under a title that has never been forgotten: "On being sane in insane places." His conclusion was blunt: "It is clear that we cannot distinguish the sane from the insane in psychiatric hospitals." Was he right? Was the problem the label, or the way the information behind the label was gathered and interpreted? Keep the eight pseudopatients in mind as you read this chapter. Every tool in it, the interview, the case history, the mental status examination, risk assessment, behavioural assessment and case formulation, exists in part to stop a clinician from doing what those admitting clinicians did: hearing one symptom, reaching for one label and reading everything afterwards through it. We will come back to the pseudopatients at the end of the chapter.

One more introduction before we begin. Throughout the chapter you will meet a young man called Ravi. He is a composite, built from the kind of client who walks into any outpatient clinic in Karnataka; he is not a real person. Following one client from the first handshake to the written report is the only way to see how the separate tools of assessment fit together.

Meet Ravi (a composite case)

Ravi is 24, the son of a farmer from a village near Mandya. He has a B.Com degree and has spent two years in a Mysuru coaching centre preparing for bank examinations. Three months ago he failed the examination for the third time, in the same week that his cousin was selected. His mother brings him to the clinic because he barely leaves his room, sleeps badly and, last week, told her that "everyone would be better off without me".

2.1 The Clinical Interview: Purposes, Types and StructureMedium yield · 2 Qs

If you could keep only one tool of clinical psychology, you would keep the interview. Tests, rating scales and observation all matter, but nearly every assessment begins with two people in a room talking, and most of what a clinician knows about a client comes from that conversation. The clinical interview is a conversation with a professional purpose: the interviewer guides it to gather information, to form and test hypotheses about the client's difficulties, and, at the same time, to begin a working relationship. That double job, collecting facts while building trust, is what makes interviewing a skill rather than a questionnaire read aloud.

Interviews classified by purpose

Interviews differ first in what they are for. The same client may meet several kinds in the course of a single episode of care.

  • The intake interview (also called the admission or screening interview) is the first contact. Its questions are practical: why has this person come now, is the problem one this service can help with, how urgent is it, and who should see the client next?
  • The case history interview gathers the longitudinal story of the person and the illness: development, family, education, work, relationships and previous episodes (Section 2.3).
  • The diagnostic interview asks systematically about symptoms in order to decide whether the criteria for a particular disorder are met. Structured interviews, described below, are diagnostic interviews in a standard form.
  • The mental status examination (MSE) is the interviewer's systematic description of the client's appearance, behaviour, speech, emotion, thinking, perception and cognition at the time of the interview (Sections 2.4 to 2.6). If the case history is a film of the person's life, the MSE is a photograph of the present moment.
  • The crisis interview is conducted when someone is in acute distress: after a suicide attempt, an assault or a sudden loss. It is brief, active and focused on immediate safety, on what has just happened and on mobilising support. Comprehensive history-taking waits. (Crisis intervention as a treatment is taught in Chapter 9.)
  • The termination interview (exit or discharge interview) closes a treatment. It reviews progress, consolidates what has been learned, plans for relapse prevention and follow-up, and asks the client for feedback on the service.

Research interviews, pre-test and post-test interviews in assessment, and interviews with family members or teachers (collateral interviews) are further variants. What unites them is that the purpose decides what is asked, in what order and how much structure the interviewer imposes.

The shape of an interview

Whatever its purpose, a good interview has a beginning, a middle and an end. The American psychiatrist Harry Stack Sullivan (1892 to 1949), who described the clinician as a "participant observer" who inevitably influences what he observes, divided the psychiatric interview into four stages in his posthumously published The Psychiatric Interview (1954): the formal inception (greeting the client and establishing why he has come), reconnaissance (a rough sketch of who this person is), the detailed inquiry (testing the hypotheses formed so far) and the termination (summarising, setting out what happens next and parting). Modern texts say the same thing more simply: an opening phase that introduces the interviewer, explains confidentiality and its limits and builds rapport; an exploratory middle phase; and a closing phase that summarises, invites questions and agrees the next step. Students tend to skip the closing. Clients remember it.

Structure: unstructured, semi-structured and fully structured interviews

Interviews differ second in how much of the wording and order of questions is fixed in advance. In an unstructured interview the clinician decides what to ask and in what order, following the client's leads. This is how most clinicians worked for most of the twentieth century, and it has real strengths: it is flexible, it lets rapport develop naturally and it can follow the unexpected detail that turns out to matter most. Its weakness is that two clinicians interviewing the same person may ask different questions, hear different answers and reach different diagnoses.

How large was that weakness? In a classic study published in 1962, Aaron Temkin Beck (1921 to 2021) and his colleagues had pairs of experienced psychiatrists interview the same patients and found that they agreed on the specific diagnosis only about half the time. When Ward, Beck and their colleagues (1962) then asked the psychiatrists to explain each disagreement, only a small share (about 5 percent) was due to the patient telling the two interviewers different things. About a third came from the diagnosticians themselves (asking different questions, weighing the same answers differently), and nearly two-thirds came from the inadequacy of the classification, which offered no clear rules for turning symptoms into diagnoses.

In 1975, Robert L. Spitzer (1932 to 2015) and his colleagues Jean Endicott and Eli Robins gave these sources of disagreement names that are still used. Subject variance arises when the patient really has different conditions at different times; occasion variance when the patient is seen at different stages of the same condition; information variance when the clinicians have different information because they asked different questions; observation variance when they notice or interpret the same information differently; and criterion variance when they use different rules for deciding whether the information adds up to a diagnosis. Criterion variance was the largest. The cure for criterion variance was explicit diagnostic criteria, which arrived with DSM-III in 1980. The cure for information variance was the structured interview.

Key study: the US-UK Diagnostic Project (Cooper and colleagues, 1972)

In the 1960s, hospital statistics suggested that schizophrenia was far more common in New York than in London, while manic-depressive illness was far more common in London. Were Americans and Britons really so different? A team led by John Cooper interviewed consecutive admissions in both cities using a standard semi-structured interview, the Present State Examination, and applied the same diagnostic rules to everyone. The difference almost vanished. The patients were similar; the psychiatrists were not. New York psychiatrists of the time used "schizophrenia" for many patients whom London psychiatrists, and the project team, would have called manic or depressed.

The lesson is the reason structured interviews exist: when the questions and the decision rules are standardised, diagnoses become comparable across clinicians, hospitals and countries.

A fully structured interview fixes the exact wording and order of every question and the rules for recording answers, so tightly that a trained lay interviewer (or even a computer) can administer it without clinical judgement. A semi-structured interview provides standard questions and a fixed sequence of topics, but the clinician may rephrase, add follow-up probes and use clinical judgement to rate whether a symptom is truly present. Most interviews used in clinical practice are semi-structured. Figure 2.1 places the best-known instruments along this continuum.

The major diagnostic interview schedules

You do not need to know the items of these instruments, but you do need to know what each is called, roughly who built it, how structured it is and what it is for. Table 2.1 collects them.

The best known is the Structured Clinical Interview for DSM-5 (SCID-5), published in 2015 to 2016 by Michael First, Janet Williams, Rhonda Karg and Robert Spitzer, and descended from the first SCID that Spitzer's group built for DSM-III in the 1980s. It walks the clinician through the DSM criteria module by module (mood episodes, psychotic symptoms, substance use, anxiety and so on), with a standard question for each criterion and room for follow-up. It comes in a clinician version (SCID-5-CV), a longer research version (SCID-5-RV) and a separate interview for personality disorders (SCID-5-PD, which replaced the older SCID-II). Although its name says "structured", the SCID requires clinical judgement and is technically semi-structured.

In Britain and in the work of the World Health Organization (WHO), the parallel tradition began with the Present State Examination (PSE), developed by John Kenneth Wing (1923 to 2010) with John Cooper and Norman Sartorius. Earlier editions were used in the US-UK Diagnostic Project and in WHO's International Pilot Study of Schizophrenia; the ninth edition (1974), with its glossary and a computer program called CATEGO that turned its ratings into diagnostic classes, became the standard version. The tenth edition of the PSE became the core of the WHO's Schedules for Clinical Assessment in Neuropsychiatry (SCAN), a semi-structured system for trained clinicians that generates ICD and DSM diagnoses and comes with a detailed glossary defining each symptom.

At the structured end sits the Diagnostic Interview Schedule (DIS), built at Washington University in St. Louis by Lee Nelken Robins (1922 to 2009) and colleagues (1981) for the Epidemiologic Catchment Area study, which needed thousands of community interviews done by lay interviewers. The WHO's Composite International Diagnostic Interview (CIDI) combined elements of the DIS and the PSE in a fully structured form for epidemiology across countries. The Mini International Neuropsychiatric Interview (MINI), developed by David Sheehan and Yves Lecrubier (1998), is a short structured interview (roughly 15 to 20 minutes) that screens for the major DSM and ICD disorders and is popular in drug trials and busy clinics.

Some schedules are specialised. The Kiddie Schedule for Affective Disorders and Schizophrenia (K-SADS) is a semi-structured interview for children and adolescents (roughly 6 to 18 years) in which both the parent and the child are interviewed and the clinician integrates the two accounts; its widely used Present and Lifetime version is the K-SADS-PL. The Diagnostic Interview Schedule for Children (DISC) is its fully structured counterpart. The Anxiety Disorders Interview Schedule (ADIS), developed by David H. Barlow and colleagues and now in its DSM-5 edition (ADIS-5, Brown and Barlow), covers anxiety, mood and related disorders in depth and adds dimensional severity ratings to the yes-or-no diagnosis. Instruments such as the Autism Diagnostic Interview-Revised (ADI-R, a caregiver interview), the Autism Diagnostic Observation Schedule (ADOS-2, a standardised play-based observation) and the Vineland Adaptive Behavior Scales (Vineland-3, an adaptive-behaviour interview with a caregiver) belong to the same family of structured and semi-structured interview and observation instruments; they are taught in the Assessment book.

Table 2.1 The main diagnostic interview schedules.
InstrumentOriginStructureTypical use
SCID-5 (CV, RV, PD)First, Williams, Karg and Spitzer (2015 to 2016)Semi-structuredDSM-5 diagnosis in clinics and research
PSE (9th ed. 1974); SCANWing, Cooper and Sartorius; WHOSemi-structured; trained cliniciansCross-national research; ICD diagnosis
DIS; CIDIRobins and colleagues (1981); WHOFully structured; lay interviewersLarge community epidemiology
MINISheehan and Lecrubier (1998)Structured, briefRapid screening, drug trials
K-SADS (K-SADS-PL)Puig-Antich and colleagues; Kaufman and colleaguesSemi-structured; parent and childChild and adolescent disorders
ADIS-5Brown and BarlowSemi-structuredAnxiety and related disorders, with severity

The trade-off between reliability and validity

Why not use a fully structured interview for everyone? Because reliability is not the same thing as validity. A structured interview makes two clinicians agree (high reliability); it cannot guarantee that what they agree on is true (validity). It may miss what the questions do not ask about, it can feel like an interrogation and damage rapport, it takes time (a full SCID can run for two hours), and it records the client's yes or no without the clinical judgement needed to decide whether a "yes" means what the criterion means. Unstructured interviews have the opposite profile: rich, flexible and good for the relationship, but vulnerable to every bias a human interviewer brings. The practical answer is a blend: an open, unstructured opening that lets the client tell the story, followed by a semi-structured check of the relevant diagnostic areas. You will see in Section 2.10 how reliability is measured.

Exam trap

"A structured interview commonly used in clinical psychology" is the SCID. The distractors are all real instruments of a different kind: the MMSE (Mini-Mental State Examination, Folstein, Folstein and McHugh, 1975) is a 30-point cognitive screening test, not a diagnostic interview; the WAIS is the Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale; the TAT is the Thematic Apperception Test, a projective technique. Note also that the MMSE is not the same thing as the MSE. If a question asks whether the SCID is "structured" or "semi-structured", textbooks differ: its title says structured and exam keys usually accept that, but strictly it is semi-structured because it depends on the clinician's judgement. The DIS and CIDI are the truly fully structured ones.

2.2 The Craft of InterviewingHigh yield · 4 Qs

Knowing what to ask is half of interviewing. The other half is knowing how to ask it so that the client keeps talking, tells you the embarrassing part and leaves feeling understood rather than processed. The skills involved were first broken down into small, teachable units in the late 1960s by Allen E. Ivey, whose microcounselling approach (from 1968, and in his book Microcounseling, 1971) trained one "microskill" at a time, from attending at the base of the hierarchy, through questioning and reflecting, up to confronting and influencing. Most of what follows is common to clinical interviewing, counselling and psychotherapy, and the person-centred tradition of Carl Ransom Rogers (1902 to 1987) runs through all of it. (Rogers' therapy itself is taught in Chapter 6.)

Rapport and attending

Rapport is the sense of trust, comfort and mutual understanding between interviewer and client that makes honest disclosure possible. You cannot order it; you create the conditions for it. Introduce yourself, explain the purpose of the meeting and what will happen to the information (including the limits of confidentiality), start with neutral topics, and show interest. In a written MSE, clinicians record whether rapport was "established easily", "established with difficulty" or "not established", because the ease of rapport is itself clinical information.

The foundation microskill is attending behaviour: showing with your body, eyes and voice that you are listening. Ivey described it in terms of culturally appropriate eye contact, vocal qualities, verbal tracking (staying with the client's topic) and body language. Gerard Egan, in The Skilled Helper (first published in 1975), summarised physical attending in the acronym SOLER: face the client Squarely, adopt an Open posture, Lean towards the client, keep good Eye contact and stay Relaxed. SOLER is a guide, not a rule: in many Indian homes and villages, and with many women clients, direct sustained eye contact from a stranger is uncomfortable, and sitting at a slight angle is kinder than sitting squarely.

Questions: open, closed and the funnel

An open question invites the client to answer in his own words and at length: "Tell me about what has been happening", "How have things been at home?", "What was that like for you?" A closed question can be answered in a word or two, usually yes or no or a fact: "Did you sleep last night?", "How old were you when it started?" Neither is better in itself. Open questions generate the story and protect rapport; closed questions pin down details (onset, frequency, the presence or absence of a specific symptom) and are necessary in diagnostic and risk assessment. The skilled interviewer uses the funnel technique: begin each topic with open questions and narrow progressively to closed ones. Begin with closed questions and the interview becomes an interrogation; never use them and you finish with a moving story and no idea how long the patient has been unable to sleep.

"Tell me about ..." is the textbook open-ended prompt. Strictly it is a request rather than a question, but it is classified as an open-ended question because it leaves the content and length of the answer entirely to the client. Ethnographers use the same move: the anthropologist James P. Spradley (1933 to 1982) built The Ethnographic Interview (1979) around "grand tour" questions such as "Tell me about a typical day", so a "Tell me about" request can appear inside an ethnographic interviewing technique, but its type is still an open question. Questions derived from a particular theory (for example, asking about antecedents and consequences because you are doing a behavioural analysis) are called theory-guided inquiry; again, the theory shapes what you ask, not whether the question is open or closed.

Encouraging the client to continue

Minimal encouragers are the small verbal and nonverbal signals that tell the client "go on, I am with you" without changing the subject: a nod, leaning forward, "Mm-hmm", "Yes", "Go on", "And then?", or repeating a key word with a questioning tone ("Worthless?"). Social-work interviewing texts (for example, those of Dean Hepworth and colleagues) call these furthering responses, which include minimal prompts and "accent" responses that echo a word. So leaning forward, saying "umm" and nodding are examples of furthering. Do not confuse furthering with paraphrasing, which restates content, or with checking (also called perception checking or checking out), in which the interviewer asks the client to confirm an understanding: "Let me check I have this right: the sleep went first, and the low mood came after?" A prompt, in the narrower sense used in survey and research interviewing, is a specific cue that directs the respondent to a particular topic or reminds him of possible answers ("What about at work?"); it steers, whereas furthering simply encourages.

Reflecting: paraphrase, reflection of feeling and summary

Paraphrasing (also called restatement or reflection of content) feeds back the essence of what the client has said, in the interviewer's own, different words and usually more briefly. It shows that you have understood and lets the client correct you. Parroting the client's exact words is not paraphrasing; it sounds mechanical and adds nothing.

Reflection of feeling goes beneath the content to name the emotion the client is expressing, stated or implied: "You sound ashamed, as if you have let everyone down." It is the signature technique of client-centred therapy. Rogers believed that accurately reflecting the client's inner experience, rather than analysing it, advising or directing, was what allowed the client to hear himself and grow. So if you are asked which technique a client-centred therapist is most likely to use, the answer is reflection. Analysis (interpretation of unconscious meaning) belongs to psychoanalysis; giving advice is a directive move that Rogers deliberately avoided; and aversive conditioning, pairing an unwanted behaviour with an unpleasant stimulus, belongs to behaviour therapy. Late in life Rogers grew uneasy with the phrase "reflection of feelings", which he thought had become a wooden technique, and preferred to describe what he did as "testing understandings".

Summarising pulls together the main points of a longer stretch of the interview, or of the whole session, in a few sentences. Summaries help the client feel heard, organise a rambling story, mark the transition from one topic to the next and close the interview.

Clarification, confrontation, interpretation and silence

Clarification asks the client to make something vague more precise: "When you say you 'can't cope', what happens exactly?" or "What do you mean by 'tension'?" In India the word "tension" can mean anxiety, low mood, anger, a headache or a family quarrel, so clarification is not optional.

Confrontation, in the interviewing sense, has nothing to do with aggression. It means pointing out, gently and with evidence, a discrepancy: between words and behaviour ("You say you are fine, and your eyes are filling with tears"), between two statements, or between what the client wants and what he is doing. Done too early or harshly it destroys rapport; done well it moves the client forward.

Interpretation offers the client a new frame of reference for his experience, a meaning or a connection he has not made himself: "I wonder whether the anger at your manager has something to do with how your father treated you." Interpretation is the central technique of psychodynamic therapy (Chapter 3); in an assessment interview it is used sparingly, because it goes beyond what the client has said.

Finally, silence is a skill too. A pause of several seconds after a painful disclosure gives the client room to feel and continue; rushing to fill it with another question tells him you are uncomfortable. Silence becomes unhelpful when it stretches into confusion or when a highly anxious or psychotic client experiences it as a threat.

Empathy, primary and advanced

Empathy is the ability to understand the client's experience from the inside, "as if" it were your own without losing the "as if", and to communicate that understanding. Rogers made accurate empathy one of his three core conditions, with unconditional positive regard and congruence. Egan distinguished two levels. Primary empathy (basic or primary-level accurate empathy) communicates understanding of what the client has openly expressed, his surface feelings and the experiences behind them. Advanced empathy (advanced accurate empathy) goes further, voicing what the client has only hinted at, the implications he has not seen or the themes running through several stories. Because advanced empathy depends on trust and on knowing the client well, it is considered inappropriate in an initial interview: offered to a stranger, it sounds presumptuous and intrusive. Primary empathy is appropriate from the first minute. Robert Carkhuff had already turned empathy into a five-level rating scale (1969), on which level 3 is an "interchangeable" response that adds nothing and subtracts nothing from what the client said, and levels 4 and 5 are additive, roughly Egan's advanced empathy.

Table 2.2 puts these skills to work on a single statement from Ravi. Read it slowly: the differences between the responses are exactly what examiners test.

Table 2.2 One client statement, many microskills. Ravi says: "My father keeps comparing me to my cousin who cleared the exam. I don't even want to go home for meals anymore."
SkillWhat it doesExample response
Minimal encourager (furthering)Keeps him talking(Nods) "Mm-hmm ... go on."
Open questionInvites elaboration"What happens at meals?"
Closed questionPins down a fact"When did you last eat with the family?"
ParaphraseRestates content in new words"Being measured against your cousin has made home hard to be in."
Reflection of feelingNames the emotion"You sound hurt, and maybe ashamed."
ClarificationMakes the vague precise"What does he actually say when he compares you?"
CheckingConfirms understanding"Have I got it right that you are skipping meals to avoid him?"
SummaryDraws threads together"So the exam result, your cousin's success and your father's words have all come together in the last three months."
ConfrontationPoints out a discrepancy"You say you don't care what he thinks, yet it is keeping you from your own home."
InterpretationOffers a new frame"Perhaps avoiding him is a way of not having to feel that you have failed him."
Advanced empathyVoices the implied"I wonder whether part of you has started to believe what he says about you."
Exam trap

Repeating what the client said in different words is paraphrasing; repeating it word for word is parroting. Questioning asks for new information, empathy is the broader capacity (paraphrase is one way of showing it), and nonverbal communication refers to posture, gesture and tone. Nodding and "umm" are furthering (minimal encouragers), not prompting or checking. And remember which empathy waits: advanced empathy is inappropriate in a first session; primary empathy is not.

Common errors

Beginners make the same handful of mistakes, and most of them come from anxiety: the wish to be useful, to fill the silence or to reach a diagnosis quickly.

  • A leading question suggests its own answer ("You've been feeling depressed, haven't you?"). Children, suggestible clients and people eager to please will simply agree.
  • A double-barrelled question asks about two things at once but allows one answer: "How often and how much time do you exercise?" The answer cannot be interpreted, because you do not know which half it addresses. Stacking several questions in a row ("How did you feel? Were you angry? Did you tell anyone?") is a related error.
  • "Why" questions ("Why did you do that?") sound accusing and invite defensiveness; "What led up to that?" gets the same information.
  • Premature advice, premature reassurance ("Don't worry, everything will be fine") and moralising close the conversation down before the client has finished.
  • Interrupting, using jargon, taking notes so busily that eye contact is lost, and talking more than the client are all signs that the interview has become about the interviewer.
  • Errors of judgement matter as much as errors of technique. The halo effect, named by Edward Lee Thorndike (1874 to 1949) in 1920, is the tendency to let one salient attribute (good looks, fluent English, a respectable job) colour judgements of everything else about a person; when an interviewer or rater judges an individual on the basis of a single attribute, it is called the halo error. Raters also show central tendency error (rating everyone near the middle), leniency or severity error, contrast error (judging a client against the previous one rather than against a standard) and similarity error (rating people who resemble oneself more favourably). In diagnosis the most dangerous bias is premature closure: deciding on an answer early and then noticing only what fits, which is what happened to the pseudopatients.

Interviewing special groups

Children. Young children cannot sit through a question-and-answer interview, so clinicians use play, drawing and stories, short sentences and concrete words. Children are suggestible, so leading questions are especially harmful (Chapter 10 discusses forensic interviewing of children). Their sense of time is weak; "Did it start before or after Deepavali?" works better than "How many months ago?" Because children's accounts are limited, the clinician gathers information from several informants, parents and teachers as well as the child. Agreement between informants is only modest (a large 1987 review by Thomas Achenbach and colleagues found that parents, teachers and children often report quite different problems), and that disagreement is information, not error: a child may be anxious only at school, or may hide sadness from parents.

Older adults. Check for hearing and vision problems before concluding that the person is confused; sit closer, face the person and speak slowly and clearly without shouting or talking down ("elderspeak"). Allow more time, ask about all medicines (several drugs together can cause confusion), screen for cognitive impairment, and remember that depression in late life often presents with bodily complaints and poor memory. Interview a family member separately as well, and keep in mind the possibility of neglect or abuse (Chapter 10).

Resistant or involuntary clients. A man brought by his family, a teenager sent by the school or a prisoner referred by the court may see no reason to talk. Arguing makes it worse. Acknowledge the situation honestly ("It sounds as if coming here wasn't your idea"), explain your role and the limits of confidentiality, ask for his view of why others are worried, and look for something he does want. Motivational interviewing's advice to "roll with resistance" applies (Chapter 6).

Suicidal clients. Ask directly. A common fear among trainees is that asking about suicide will put the idea into the client's head; a 2014 systematic review of the research (Dazzi and colleagues) found no evidence that asking increases suicidal thinking, and many clients feel relief that someone has finally asked. Move step by step from general to specific ("Have you felt that life is not worth living?", "Have you thought of ending your life?", "Have you thought how?", "Have you done anything to prepare?"). The content of a suicide risk assessment is in Section 2.7.

Culture, language and interpreters

Every interview happens inside a culture. Clients differ in how they describe distress, whom they consider the right person to help, how much they disclose to a stranger and how decisions are made. The psychiatrist and anthropologist Arthur Kleinman showed (most influentially in Patients and Healers in the Context of Culture, 1980) that every patient has an explanatory model of his illness: what he calls it, what he thinks caused it, why it started when it did, what it does to him, how severe it is, what treatment he expects and what he fears most. Asking about the explanatory model ("What do you think is causing this?") is the simplest cultural skill there is. DSM-5 (2013) built on this tradition with the 16-question Cultural Formulation Interview, which asks about the cultural definition of the problem, cultural perceptions of cause and context, cultural factors affecting coping and help-seeking, and current help-seeking.

Cultures also supply idioms of distress, shared ways of expressing suffering. In India these include describing depression and anxiety through the body ("weakness", "burning in the head", "gas", aches all over), the concept of "tension", and dhat, anxiety about losing semen in the urine, named "dhat syndrome" by the Indian psychiatrist N. N. Wig in 1960. An interviewer who dismisses these as "only somatic" loses the client; one who explores them finds the depression underneath.

Indian context

In India the patient rarely comes alone. Families usually bring the client, provide much of the history and expect to be involved in decisions, so the clinician must balance the family's account with time alone with the client (especially for adolescents, married women and questions about sex, substance use or abuse). Mysuru clinics routinely see Kannada, Tamil, Telugu, Hindi and Urdu speakers. When the client and the clinician do not share a language, use a trained interpreter, not a family member and never a child: relatives edit, soften or answer for the patient, and confidentiality is lost. Brief the interpreter beforehand, ask for word-for-word translation, speak directly to the client in the first person ("How is your sleep?", not "Ask him how his sleep is"), use short sentences, and debrief afterwards.

Exam trap

The explanatory model is Kleinman's (1980): the patient's own account of what the illness is, why it began and what should be done. The Cultural Formulation Interview is the DSM-5 instrument (2013) that turns this into 16 standard questions. Do not swap them. An idiom of distress is a shared cultural way of expressing suffering ("tension", "weakness", dhat), not a symptom of a disorder in itself; dhat syndrome was named by N. N. Wig (1960). And if an option offers a family member, especially a child, as the interpreter, it is the wrong option: use a trained interpreter and speak to the client, not to the interpreter.

You now know how to conduct the conversation. The next question is what to fill it with. A clinician who simply follows the client's story wherever it goes will finish with a vivid account of the last three months and nothing about the client's birth, schooling, marriage or the uncle who was treated for depression twenty years ago. To make sure nothing important is left out, clinicians organise what they learn under a fixed set of headings. That set of headings is the case history.

2.3 The Case HistoryLower yield · not asked yet

A case history is the systematic, chronological account of a person and his illness: who he is, what is wrong, how it began and developed, and what kind of life and personality it grew out of. Section 2.1 compared it to a film of the person's life, set against the MSE's photograph of the present moment. The comparison is worth holding on to, because many of the mistakes students make come from mixing the two. "He has been sleeping badly for three months" belongs to the history; "he is slow, tearful and avoids my eyes" belongs to the MSE.

The idea that a psychiatric patient should be understood through his whole life story owes most to Adolf Meyer (1866 to 1950), the Swiss-born psychiatrist who led American psychiatry from Johns Hopkins University in the first half of the twentieth century. Meyer's "psychobiology" held that mental disorders were reactions of the whole person to the biological, psychological and social circumstances of a life. He taught his residents to construct a "life chart", setting out year by year the person's illnesses, moves, losses, failures and achievements, so that the timing of an illness could be read against the events around it. His influence is visible in the word "reaction" that runs through DSM-I (1952), and in the structure of every case-record form you will meet. In India most hospitals and training programmes use a format descended from the British version of this tradition, the Institute of Psychiatry's Notes on Eliciting and Recording Clinical Information (1973), adapted for Indian families.

Table 2.3 sets out the standard order. The order is not arbitrary. It moves from the present problem backwards into the person's past, and from the illness outwards to the family and the personality, so that by the end the clinician can see both the illness and the soil it grew in.

Table 2.3 The headings of a psychiatric case history, in their usual order.
HeadingWhat it captures
Identifying dataName, age, sex, education, occupation, marital status, religion, language, socioeconomic status, address, source of referral
Informants and reliabilityWho gave the history, their relationship to the patient and how far their account can be trusted
Chief complaintsThe main problems, in the patient's and informant's own words, each with its duration
History of present illnessOnset, course, progress, precipitating factors, symptoms in sequence, biological functions, negative history, treatment so far
Past historyPrevious psychiatric episodes and their treatment; past medical and surgical illness
Family historyFamily structure (genogram), mental illness, suicide and substance use in relatives, family relationships and atmosphere
Personal historyBirth and development, childhood, education, occupation, menstrual, sexual and marital life, substance use, forensic history
Premorbid personalityWhat the person was like before the illness began
ThenPhysical examination, MSE, summary, provisional and differential diagnosis

Identifying data and the informants

The history opens with the facts that place the patient in the world: name, age, sex, education, occupation, marital status, religion, mother tongue, socioeconomic status, rural or urban background, and who referred him. These are not clerical details. Age and sex shape the likely diagnoses (a first episode of mania at 60 should make you look for a brain lesion); education tells you how to pitch your questions and how to interpret the cognitive tests in Section 2.6; religion and language tell you what idioms of distress to listen for; and the source of referral (self, family, a general practitioner, a court) tells you how willing the client is likely to be.

The next heading is peculiar to psychiatry and especially important in India: the informants. A patient with an acute psychosis, a severe depression, dementia or a young child cannot give a complete history, and many adult patients in India are accompanied by a parent, spouse or sibling who expects to speak. The clinician therefore records who gave the information and judges how reliable each account is. Indian training notes condense the criteria into five Cs: the informant's contact with the patient (did he live with him through the illness or see him twice a year?), the closeness of the relationship, the continuity of the account (does it hang together in time?), its consistency (does the same informant tell the same story when asked again?) and its corroboration (do different informants and records agree?). The informant's concern for the patient matters too: a husband who wants a quick divorce on grounds of "madness", or a family fighting over property, may exaggerate; a family ashamed of the illness may minimise it. The record ends with a one-line judgement: "The information is reliable and adequate" or "partly reliable; the father has had little contact with the patient in the past year".

Why collect several accounts at all? Because each informant sees a different slice of the person. You met this idea in Section 2.2: parents, teachers and children agree only modestly about a child's problems, and the disagreement is information. A boy may be inattentive and restless in a crowded classroom and perfectly settled at home, and a woman may hide her drinking from her husband but not from her sister. Symptoms vary across contexts, which is why multi-informant assessment is recommended for children and why, with adults, the clinician always tries to see the patient alone as well as with the family.

Reliability also covers the patient himself. A history may be distorted unintentionally by poor memory, by the current mood (depressed people recall their past as bleaker than it was, a bias called retrospective falsification) or by lack of insight. It may also be distorted deliberately. Consciously producing or exaggerating symptoms for an external gain, such as compensation, avoiding a court case or obtaining drugs, is malingering; producing them to take on the sick role itself, without any external reward, is factitious disorder. (Both are taught in the Psychiatric Disorders book.) A clinician does not begin by suspecting every patient, but notes when the history is inconsistent, when the complaints do not match what is observed, and when there is an obvious external incentive.

Exam trap

Multi-informant assessment is recommended because symptoms may vary across contexts, not because parents over-report, not because teachers are irrelevant and not simply because clinic observation is insufficient (true, but it is not the reason). Malingering is conscious faking for an external goal; confabulation (Section 2.6) is filling memory gaps without any intent to deceive. Informant reliability is judged on contact, closeness, continuity, consistency and corroboration.

Chief complaints

The chief complaint (or presenting complaint) is the main problem that has brought the person for help, recorded as far as possible in his own words, with its duration. "Feeling useless, 3 months" tells the next reader far more than "depressed mood", which is already an interpretation. When there are several complaints, each is listed with its own duration in the order in which they began, so that the list itself sketches the illness: "Poor sleep, 3 months; feeling useless, 3 months; stays in his room, 2 months; talks of being better off dead, 1 week." Where the informant's complaints differ from the patient's, both are recorded. They often differ in instructive ways: the patient with mania complains of nothing, while his wife complains that he has spent their savings in a week.

The chief complaint is not always the main problem. In a case described by Barlow and Durand, a 24-year-old mechanic called Frank was referred for distress and anxiety centring on his marriage; only after half an hour, when the interviewer asked about his habit of closing his eyes and twitching his leg, did he reveal frightening, unwanted thoughts that he was going to have a seizure, which he found "hard to even talk about". Record what the client says. Then keep listening.

History of present illness

The history of present illness (often abbreviated HOPI in Indian case records) is the heart of the history: the story of the current episode from its first sign to the day of the interview, told in chronological order. Four features organise it.

  • Onset is how quickly the illness developed. Indian case formats, following the cut-offs that ICD-10 used for acute psychoses, describe onset as abrupt (fully developed within about 48 hours), acute (within about two weeks), subacute (over weeks to about three months) or insidious (creeping on over months). Onset matters because it points to causes: an abrupt change in a previously well person suggests a medical, toxic or acute psychotic cause; an insidious decline over years suggests a different picture altogether.
  • Course is the pattern over time: continuous (symptoms present throughout), episodic (episodes separated by periods of recovery) or fluctuating (waxing and waning without full recovery).
  • Progress is the direction: improving, static or deteriorating.
  • Precipitating factors are the events that occurred shortly before the onset and seem to have triggered it: a failure, a loss, a childbirth, an illness, starting or stopping a drug. The clinician also notes whether the precipitant is clear and proportionate. (These factors become one of the five Ps of the case formulation in Section 2.9.)

Within that frame the symptoms are described in the order in which they appeared, with examples. "He stopped going to his coaching classes about a month after the result; two weeks later he stopped eating with the family" is better than any list. Indian formats then add two headings that students sometimes skip. The first is the biological functions: sleep (difficulty falling asleep, waking in the night, early morning awakening), appetite and weight, bowel and bladder habits, sexual interest and, in women, menstruation. Biological functions are hard to fake and change early in depression, mania and psychosis, so they are some of the most informative questions you can ask. The second is the negative history: the important things that are absent. "No history of head injury, seizures, fever, substance use, elated mood or hearing voices" tells the reader which alternatives the clinician has already considered and set aside. A history without a negative history is an argument with only one side.

The HOPI ends with the treatment history: whom the family consulted, what was given, for how long, with what effect, and whether the patient took it. Adherence (the older word is compliance) is the extent to which the patient's behaviour, in taking medicines or following other advice, matches the recommendations. In India the treatment history often includes a temple, a dargah, a faith healer, an ayurvedic practitioner and a general physician before a mental health professional is reached. Record these without judgement; they are part of the family's explanatory model, and the healer may become an ally.

Clinical link

The best single question for dating an onset is "When were you last completely well?" Patients and families date an illness from the day it became undeniable (the day he stopped eating, the day she broke the window). Asking when the person was last his usual self usually pushes the real onset back by weeks or months, and often uncovers the precipitant.

Past psychiatric and medical history

The past history asks whether this has happened before. For each previous psychiatric episode the clinician records the age, the symptoms, the duration, the treatment, the response and the level of recovery between episodes. The pattern often decides the diagnosis: a second depression turns a "single episode" into a recurrent disorder, and a forgotten week of sleeplessness and overspending at 19 may turn "depression" into bipolar disorder, which changes the treatment. Any previous self-harm is recorded in detail, because a previous attempt is the strongest single predictor of suicide (Section 2.7).

The past medical history covers serious illnesses, operations, head injuries, seizures, thyroid disease, diabetes, hypertension, infections such as HIV and syphilis, and current medicines, many of which can cause psychiatric symptoms (corticosteroids can cause mania or depression; some antihypertensive and antiepileptic drugs can lower mood). Medical illness can mimic mental illness: hypothyroidism can look like depression, hyperthyroidism like anxiety, and a frontal tumour like a change of personality. This is why the history is followed by a physical examination and why medical causes are the first to be ruled out in a differential diagnosis (Section 2.9).

Family history and the genogram

The family history has two jobs. The first is genetic and diagnostic: which relatives have had a mental illness, an attempted or completed suicide, a substance use problem, epilepsy, intellectual disability or a "nervous breakdown", and how were they treated? Families often do not know psychiatric labels, so the clinician asks descriptively: "Has anyone in the family been very sad for a long time, or been treated with injections, or stayed in a hospital for the mind?" The second job is psychological and social: who lives in the household, who holds authority, how members get along, how the family has reacted to the illness and what it can offer. In India the clinician also asks about consanguinity, marriage between blood relatives (cross-cousin and uncle-niece marriages are common in parts of Karnataka, Tamil Nadu and Andhra Pradesh), because it raises the risk of recessive genetic conditions.

The most efficient way to record all this is a genogram, a diagram of the family tree, normally across at least three generations, that uses standard symbols to show sex, age, marriages, children, deaths and illnesses at a glance. Genograms were popularised in family therapy by Murray Bowen (1913 to 1990), who used them to trace emotional patterns across generations (his family systems therapy is in Chapter 7), and their symbols were standardised by Monica McGoldrick and Randy Gerson in Genograms in Family Assessment (1985). The conventions you need are simple. A square is a male and a circle a female; the person being assessed (the index patient or proband) is marked with a double outline or an arrow; a cross inside a symbol, or across it, means the person has died; a horizontal line joins a married couple, with the husband conventionally on the left, and the children hang from that line in birth order from left (eldest) to right; a slash across the marriage line means separation and two slashes mean divorce; a filled or shaded symbol marks someone with the illness in question; and in Indian case records a double marriage line marks a consanguineous marriage. Figure 2.2 is Ravi's genogram.

Read the figure and the clinical questions arrive by themselves. Depression in a first-degree relative of Ravi's mother raises his genetic loading. Heavy drinking in the paternal grandfather is a reason to ask carefully about Ravi's own drinking. The cousin who got the bank job is the son of Ravi's father's elder brother: the comparison that hurts Ravi most is being made inside the joint family, at the same dinner table.

Two relatives of the genogram are easily confused with it. An ecomap, devised by the social worker Ann Hartman in the 1970s, places the family in a central circle and draws its links to the outside world (school, workplace, temple, relatives, health services, moneylenders) with lines whose style shows whether each connection is strong, weak or stressful. Where the genogram maps the family across time, the ecomap maps it across its social environment. A sociogram is older and comes from group psychology: Jacob Levy Moreno (1889 to 1974), the founder of sociometry and of psychodrama, used it in Who Shall Survive? (1934) to map who in a group chooses, likes, rejects or communicates with whom, revealing cliques, stars and isolates. It captures the explicit and implicit communication and interaction patterns of a group, not a family tree.

Exam trap

A genogram is a multigenerational family tree with standard symbols (squares for males, circles for females). An ecomap (Hartman) shows the family's connections with outside systems. A sociogram (Moreno) maps the interaction and communication patterns within a group, so it is the answer when a question asks what "captures implicit and explicit group communication and interaction patterns". A cognitive map is Edward Tolman's term for a rat's mental representation of a maze (General Psychology) and has nothing to do with families.

Beyond the diagram, the family history describes the family as a living system: its type (nuclear, joint or extended), its finances, who makes decisions, how members communicate, recent events (deaths, marriages, migrations, debts) and how the family has responded to the patient's illness. Families that respond with much criticism, hostility or emotional over-involvement, a pattern known as high expressed emotion, are associated with higher relapse rates in schizophrenia and depression; the research is described in Chapter 7. Ravi's father's repeated comparisons are exactly the kind of criticism a clinician notes here.

Personal history

The personal history is the patient's own biography, told from before birth to the present. It is long, and in a busy clinic it is often abbreviated, but each heading earns its place.

  • Birth and early development. The mother's health in pregnancy, whether the child was planned and wanted, full term or premature, the type of delivery, any delay in the birth cry, birth weight and neonatal illness. Then the developmental milestones, compared with the usual ages: a social smile by about 2 months, head control by 3 to 4 months, sitting without support by 6 to 8 months, walking alone by 12 to 15 months, first words around 12 months and two-word phrases by about 2 years. Delays point towards neurodevelopmental conditions (developmental norms are taught in the Developmental Psychology book).
  • Childhood. Home atmosphere, separations from parents, who brought the child up, physical or sexual abuse, and the so-called neurotic traits of childhood: persistent temper tantrums, nail-biting, thumb-sucking beyond the usual age, bed-wetting, night terrors, sleepwalking, stammering and marked fears.
  • Education. Age at starting school, medium of instruction, performance, highest qualification, failures, reasons for leaving, relationships with teachers and peers, and extracurricular interests. A steady fall in marks over several years before an illness can be the earliest sign of a slowly developing psychosis.
  • Occupation. Each job, its duration, the reasons for changing, the relationship with employers and colleagues, and satisfaction. Frequent job changes, and the reasons given for them, say much about personality.
  • Menstrual history (for women). Age at menarche, regularity, premenstrual changes, and pregnancies, abortions and menopause, because mood disorders cluster around reproductive events.
  • Sexual history. Source and adequacy of sexual knowledge, sexual orientation, sexual experiences, masturbation and the client's attitude to it (worry about semen loss is common among young Indian men), sexual difficulties and any abuse. Ask privately, in plain language and without embarrassment; clients follow the interviewer's lead.
  • Marital history. Age at marriage, whether arranged or by choice, consanguinity, the spouse's age, education, occupation and personality, children, and the quality of the relationship, including conflict and violence.
  • Substance use. For tobacco, alcohol, cannabis and any other drug: age at first use, pattern, amount, last use, attempts to stop, and signs of dependence such as tolerance and withdrawal. A useful screen for alcohol is the four-question CAGE (John Ewing, 1984): has the person felt he should Cut down, been Annoyed by criticism of his drinking, felt Guilty about it, or needed an Eye-opener in the morning? Two or more "yes" answers suggest a drinking problem.
  • Forensic history. Arrests, convictions, imprisonment, pending cases and violence, recorded factually.

Some formats add religious and cultural history (beliefs, practices, whether the family attributes the illness to spirits, the evil eye or sorcery) and the patient's present life situation (housing, income, debts, support).

Premorbid personality

The last heading of the history, and the one students find hardest, is the premorbid personality: the person's characteristic, long-standing ways of thinking, feeling, behaving and relating to others before the illness began. Why should a clinician care what someone was like before? There are four reasons. First, premorbid personality is the baseline against which change is judged: "he has become withdrawn" means something only if he used to be sociable. Second, some traits predispose to particular disorders; perfectionistic, self-critical, dependent people are more vulnerable to depression, and anxious, rigid, orderly people to obsessional problems. Third, premorbid adjustment predicts outcome; in schizophrenia, for example, good premorbid social and work functioning is one of the best predictors of a good prognosis. Fourth, the history helps separate an illness, which is a change from the person's usual self, from a personality disorder, which is the usual self.

The Maudsley tradition describes premorbid personality under a set of headings: attitude to self (self-confidence, self-criticism, ambitions); relations with others (friends, family, intimacy, social ease); work and responsibility; interests and leisure; predominant mood (cheerful, anxious, gloomy, even, changeable); character traits (shy, obstinate, perfectionistic, impulsive, suspicious, dependent, jealous); moral and religious standards; fantasy life; habits (eating, sleeping, substances); energy and initiative; and characteristic reactions to stress and frustration. Because the patient's present mood colours how he describes his past self, the premorbid personality is best obtained from an informant who has known him for years, and then compared with his own account.

Exam trap

Premorbid means "before the illness": it is the personality before onset, not a personality disorder and not the personality during the illness. Three easily confused pairs from the history: onset (how fast the illness developed: abrupt, acute, subacute, insidious) versus course (continuous, episodic, fluctuating) versus progress (improving, static, deteriorating); precipitating factors (triggers just before onset) versus predisposing factors (long-standing vulnerabilities); and the chief complaint (recorded in the patient's own words, with duration) versus the clinician's diagnosis.

Putting the history together: Ravi

All of this sounds like a great deal of information, and it is. In practice an experienced clinician gathers most of it in an hour, partly from the patient and partly from the family, and writes it up in two or three pages. Here, abridged, is what Ravi's history looked like.

Ravi's case history (abridged)

Identifying data. Ravi, 24, male, Hindu, Kannada-speaking, unmarried, B.Com, unemployed (preparing for bank examinations), lower-middle socioeconomic status, from a village near Mandya; lives in a shared rented room in Mysuru. Self-referred with his mother. Informants: Ravi; his mother (46), who speaks to him daily and has stayed with him for the past ten days. Information reliable and adequate. Chief complaints (Ravi): "I feel useless", 3 months; "I can't sleep", 3 months; "nothing goes into my head", 3 months. (Mother): stays in his room and eats little, 2 months; said everyone would be better off without him, 1 week.

History of present illness. Onset subacute, over two to three weeks after the bank examination result three months ago; course continuous; progress deteriorating. Precipitants: third failure, in the week his cousin was selected; his father's repeated comparisons. Low mood most of the day, worse in the mornings; loss of interest in studies and in cricket; waking at 3 a.m.; appetite reduced, weight loss of about 5 kg; tiredness; guilt that his parents pledged his mother's gold to pay his coaching fees; hopelessness; a passive wish to be dead, with no plan and no attempt. Stopped going home at weekends two months ago. Negative history: no elated periods, no hearing voices, no head injury, seizures or fever, no alcohol or tobacco. Treatment: a general practitioner prescribed vitamins; his mother made a vow at the village temple.

Past history. About a month of sadness and withdrawal at 17 after his second PUC results, which passed without treatment. No significant medical illness. Family history (Figure 2.2): parents are first cousins; maternal uncle treated for depression; paternal grandfather drank heavily. Father critical and proud, mother warm; younger sister studying nursing. Personal history: full-term hospital delivery, milestones normal; nail-biting in childhood; Kannada-medium schooling with high marks ("the bright one" of the family); B.Com first class; six months as an accounts assistant in a cooperative society; heterosexual, no relationships; no substance use; no forensic history. Premorbid personality (from Ravi and his mother): conscientious, perfectionistic, self-critical, shy, a few close friends, devout, usually even in mood, a dutiful eldest son who took criticism to heart.

Notice what the history has already done. Before a single question about his present mental state, it has set out a likely sequence (a vulnerable, perfectionistic young man, a family history of depression, a humiliating failure inside a family that measures worth by results), suggested a diagnosis to test (a depressive episode, possibly recurrent), raised a risk to assess (the passive wish to be dead) and flagged something to check (thyroid function and haemoglobin, because tiredness and weight loss have medical causes too). What it cannot tell us is how Ravi is now, in this room. For that the clinician turns from the film to the photograph.

Scan Your Brain

  3. A semi-structured interview developed for DSM diagnoses is the ; a fully structured interview built for lay interviewers in epidemiology is the .
  4. Leaning forward, nodding and saying "umm" are examples of .
  5. Restating what the client said in different words is .
  6. Voicing what the client has only hinted at is empathy, which is inappropriate in a first session.
  7. The tendency to let one salient attribute colour judgements of everything else about a person is the effect.

2.4 The Mental Status Examination I: Appearance, Behaviour, Speech, Mood and AffectLower yield · 1 Q

When the clinician called Ravi's name in the waiting room, he did not look up at once. He rose slowly, walked in a step behind his mother, sat on the edge of the chair with his shoulders rounded and his hands pressed between his knees, and studied the floor. His shirt was clean but creased, his chin unshaven. Asked how he was, he paused for several seconds and said, very quietly, "Okay." Nobody had asked him a single question about his symptoms, and the clinician had already started writing his mental status examination.

Section 2.1 defined the mental status examination (MSE) as the clinician's systematic description of the client's appearance, behaviour, speech, emotion, thinking, perception and cognition at the time of the interview, and Section 2.3 contrasted it, as a photograph, with the film of the case history. This section and the next two take the photograph apart, heading by heading. This section deals with everything the clinician can observe or hear in the first few minutes: how the person looks, moves and talks, and what he feels. Section 2.5 turns to what he thinks and perceives, and Section 2.6 to cognition, judgement and insight, and to writing the whole thing up.

Describing, not diagnosing

Why does a clinician need a special vocabulary to describe what a patient looks like and says? Because ordinary words blur exactly the distinctions that matter. "He was confused" might mean that he did not know the date, that his sentences did not hang together, that he was puzzled by his own experiences or that he was drowsy, and each points to a different problem. The precise vocabulary used in the MSE comes largely from one book. In 1913 a young psychiatrist at Heidelberg, Karl Jaspers (1883 to 1969), published General Psychopathology. Jaspers argued that before psychiatry could explain mental illness it had to describe it, accurately and without theory, and that the only way to describe experiences the clinician cannot see (a voice, a conviction, a sense that the world has become unreal) is to question the patient carefully and empathically until the clinician can picture what the experience is actually like from the inside. He called this method phenomenology. The discipline it founded, the precise description of abnormal experience and behaviour without commitment to any theory of its cause, is descriptive psychopathology, and it is the language in which every MSE is written. (Jaspers later left psychiatry for philosophy and became one of the leading existentialist philosophers.)

Jaspers also drew a distinction that runs through the rest of this chapter: between the form of an experience and its content. The form is its structure: is this a perception, a belief, a memory, an image? The content is what it is about. If Ravi heard a voice saying "you have failed everyone", the form would be an auditory hallucination and the content would be failure. The content tells you about the person and his life (a Mandya farmer's son who has failed his examinations will hear about failure, a devout woman about sin); the form tells you what kind of disturbance you are dealing with. Diagnosis depends mostly on form.

Three working rules follow from Jaspers' approach. First, describe; do not label. "Sat with head bowed, answered in a whisper after long pauses, wept twice while speaking of his father" can be checked, compared with next week's examination and understood by any reader; "depressed" is already a conclusion. Second, record the here and now. The MSE is cross-sectional: what the patient shows and reports at this examination (for experiences such as voices or fears, the clinician also asks about the last few days). The history of how the illness developed belongs in the case history. Third, give examples, and wherever possible quote the patient's own words. The MSE records two kinds of information: signs, which the clinician observes (tearfulness, slowed movement, a tremor), and symptoms, which the patient reports (low mood, hearing voices). Many headings need both.

The headings themselves follow a fixed order, shown in Table 2.4. It moves from what can be observed without asking (appearance, behaviour, speech) through what must be asked about (mood, thought, perception) to what must be formally tested (cognition), and ends with the patient's understanding of his own condition. British trainees often remember the headings with the mnemonic ASEPTIC: Appearance and behaviour, Speech, Emotion (mood and affect), Perception, Thought, Insight and judgement, Cognition. American texts often compress the MSE further; Barlow and Durand, for example, describe five categories: appearance and behaviour, thought processes, mood and affect, intellectual functioning and sensorium (awareness of the surroundings, which Section 2.6 calls consciousness and orientation). The contents are the same; only the packaging differs.

Table 2.4 The headings of the mental status examination, in the order used in most Indian case records.
HeadingThe question it answersTaught in
General appearance and behaviourWhat does the person look like and how does he behave and relate to me?2.4
Psychomotor activityIs movement increased, decreased or abnormal?2.4
SpeechHow does he talk: rate, volume, tone, amount, latency, coherence?2.4
Mood and affectWhat does he say he feels, and what emotion do I see?2.4
ThoughtStream, form, content and possession of thought2.5
PerceptionHallucinations, illusions, depersonalisation and derealisation2.5
Cognitive functionsConsciousness, orientation, attention, memory, intelligence, abstraction2.6
Judgement and insightCan he judge sensibly, and does he know he is ill?2.6
Exam trap

The MSE is cross-sectional (the present state); the case history is longitudinal. A sign is observed by the clinician; a symptom is reported by the patient. Descriptive psychopathology and the phenomenological method go back to Karl Jaspers' General Psychopathology (1913), who also taught that form, not content, is what matters most for diagnosis. In older American usage "sensorium" means awareness of the surroundings (consciousness and orientation), not the sense organs.

General appearance and behaviour

The examination starts with what a stranger would notice, organised so that nothing is missed. The clinician records:

  • Apparent age compared with the stated age. Looking much older than one's years suggests long-standing alcohol dependence, chronic physical or mental illness, severe depression or a hard life; looking much younger is less common and is worth a note.
  • Build and nourishment. Thin and wasted (anorexia nervosa, severe depression, self-neglect, physical illness), obese (some antipsychotic and other medicines cause marked weight gain), or showing recent weight loss: a belt pulled to a new hole, a collar that has become too loose.
  • Grooming, hygiene and dress. Well kempt or unkempt; clean or dirty; dressed appropriately for age, sex, season, setting and culture. A man in mania may arrive in a bright shirt with a garland and three rings, talking to everyone in the corridor; a woman with chronic schizophrenia may have uncut nails, matted hair and a body odour she does not notice; a person with dementia may have buttoned his shirt wrongly; a girl with anorexia nervosa may be wearing several loose layers that hide her body; long sleeves in the Mysuru summer can hide the scars of self-harm.
  • Facial expression. Sad, anxious, angry, vacant, perplexed (bewildered, as if trying to make sense of something strange, common early in an acute psychosis and in delirium), smiling to himself, or mask-like and unblinking, as in parkinsonism.
  • Posture and gait. Slumped, stooped, rigid, tense on the edge of the chair, or holding an odd posture (see psychomotor activity below).
  • Physical signs: scars, cuts and burns (note where, how old, how many), injection marks, tremor, the smell of alcohol, a goitre or yellowed eyes. The MSE is not a physical examination, but it should not ignore one in front of it.
  • Hallucinatory behaviour: muttering, smiling or laughing to himself, pausing in mid-sentence as if listening, turning towards a corner of the room, answering when no one has spoken, putting cotton in his ears. These are recorded as observations ("appeared to be listening and muttered in reply twice during the interview"); whether he is actually hallucinating is asked about later (Section 2.5).
  • Level of consciousness, noted in passing: alert, drowsy, fluctuating. Its formal assessment belongs to the cognitive examination (Section 2.6).

Every one of these observations has to be read against the person's culture and circumstances, and Indian clinics test this constantly. Vibhuti or kumkum on the forehead, a sacred thread, a black thread or a tabeez worn against the evil eye, a burqa, an uncut beard, a saree draped in the village style or old, much-mended clothes are not eccentricities and not signs of neglect. The useful distinction is between poverty and self-neglect: old clothes that are clean and carefully worn tell you about the family's income; unwashed clothes on a person whose family is well off tell you about the person.

Exam trap

The MSE records what is observed, so "hallucinatory behaviour" (muttering, smiling to self, listening postures) is noted under appearance and behaviour, while the patient's report of hearing voices goes under perception. "Perplexity" is a facial expression of bewilderment, not a disorder of thought. And remember the cultural rule: religious marks, traditional dress and poverty are not evidence of psychopathology.

Attitude towards the examiner, eye contact and rapport

The next heading records how the person relates to the clinician. The common descriptions are cooperative, uncooperative, guarded (answers cautiously, gives little away), suspicious (watches the clinician, asks why notes are being taken), hostile or irritable, evasive (avoids certain topics), defensive, ingratiating (eager to please), seductive, over-familiar or disinhibited (calls the clinician by his first name, comments on his clothes, as is common in mania), demanding, and indifferent or apathetic (shows little interest in the interview at all). These are not character judgements. A suspicious attitude may be the first clue to a persecutory belief, an over-familiar one to an elevated mood, an indifferent one to the negative symptoms of a psychosis or to severe depression, and a guarded one simply to a teenager brought by his parents. The clinician's own reaction is information too: feeling irritated, bored, protective or oddly uneasy with a client is worth noticing, though not writing into the record as fact.

Eye contact is recorded here: maintained, avoided (eyes down, as in depression, shame or social anxiety), fleeting, staring, or wandering around the room (as in a distractible manic patient or a hallucinating one). Section 2.2 warned that SOLER's "good eye contact" is a cultural rule, and the warning applies to the MSE as well: a young village woman who keeps her eyes lowered before a male doctor is following a social convention. Finally the clinician grades rapport, using the scale you met in Section 2.2: established easily, established with difficulty, or not established. With Ravi it came slowly; he answered more freely after the first fifteen minutes, and his eyes rose from the floor when he talked about cricket.

Psychomotor activity and abnormal movements

Psychomotor activity is the overall amount and speed of a person's bodily movement, which in mental illness rises and falls with the speed of mental activity. When it is increased, the clinician distinguishes two patterns. Psychomotor agitation is excessive, purposeless motor activity driven by inner tension: hand-wringing, pacing, picking at the skin or clothes, being unable to sit still, getting up and sitting down again. It is common in agitated depression, severe anxiety and delirium. The overactivity of mania is different: it is purposeful but excessive and distractible, a person starting ten tasks and finishing none. When activity is decreased, the term is psychomotor retardation: slowed movement and speech, few spontaneous gestures, a long delay before each answer, an effortful, heavy quality to everything. Barlow and Durand note it as a sign of severe depression, and it is also seen in hypothyroidism and parkinsonism. At its extreme lies stupor, an absence of voluntary movement and speech in a person who is not unconscious (the eyes may follow the examiner, and the patient may later recall what was said). Stupor can be depressive, catatonic, dissociative or organic, and it is a medical emergency, because the patient is not eating or drinking.

Some abnormal movements form a cluster with a long history. The German psychiatrist Karl Ludwig Kahlbaum (1828 to 1899) described catatonia in 1874 as a syndrome of motor signs: stupor and mutism alternating with excitement, strange postures, rigidity and imitation. The German psychiatrist Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926) later folded catatonia into his concept of dementia praecox, and for most of the twentieth century it was treated as a subtype of schizophrenia. DSM-5 (2013) abandoned that view. Catatonia is now recognised as a syndrome that occurs with depressive and bipolar disorders, with psychotic disorders and with medical conditions, and DSM-5-TR requires three or more of the twelve signs in Table 2.5 to diagnose it. Several are tested at the bedside. To test for waxy flexibility, the examiner gently moves the patient's arm and feels a slight, even resistance, like bending a candle; in catalepsy a posture that the examiner has placed the patient in is then held against gravity, sometimes for minutes. Older Indian and British texts add further signs: automatic obedience (doing whatever one is told, regardless of consequences), mitgehen (moving a limb in whatever direction the examiner's lightest touch suggests, even after being told to resist), and ambitendency (beginning a movement and stopping it, over and over, as if two opposed impulses were at work).

Table 2.5 The twelve signs of catatonia in DSM-5-TR (three or more are needed), with what the clinician sees.
SignWhat the clinician sees
StuporNo psychomotor activity; not actively relating to the surroundings
CatalepsyA posture placed by the examiner is held against gravity
