Waxy flexibilitySlight, even resistance when the examiner moves a limb
MutismNo, or very little, speech (not due to aphasia)
NegativismOpposes or does not respond to instructions
PosturingSpontaneously holds a posture against gravity
MannerismOdd, caricatured version of a normal, goal-directed action
StereotypyRepetitive, abnormally frequent movement with no goal
AgitationExcitement not influenced by external stimuli (catatonic excitement)
GrimacingFixed or repeated distorted facial expressions
EcholaliaRepeats the examiner's words
EchopraxiaImitates the examiner's movements

Notice the difference between two signs that students often confuse. A mannerism is a normal, goal-directed action performed in an odd, stilted or exaggerated way: a man who salutes every time he picks up a glass, or who writes his name with an elaborate flourish of the whole arm. A stereotypy is a repetitive movement with no goal at all: rocking, rubbing the same spot on the thigh for an hour, tapping in a fixed rhythm. Mannerisms decorate purposeful acts; stereotypies replace them. Both differ from a tic, a sudden, rapid, recurrent, non-rhythmic movement or vocalisation (a blink, a shoulder shrug, a sniff) that the person feels as an urge and can suppress only briefly, and from a compulsion, a repetitive act (washing, checking, touching) that the person feels driven to perform to reduce anxiety or prevent a feared event. Catatonic excitement, too, is a motor sign: purposeless, excessive activity unconnected with anything around the patient. It is not one of the first-rank symptoms of Section 2.5.

Finally, the clinician looks for movements that are caused by treatment, because they are easily mistaken for psychopathology. Antipsychotic medicines can cause an acute dystonia (a sudden, painful muscle spasm that twists the neck or rolls the eyes upwards, usually within days of starting); akathisia, a subjective inner restlessness with an inability to keep still, the patient shifting from foot to foot and crossing and uncrossing his legs; drug-induced parkinsonism, with tremor, rigidity, a shuffling gait and a mask-like face; and, after long use, tardive dyskinesia, involuntary movements of the mouth, tongue and face such as lip-smacking, chewing and tongue protrusion. Akathisia is the dangerous one to miss: it looks like agitation, and if the dose of the drug causing it is increased, the restlessness gets worse. The parkinsonian mask is the other trap, because a face that cannot move looks like an emotion that is not there (see affect, below).

Exam trap

Waxy flexibility is the slight, even resistance felt as the examiner moves the limb; catalepsy is holding a posture the examiner has imposed; posturing is holding a posture the patient chose himself. A mannerism is an odd version of a goal-directed act; a stereotypy is a repetitive act with no goal. Echolalia echoes words, echopraxia echoes movements. Catatonia was described by Kahlbaum (1874), not Kraepelin or Bleuler, and in DSM-5-TR it is not tied to schizophrenia. Akathisia (a drug side effect) can masquerade as psychomotor agitation, and psychomotor agitation is a sign, not a disorder of thought.

Speech

The clinician listens to how the person speaks before attending to what he says, because the physical characteristics of speech change early in many conditions and are hard to fake. The usual dimensions are set out in Table 2.6.

Table 2.6 The dimensions of speech recorded in the MSE.
DimensionNormal descriptionAbnormal descriptions
Rate (speed)Normal rateSlow; rapid; pressured
Volume (intensity)AudibleLoud; soft; whispering
Tone, pitch, prosodyNormally modulatedMonotonous; high-pitched; hoarse
Quantity (productivity)AdequateIncreased; poverty of speech; mutism
SpontaneitySpontaneousSpeaks only when questioned
Reaction time (latency)NormalIncreased; decreased
Ease, articulationFluent, clearHesitant; stammering; slurred (dysarthria)
Relevance, coherence, goalRelevant, coherent, goal-directedIrrelevant; incoherent; not goal-directed

Take them in turn. Rate may be slow, as in depression, dementia and hypothyroidism, or rapid, as in mania, anxiety and hyperthyroidism. Pressure of speech is speech that is rapid, increased in amount, often loud, and difficult or impossible to interrupt: the patient talks over the clinician's questions and keeps going after the interview has ended. It is the speech of mania. Volume ranges from loud (excitement, mania, some hearing loss) to soft and barely audible (depression, shyness, a suspicious patient who does not want to be overheard). Tone and prosody concern the melody of speech: the rise and fall of pitch, the stress and rhythm that carry emotion and meaning. Normal speech is modulated; speech that stays on one note is monotonous, as in depression, the negative symptoms of schizophrenia and parkinsonism.

Quantity is how much the person says. At one extreme is a flood of talk; at the other is poverty of speech (also called alogia or laconic speech), in which replies are brief, often a single word, and nothing is offered spontaneously ("How have you been?" "Fine." "And your sleep?" "Bad."). Complete absence of speech is mutism, seen in stupor, catatonia, severe depression and some dissociative states; in children who speak freely at home but not at school it is selective mutism. Closely related is spontaneity: does the person ever volunteer anything, or speak only when questioned?

Reaction time, or latency, is the time between the end of the question and the beginning of the answer. It is increased in depression, in hypothyroidism and in some patients with schizophrenia, who may pause so long that the clinician wonders whether the question was heard. It is decreased in mania, where the patient may start answering before the question is finished. Ravi's latencies were several seconds long, and the clinician noted that they shortened as rapport grew.

Next come ease and articulation. Speech may be hesitant, stammered, or slurred. Dysarthria is a difficulty in articulating words (the speech is slurred, thick or poorly formed) because of a problem with the muscles of speech or their nerve supply, as in intoxication, cerebellar disease, stroke or some drug side effects; the language itself is intact. It must be kept separate from dysphasia (or aphasia), a disorder of language in which the person has trouble finding, choosing or understanding words, because of damage to the language areas of the brain (taught in the Physiological Psychology book). A patient with dysarthria says the right words badly; a patient with dysphasia says the wrong words clearly.

Last come relevance, coherence and goal-directedness: whether the answers address the questions asked, whether the speech hangs together as connected discourse, and whether it reaches its point. These three are where speech shades into thought. The clinician cannot see thinking; he infers it from speech. When the answers wander, never arrive, or make no sense, the disturbance is recorded under the form of thought (Section 2.5), with a verbatim sample.

A few unusual patterns of speech are worth knowing by name. Verbigeration is the meaningless repetition of the same words or phrases, sometimes for hours, seen in chronic schizophrenia and catatonia. Palilalia is the repetition of one's own last words or phrases, often faster and faster, seen in parkinsonism and other brain disease. Echolalia, repeating the examiner's words, is one of the catatonic signs. Rhyming and punning are common in mania. And talking past the point, giving approximate answers that show the right answer is known ("How many legs does a cow have?" "Five." "What colour is snow?" "Black."), was described by Sigbert Ganser in 1898 in prisoners awaiting trial, and is still called the Ganser syndrome.

One practical point matters especially in India. Speech should be assessed in the patient's own language. A Kannada-speaking man answering a clinician's English questions will be slow, hesitant and short in his answers, and that tells you about his English, not about his mind.

Exam trap

Pressure of speech is a property of speech (rapid, copious, hard to interrupt); flight of ideas (Section 2.5) is a property of thought (rapid jumps between connected ideas). They usually occur together in mania, but they are recorded under different headings. Reaction time is increased in depression and decreased in mania. Dysarthria is a problem of articulation; dysphasia is a problem of language. Poverty of speech is a reduced amount of speech; mutism is its absence. Approximate answers belong to Ganser (1898).

Mood: what the patient reports

With mood and affect the MSE reaches emotion, and here two words that everyday English treats as near-synonyms acquire precise and different meanings. Mood is the pervasive and sustained emotional state that a person experiences over a period of time, days or weeks rather than minutes, and that colours the way he perceives himself and the world. It is subjective: only the patient can tell you what it is, so it is elicited by asking. Affect, the subject of the next subsection, is the observable expression of emotion from moment to moment during the interview: what the clinician sees in the face, hears in the voice and notices in posture and gesture. A useful analogy: mood is the emotional climate; affect is the emotional weather. A climate can be cold while a particular afternoon is warm, and a depressed patient may laugh at a joke for a moment without his mood having changed at all.

The clinician asks about mood with open questions first ("How have you been feeling in yourself?", "How are your spirits these days?") and then specific ones ("Have you been feeling low? Most of the day? Every day?"). The answer is recorded in the patient's own words, in quotation marks, which is the single most informative thing a mood record can contain: Ravi said, "I feel useless, like a burden on my family." Many clinicians also ask the patient to rate his mood from 0 (the worst he has ever felt) to 10 (the best), which gives a number to compare at the next visit; Ravi said "two". The clinician then adds the conventional label.

The labels most often used are these. Euthymic mood is mood within the normal range, neither depressed nor elevated; it is what you write for a person whose mood is unremarkable. Depressed mood is sadness, lowness or hopelessness. Dysphoria is an unpleasant mood, often a mixture of sadness, irritability, tension and discontent. Anxious mood is apprehension, worry and tension about what may happen. Irritable mood is being easily annoyed and provoked to anger. Elevated mood is more cheerful than usual, and Indian textbooks grade it in order of increasing intensity: euphoria (a mild elevation, an exaggerated feeling of well-being out of keeping with events, as in hypomania), elation (a moderate elevation, with confidence, enjoyment and increased activity, as in mania), exaltation (intense elation with grandiose ideas, as in severe mania) and ecstasy (a feeling of intense rapture, seen in the most severe, delirious forms of mania and also, sometimes with a sense of union with the divine, in religious experience, in epilepsy and with some drugs). Expansive mood is the lack of restraint in expressing one's feelings, often with an inflated sense of one's own importance. Apathy is the absence of feeling, interest and concern.

Two further terms describe what the patient cannot feel. Anhedonia, a word introduced by the French psychologist Théodule-Armand Ribot (1839 to 1916) in 1896, is the loss of the capacity to experience pleasure, a markedly diminished interest or pleasure in activities the person once enjoyed. Ravi used to play cricket every Sunday and follow every match on his phone; now, he said, "I don't feel like it. Even when India wins, nothing." In DSM-5-TR, anhedonia and depressed mood are the two core symptoms of a major depressive episode, at least one of which must be present (the episode itself is taught in the Psychiatric Disorders book). Alexithymia, a term introduced by the psychiatrist Peter Emanuel Sifneos (1920 to 2008) in 1973 (some sources say 1972), from Greek words meaning "no words for feelings", is a difficulty in recognising and describing one's own emotions; Sifneos first noticed it in patients with psychosomatic illnesses, who could describe their bodily symptoms in great detail but could say little about what they felt.

Beyond the label, the clinician records several features of the mood. Its intensity and pervasiveness: is the patient low all day, every day, or only in certain situations? Its reactivity: does it lift, even briefly, when something good happens, such as a visit from a friend? (Mood reactivity is preserved in what DSM calls depression with atypical features, and lost in melancholic depression.) And its diurnal variation, a regular change over the course of the day: in melancholic depression the mood is characteristically worst in the morning and lifts a little towards evening, while the confusion of delirium is often worst in the evening and at night (sundowning). Ravi's mood was worst in the early mornings, after he woke at three and lay awake.

Exam trap

Anhedonia is markedly diminished interest or pleasure in nearly all activities (Ribot, 1896). It is not excessive guilt, which is a disturbance of thought content (Section 2.5); not psychomotor agitation, which is a motor sign (described above); and not difficulty concentrating, which is a cognitive symptom (Section 2.6), although all four can appear in the same depressive episode. Alexithymia (Sifneos, 1973) is not being able to put feelings into words; it is not anhedonia. Euthymic means normal mood, not "happy". The grades of elevated mood run euphoria, elation, exaltation, ecstasy.

Affect: what the clinician observes

While the patient describes his mood, the clinician watches the affect. Because affect is observed, it is described along several dimensions at once, and each dimension has its own vocabulary.

The first is quality: which emotion is shown? Sad, anxious, angry, cheerful, fearful, perplexed. The second is range: how many different emotions appear over the interview? A person with a broad (or full) range moves naturally between seriousness, worry, amusement and sadness as the conversation moves. In restricted affect (or constricted affect) the range and intensity of expression are mildly reduced: the person shows some emotion, but less, and of fewer kinds, than you would expect. The third is intensity. Blunted affect is a severe reduction in the intensity of emotional expression: the face is largely immobile, the voice flat, the gestures few, even when the patient talks about things that matter to him. Flat affect is the absence or near-absence of any sign of emotional expression. Restricted, blunted and flat therefore form a sequence of increasing severity, as Figure 2.3 shows, and blunted and flat affect are among the negative symptoms of schizophrenia (DSM-5-TR calls them diminished emotional expression). Some texts add shallow affect, emotion that is expressed but seems to lack depth, and at the other end heightened or exaggerated affect, emotion shown more intensely than the situation warrants.

The fourth dimension is reactivity (sometimes called mobility): does the affect respond to the conversation, softening when a painful topic comes up, brightening at a joke or at the mention of something the patient loves? An affect that stays the same whatever is discussed is described as fixed or unreactive. The fifth is appropriateness (or congruence): does the emotion shown fit what the patient is talking about and the situation he is in? Inappropriate affect (also called incongruous affect) is a mismatch between the emotion expressed and the content of speech or thought: a woman who giggles while describing her mother's death, a man who looks angry while saying he is perfectly content. Older European psychiatry called incongruous affect parathymia (Bleuler's word for it); Indian notes reserve the term for its extreme form, expressing an emotion exactly opposite to what the circumstances call for (laughing at news of a death), and use paramimia for a lack of unity between the different channels of expression (smiling face, tearful voice). Neither word is used in DSM-5-TR, so treat them as exam vocabulary rather than as standard clinical terms. Inappropriate affect is one of the features Eugen Bleuler regarded as fundamental to schizophrenia (Section 2.5).

The sixth dimension is stability. Normal affect changes, but in step with the conversation. Labile affect shows repeated, rapid and abrupt shifts between emotions, tears one moment and laughter the next, out of proportion to anything that has been said. It is seen in mania, in some personality disorders and in brain disease. Its most extreme form, emotional incontinence (pathological laughing and crying, also called pseudobulbar affect), is sudden, uncontrollable crying or laughing that the patient does not actually feel, and it occurs after strokes and in diseases such as multiple sclerosis and motor neuron disease. Some formats add a seventh dimension, communicability: can the clinician feel with the patient, or does the emotion fail to reach him? An experienced clinician's sense that he cannot make emotional contact with a patient is itself worth recording.

Finally, the clinician compares mood with affect. Usually they match: Ravi said he felt useless and he looked sad. When they do not, the mismatch is important. A patient who says "I am fine, doctor, there is nothing wrong" while tears run down his face has a depressed affect that contradicts his reported mood, and you met the skill for handling this, confrontation, in Section 2.2. Two famous mismatches deserve names. One is la belle indifférence, a striking lack of concern about a dramatic disability, such as a paralysed arm, that is sometimes seen in conversion (functional neurological) disorder, though it is no longer considered a reliable sign of it. The other is the "smiling depression" of a patient who shows a social smile throughout and may be at high risk precisely because no one has noticed (Section 2.7).

Table 2.7 The vocabulary of affect.
TermDimensionWhat the clinician sees
Broad (full)RangeNormal variety of emotions, in step with the conversation
Restricted (constricted)Range, intensityMild reduction in range and intensity
BluntedIntensitySevere reduction in intensity of expression
FlatIntensityAbsence or near-absence of expression
Unreactive (fixed)ReactivityDoes not change with the topic
Inappropriate (incongruous)AppropriatenessEmotion does not fit the content or situation
LabileStabilityRapid, abrupt shifts out of proportion to events
Emotional incontinenceStabilityUncontrollable laughing or crying not felt inside
Clinical link

Before writing "blunted affect", ask three questions. Is the patient on an antipsychotic that could be producing a parkinsonian mask? Is he severely depressed, so that the reduced expression is depressive retardation? Is he from a family or culture in which showing emotion to a stranger, or to a doctor, is simply not done? Each of these can mimic the negative symptoms of schizophrenia, and each leads to a different treatment. The same caution applies in reverse: a patient whose affect looks normal can still be in great distress.

Exam trap

Mood is subjective (what the patient reports, sustained over time); affect is objective (what the clinician observes, moment to moment). Some Indian case formats write the same pair as "subjective affect" and "objective affect", so read the question carefully. The order of severity is restricted, then blunted, then flat. Labile is a problem of stability (rapid shifts); inappropriate is a problem of fit (emotion that does not match the content). A patient can have labile but appropriate affect, or stable but inappropriate affect. Euthymic describes mood, not affect.

Putting it together: the first half of Ravi's MSE

Here is how the first part of Ravi's MSE was written, a few minutes after the interview. Notice that it contains no diagnosis, that every judgement has an observation behind it, and that his own words appear wherever they could.

Ravi's MSE (first half)

General appearance and behaviour. A thin young man who looks his stated age; unshaven, hair uncombed, clothes clean but creased; belt fastened at a new hole. Sat hunched forward with hands between his knees. Eye contact poor initially, mostly towards the floor, improving as the interview went on. No scars or marks of self-harm. No hallucinatory behaviour. Alert. Attitude: cooperative but reticent; rapport established with some difficulty. Psychomotor activity: generally decreased; few spontaneous movements; slowed. No abnormal movements. Speech: soft, slow and monotonous; reduced in quantity; spoke mainly in answer to questions; reaction time increased (several seconds), shortening later; relevant and coherent, goal-directed. Mood: subjectively "I feel useless, like a burden on my family"; rates it 2 out of 10; worst in the early morning; loss of pleasure in cricket ("Even when India wins, nothing"). Affect: depressed; restricted in range; reactive (brief smile when speaking about cricket, tearful when speaking of his mother's gold); appropriate to the content of his thought; stable; congruent with mood.

Every word of that record can be checked by another clinician next week, and that is the point. But it is still only the outside of Ravi. What is he thinking about as he sits there, and is he perceiving anything that is not there? Those questions take us inside, to the stream, form, content and possession of thought, and to perception.

2.5 The Mental Status Examination II: Thought and PerceptionMedium yield · 2 Qs

A clinician can watch a patient move and hear him speak, but no one has ever observed a thought. Everything the MSE says about thinking is an inference from what the patient says, and from how he says it. The American psychiatrist Nancy Coover Andreasen (born 1938) made this point the foundation of her work on what is usually called thought disorder: since thought is known only through speech, she argued, the honest name for what clinicians record is a disorder of thought, language and communication. Keep that in mind throughout this section. When you write "flight of ideas" or "delusion of persecution", you are describing a sample of speech and drawing a conclusion about the mind behind it, which is why the MSE asks for the sample, in the patient's own words, alongside the conclusion.

This section classifies the disturbances of thought and of perception as the clinician meets and records them, each with a short example of what the patient actually says. The examples are composites, written to show the phenomenon clearly. The disorders in which these phenomena occur (schizophrenia, mania, depression, obsessive-compulsive disorder and the rest) are described in the Psychiatric Disorders book; here the aim is to recognise the phenomenon and to name it correctly.

Four headings for thought

The British descriptive tradition that Indian case records follow, set out in texts such as Frank Fish's Clinical Psychopathology and Andrew Sims' Symptoms in the Mind, examines thought under four headings, and the headings are worth learning precisely because examiners ask which heading a phenomenon belongs to.

  • The stream of thought is its flow: how fast thoughts come (the tempo) and whether the flow is continuous or interrupted (the continuity).
  • The form of thought is its organisation: whether ideas are linked to one another in a logical, understandable sequence that reaches a goal. Disturbances of form are called formal thought disorder.
  • The content of thought is what the person thinks about: his preoccupations, worries, fears and beliefs, including delusions.
  • The possession of thought is the normal, taken-for-granted experience that one's thoughts are one's own and under one's own control. It is disturbed when a patient feels that thoughts are being put into his mind, taken out of it or broadcast to others.

American textbooks and many examination keys use a simpler division into two: thought process (the stream and form together: how the person thinks) and thought content (what he thinks, with the possession phenomena counted as delusions of control). The two systems describe the same phenomena; they draw the boundaries differently. Table 2.8 lays them side by side, and Figure 2.4, in the subsection on form, shows how the main disorders of stream and form differ in the path they take from a question to its answer.

Table 2.8 The four headings for disorders of thought, with the American two-heading equivalent.
HeadingThe question it asksMain examplesAmerican heading
Stream (tempo)How fast do thoughts come?Flight of ideas, pressure of thought, retardation, poverty of thoughtThought process
Stream (continuity)Is the flow interrupted?Thought block, perseverationThought process
FormAre ideas logically linked?Loosening, derailment, tangentiality, circumstantiality, neologisms, word salad, clang associationsThought process
ContentWhat does he think about?Delusions, overvalued ideas, phobias, preoccupations, suicidal and homicidal ideasThought content
PossessionAre the thoughts his own?Thought insertion, withdrawal, broadcasting; obsessions (British usage)Thought content
Exam trap

"Circumstantiality is a disorder of ..." The answer is form of thought (thought process). It is never a disorder of content (it says nothing about what the patient believes), of possession (the patient's thoughts feel entirely his own) or of perception. A complication you should know about: British texts in the tradition of Fish's Clinical Psychopathology put circumstantiality under the stream of thought (its tempo, beside flight of ideas and retardation), and many Indian notes follow it, listing circumstantiality and tangentiality under stream because they change the way thinking flows towards its goal. American texts (Kaplan and Sadock's among them) put all of these, flight of ideas included, under form or thought process. The keyed answer follows the American usage, and this chapter's Table 2.8 does too for circumstantiality and tangentiality. So if "form" or "thought process" is offered, choose it; only if a question offers "stream" and not "form" should you choose stream.

Disorders of the stream: tempo and continuity

Start with speed. The most famous disorder of tempo is flight of ideas: thoughts follow one another very rapidly, there is no general direction to the thinking, and each idea is linked to the next by chance associations, by rhymes and puns, or by something the patient has just noticed in the room. The crucial point is that every individual link can be understood, even though the train as a whole goes nowhere. Asked how he travelled to the clinic, a man in a manic episode says:

"By bus, a big red bus, red like the chillies my mother grows, she grows them for the market, market prices are going up, up, up, I will buy the whole market, I am going into business, doctor, business class, I will fly business class to Dubai, do you want to come?"

The clinician can trace every step (bus, red, chillies, market, prices, business, flying), but the question has been left far behind. Flight of ideas is characteristic of mania and usually comes with pressure of speech (Section 2.4). A milder form, in which the patient wanders through many irrelevances but does eventually return to the topic, is called prolixity. Alongside the observed flight goes a subjective experience, pressure of thought or racing thoughts: "My thoughts are going too fast, I can't keep up with them, they are crowding my head."

At the other end of the scale, retardation of thought (inhibition of thought, sometimes called bradyphrenia) is a slowing of thinking: fewer ideas come, each one arrives slowly, and the patient finds it hard to think at all. "My mind is slow, like it is full of mud. I try to think and nothing comes." It is common in depression and goes with the psychomotor retardation and long reaction times of Section 2.4. Poverty of thought is a reduction in the number of thoughts, so that the patient's mind feels empty; it shows itself as poverty of speech and is one of the negative symptoms of schizophrenia, though it also occurs in severe depression and dementia.

Now continuity. In thought block (thought blocking) the train of thought stops suddenly and completely, often in the middle of a sentence, leaving a blank. After a pause, which may last many seconds, the patient may start again on a different subject, and he cannot say what he was going to say:

"My brother came to the hostel and he told me that... [silence of about twenty seconds] ... I don't know. It went. What were you asking?"

Everyone occasionally loses the thread when tired or anxious, and that is not thought block. True blocking is sudden, complete and unexplained, and it is seen most characteristically in schizophrenia. Keep it separate from a phenomenon it is often confused with. Thought block is what the clinician observes; if the patient explains the blank by saying that someone or something took his thoughts away, he is describing thought withdrawal, a disorder of possession (see the subsection on possession below). The same observed gap can be one or the other, and only questioning can tell.

Perseveration is the persistent repetition of the same word, idea or response beyond the point at which it was relevant, so that it keeps returning in answer to new questions:

Clinician: "What is your name?" Patient: "Ramesh." Clinician: "Where do you live?" Patient: "Ramesh." Clinician: "What did you eat this morning?" Patient: "Ramesh... Ramesh."

Perseveration strongly suggests organic brain disease, especially of the frontal lobes, and is common in dementia; it also occurs in severe psychosis. It is one of three kinds of repetition you must keep apart. Perseveration repeats the patient's own earlier response when a new one is called for; verbigeration (Section 2.4) repeats meaningless words or phrases, as a stereotypy of speech; and echolalia repeats the examiner's words.

Exam trap

In flight of ideas each link between ideas is understandable (rhymes, puns, distractions) even though the goal is lost; in loosening of associations (next subsection) the links themselves cannot be followed. Thought block is an observed sudden stop; thought withdrawal is the patient's belief that his thoughts have been removed by an outside force. Perseveration (repeating one's own previous response, typical of organic brain disease) is not echolalia (repeating the examiner) and not verbigeration (repeating meaningless phrases).

Disorders of form: when the links break

Normal thinking is goal-directed: asked a question, a person moves from the question to the answer by a chain of ideas, each linked logically to the next. The disorders of form are the ways in which that chain bends, wanders or breaks. Figure 2.4 draws them as paths.

The idea that the links between ideas can loosen comes from Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939), the Swiss psychiatrist who introduced the word schizophrenia in 1908 and set out his view of the illness in his 1911 monograph Dementia Praecox, or the Group of Schizophrenias. For Bleuler the fundamental disturbance was a "breaking of associative threads", and he listed four fundamental symptoms that students remember as the four As: disturbance of associations, disturbance of affect (blunting and incongruity, Section 2.4), ambivalence (holding opposite feelings or wishes towards the same thing at the same time) and autism (withdrawal into an inner world; Bleuler's word, later borrowed for a quite different developmental condition).

In loosening of associations the connection between successive ideas is lost or so remote that the listener cannot follow it. The patient moves from one topic to another with no apparent link, and does not seem to notice:

"I have been tired lately. The government should build more temples near the lake. My chappals are from the Bata shop, and the cricket team needs a spinner because the moon is closer this month."

Andreasen preferred the term derailment, because "loosening" had come to be used loosely: in derailment the patient's ideas slip off one track onto another that is completely unrelated or only obliquely related. British clinicians sometimes call the abrupt, unexpected jumps "knight's move thinking", after the chess piece that moves sideways as well as forwards. Compare the example with the manic patient's flight of ideas above. There you could trace each step; here you cannot. That is the single most useful distinction in the whole subject.

Tangentiality is replying to a question in an oblique, tangential or even irrelevant way. The answer starts somewhere near the question, then heads off in another direction and never returns to it:

Clinician: "What brought you to the hospital today?" Patient: "Hospitals in Mysuru are very crowded. My uncle worked in the railway hospital. The railways were built by the British, and the British were very clever people with their clocks."

Circumstantiality is also indirect, but it arrives. The patient takes a long, roundabout route full of unnecessary and trivial detail and side-tracks, and the listener grows impatient, but he does eventually reach the point and answer the question:

Clinician: "When did your sleep problem start?" Patient: "It was the Dasara holidays, my sister had come home from her engineering college in Hassan, the Hassan bus is always late, it came at nine that night, and my mother had made obbattu, which she makes only for festivals, and I ate three, and that was the week, doctor, about four months ago, that I stopped sleeping."

Circumstantiality is seen classically in epilepsy (especially of the temporal lobe), in people with obsessional personalities, in intellectual disability and early dementia, and in anxious or pedantic people; a mild degree is normal in many talkers. The difference from tangentiality is simply whether the goal is reached, as Figure 2.4 shows.

At the extreme of disorganisation, speech loses its structure even within sentences. Word salad (also called schizophasia, or incoherence in Andreasen's terms) is a jumble of words and phrases with no logical or grammatical connection, so that nothing can be understood: "Tree when blue the spoon running government sad of the Tuesday." DSM-5-TR notes that speech this disorganised resembles the speech of receptive aphasia; a neurological examination helps tell them apart. A neologism is a new word coined by the patient, or an ordinary word used in a private, idiosyncratic sense that only he understands: "The neighbours are sending glorbination through the wall to stop my thoughts." A related error, which Andreasen called a word approximation, builds a new expression from ordinary words ("I keep the time-shoe on my wrist" for a watch). In clang association words are chosen for their sound rather than their meaning, so that speech moves by rhymes and puns: "I am fine, wine, mine, divine, the line is mine, shall we dine?" Clanging is commonest in mania and also occurs in schizophrenia.

Not every disorder of form is a matter of too much or too strange. Andreasen drew a sharp line between poverty of speech (Section 2.4), in which the patient says very little, and poverty of content of speech, in which the amount of speech is adequate but it conveys little information, because it is vague, over-abstract, over-concrete, repetitive or stereotyped:

Clinician: "What are your plans for the future?" Patient: "The future is the future. Life has its things, and the things of life are what they are, so one has to think about the things that come, and the things will come."

Two further terms complete the list. Over-inclusion, a term associated with the American psychiatrist and psychologist Norman Cameron, is a widening of the boundaries of concepts so that ideas and objects that are not closely connected are grouped together and irrelevant details are included in thinking. And thinking may be concrete, tied to the literal and the immediate and unable to deal in abstractions, which is tested by asking the meaning of proverbs (Section 2.6). Table 2.9 collects the disorders of stream and form with the patient's words beside each.

Table 2.9 Disorders of stream and form of thought, as heard in the interview.
PhenomenonWhat the clinician noticesThe patient says (short example)
Flight of ideasRapid jumps; each link understandable; goal lost"Red bus, red chillies, chilli market, I'll buy the market"
RetardationFew, slow thoughts"My mind is full of mud"
Thought blockSudden stop, blank, new topic"He told me that... [silence] ... it went"
PerseverationSame response to new questions"Ramesh... Ramesh"
Loosening (derailment)Links between ideas cannot be followed"More temples. My chappals are from Bata"
TangentialityOblique answer that never returns"Hospitals are crowded... the British had clocks"
CircumstantialityLong detour of detail, but reaches the point"... and that was the week I stopped sleeping"
Word saladNo connection even within sentences"Tree when blue the spoon running"
NeologismInvented word or private meaning"They send glorbination through the wall"
Clang associationWords linked by sound, not meaning"Fine, wine, mine, divine"
Poverty of contentEnough words, little information"The future is the future, life has its things"
Key study: Andreasen's thought, language and communication disorders (1979)

For most of the twentieth century, clinicians assumed that formal thought disorder was the hallmark of schizophrenia, but they could not agree on what its terms meant, and "loose associations" was applied to almost any speech that puzzled the listener. Nancy Andreasen set out to fix the vocabulary. In two papers published in 1979 she gave each kind of thought, language and communication disorder a precise definition with examples, showed that trained raters could use the definitions reliably, and then rated the speech of 113 patients with mania, depression and schizophrenia.

The result upset the old assumption. Formal thought disorder turned out to be common in mania as well as in schizophrenia: "positive" forms such as derailment, tangentiality, incoherence, illogicality and loss of goal were found about equally often in both, so that, as Andreasen put it, associative loosening "can no longer be considered pathognomonic of schizophrenia". Pressure of speech, distractible speech, clanging and circumstantiality pointed more towards mania, while the "negative" group, poverty of speech and poverty of content of speech, was more characteristic of schizophrenia; telling the positive from the negative forms often allowed the two diagnoses to be separated. Some celebrated forms, such as neologisms and blocking, turned out to be so rare that they added little to diagnosis. The definitions became the Scale for the Assessment of Thought, Language and Communication (TLC), and the lesson stuck: formal thought disorder is a sign to be described precisely, not proof of any one diagnosis.

Exam trap

Circumstantiality reaches the goal after a detour; tangentiality never does. Flight of ideas (mania) has understandable links; loosening of associations or derailment (Bleuler's term and Andreasen's preferred one) does not. Poverty of speech is too few words; poverty of content is enough words saying almost nothing. A neologism is a single new word; word salad is a stream of real words with no connection. Clang associations follow sound (rhymes and puns). Bleuler's four As are associations, affect, ambivalence and autism; hallucinations and delusions were, for him, accessory symptoms, not fundamental ones.

Disorders of content I: delusions

Karl Jaspers called the delusion "the basic characteristic of madness", and it is the most important disturbance of thought content. A delusion is a false, firmly held belief that is not amenable to change in the light of conflicting evidence or reasoned argument, and that is out of keeping with the person's educational, cultural and social background. DSM-5-TR puts the core of it in one line: delusions are "fixed beliefs that are not amenable to change in light of conflicting evidence". Each part of the definition does work. "Firmly held" separates a delusion from a passing suspicion. "Not amenable to change" is the clinical test: the clinician gently offers an alternative explanation ("Could there be any other reason the neighbour looked at you?") and sees whether the belief moves. "Out of keeping with the background" excludes beliefs that the person's community shares.

Jaspers described three external characteristics of delusions: an extraordinary conviction, a subjective certainty that nothing can shake; imperviousness to other experiences and to compelling counter-argument; and an impossibility or falsity of content. He also saw that falsity is not the essence. A man with delusional jealousy may, by chance, have a wife who really is unfaithful; his belief is still delusional, because of the way he reached it (from the colour of a scooter parked outside, say) and the way he holds it. What makes a belief delusional is less what is believed than how it arose and how it is held.

That point leads to Jaspers' most influential distinction. A secondary delusion (Jaspers called it a delusion-like idea) can be understood as arising from something else in the patient's mental state: a depressed man who believes he has ruined his family, a woman who hears voices threatening her and concludes that she is being persecuted, a manic patient who feels so powerful that he decides he is a prophet. A primary delusion cannot be traced psychologically to any other experience; it arises directly and is, in Jaspers' sense, ununderstandable. Primary delusional experience takes several forms. In delusional mood (Wahnstimmung) the patient feels that something strange and sinister is going on around him, that the world has subtly changed, without knowing what: "Something is happening in the village. Everyone is behaving differently. I don't know what it is, but it is about me." The relief when a full delusion crystallises out of this uneasy mood can be striking. In delusional perception a normal perception is suddenly given a delusional meaning, with no understandable link between the two:

"I saw the neighbour's dog lift its front paw, and at that moment I knew that I had been chosen to save India."

The perception (a dog lifting its paw) is real and accurately perceived; the meaning is delusional; and the two are joined by nothing the listener can follow. Delusional perception is one of Schneider's first-rank symptoms (below); delusional mood is not. An autochthonous delusion (a sudden delusional idea) arrives fully formed, out of the blue, without any perception to trigger it, and a delusional memory gives a remembered event a new delusional significance ("Now I understand why the teacher gave me that pen in Class 5; it was a sign I was royal").

Several other descriptions are added in the record. Delusions are called bizarre delusions when they are clearly implausible, not understandable to people of the same culture and not derived from ordinary life experience (DSM-5-TR's example is the belief that an outside force has removed one's internal organs and replaced them with someone else's without leaving a scar), and non-bizarre when they could conceivably happen (being followed by the police). They are mood-congruent when their content fits the mood (guilt, poverty, disease and death in depression; power and special identity in mania) and mood-incongruent when it does not. They may be systematised, forming one logically elaborated system that the patient can defend in detail, or unsystematised and fragmentary. And they may be shared: a belief that passes from one person to a close companion who lives with him is the shared delusion that Charles Lasègue and Jules Falret called folie à deux in 1877.

Delusions are classified by content, and Table 2.10 lists the types examiners ask about, with the patient's words for each.

Table 2.10 Delusions classified by content, with eponyms and a typical statement.
Delusion ofThe beliefThe patient says
PersecutionHe is being harmed, harassed, plotted against, poisoned or followed"My neighbours mix something in our well; they want my land."
ReferenceEvents, remarks, gestures or the media refer specially to him"When the newsreader touched her ear, it was a signal to me."
GrandiosityHe has exceptional powers, wealth, identity or mission"I can end all poverty just by signing my name."
Control (passivity)His will, thoughts or acts are controlled from outside"A machine in the temple tower moves my hands."
Guilt or sinHe has committed a terrible wrong and deserves punishment"The floods are my fault. I must be punished."
PovertyHe or his family is ruined"We have nothing. We will be on the street." (savings intact)
Nihilism (Cotard)He, his body or the world does not exist or is dead"My intestines have rotted. I am already dead."
Somatic; infestation (Ekbom)His body is diseased or changed; infested by parasites"Insects are breeding under my skin."
Jealousy (Othello)His partner is unfaithful"She meets a man at the market. I checked the scooter's mileage."
Erotomania (de Clérambault)A person, usually of higher status, loves him"The collector madam loves me; she signals through jeep number plates."
Misidentification (Capgras)A familiar person has been replaced by an identical impostor"She looks exactly like my mother, but she is not my mother."
Misidentification (Fregoli)A persecutor disguises himself as different strangers"The conductor, the waiter, the nurse: all are the same man."

A few of these need more than a line. Persecutory delusions, the belief that one is being deliberately harmed, harassed, interfered with, plotted against or mistreated, are the most common of all. A delusion of reference is the belief that ordinary events, remarks or objects carry a special personal meaning, and it must be distinguished from ideas of reference, the same kind of thought held without delusional conviction: the self-conscious student who feels that people laughing in the canteen might be laughing at him, but accepts that they probably are not. Ideas of reference occur in social anxiety, in paranoid and schizotypal personality and in ordinary embarrassment; delusions of reference occur in psychosis. A grandiose delusion is a belief in one's exceptional powers, wealth, identity or mission, and a delusion of control is the belief that one's will or actions are directed by an outside force (it overlaps with the passivity experiences described below).

The eponyms are favourites with examiners. A nihilistic delusion, in its fullest form Cotard syndrome, was described by the French neurologist Jules Cotard in 1880: the patient believes that he is dead, that his organs have rotted or that he or the world does not exist; it occurs in very severe depression, often in older people. Delusional infestation, the belief that one's skin or body is infested with insects or parasites, is called Ekbom syndrome after the Swedish neurologist Karl-Axel Ekbom, who described it in 1938 (do not confuse it with restless legs syndrome, which also carries Ekbom's name). Delusional jealousy, the belief that one's partner is unfaithful, is called Othello syndrome after Shakespeare's tragic hero; it is dangerous, because the patient searches, interrogates and may attack the partner. Erotomania, the belief that another person, usually of higher social status and often a stranger or a celebrity, is secretly in love with the patient, is named de Clérambault syndrome after the French psychiatrist Gaëtan Gatian de Clérambault. In Capgras syndrome, described by Joseph Capgras and Jean Reboul-Lachaux in 1923, a familiar person (usually a spouse or parent) is believed to have been replaced by an identical impostor; in Fregoli syndrome, described by Paul Courbon and Gabriel Fail in 1927 and named after an Italian actor famous for his quick changes of costume, strangers are believed to be one familiar person, usually a persecutor, in disguise. Capgras says the familiar is a stranger; Fregoli says the strangers are a familiar.

How does a clinician record a delusion? Never by arguing with it, which only hardens it and destroys rapport. He asks for the belief in the patient's words, then for the evidence ("How did you come to know this?"), tests the conviction ("Is there any chance you could be mistaken?"), and asks what the patient has done or plans to do about it, because a man convinced that his neighbour is poisoning him may decide to act first. The record reads something like: "Delusion of persecution: believes that his neighbours are poisoning the family well to take his land; first noticed 'a bitter taste' two months ago; held with full conviction; not shaken by the negative water test; has stopped drinking water at home and has threatened the neighbour."

Indian context

Belief in black magic, the evil eye (drishti or nazar), spirit possession and the effects of planets is widespread in India, and DSM-5-TR itself warns that such beliefs "may be viewed as bizarre and possibly delusional in some cultural contexts but be generally accepted in others." A belief is not a delusion simply because the clinician does not share it. Ask whether the family and community hold the same belief and hold it in the same way. A woman who thinks a jealous relative has cast the evil eye on her child, ties a black thread and visits the temple, is within her culture. A woman who is certain that her neighbour sends spirits through the television every night to stop her heart, and who is more convinced than anyone around her and cannot be reassured by the priest, has gone beyond it.

Exam trap

Being "deliberately interfered with, discriminated against, plotted against, threatened or otherwise mistreated" is a delusion of persecution, not reference (special personal meaning in events), control (outside force directing one's actions) or grandiosity. Othello syndrome is delusional jealousy (infidelity); Ekbom is infestation; de Clérambault is erotomania; Couvade syndrome is not a delusion at all, but pregnancy-like symptoms (nausea, weight gain, abdominal pain) in an expectant father. Capgras: a familiar person replaced by an impostor; Fregoli: strangers are a familiar person in disguise; Cotard: one is dead or does not exist. Ideas of reference lack delusional conviction; delusions of reference do not. Delusional perception is a first-rank symptom; delusional mood is not.

Disorders of content II: overvalued ideas, obsessions, phobias and preoccupations

Not every abnormal belief is a delusion. An overvalued idea, a term introduced by the German neuropsychiatrist Carl Wernicke (1848 to 1905), is a solitary, understandable belief that the person holds with intense emotional investment and that comes to dominate his life and actions far beyond what its truth or importance would warrant. It is not as fixed as a delusion, it is comprehensible given the person's background and personality, and he identifies with it and defends it rather than experiencing it as alien. The young woman with anorexia nervosa who says, "I know I am not fat by other people's standards, but if I go above 38 kilos I will be disgusting, and nothing matters more than that", is describing an overvalued idea. So are the conviction of a man with body dysmorphic concerns that his nose is grotesque, many cases of morbid jealousy that stop short of delusion, and the consuming sense of injustice that drives some people from court to court for decades.

An obsession (from the Latin for "to besiege") is a recurrent, persistent thought, image, impulse or urge that intrudes into the mind against the person's will, that he recognises as the product of his own mind, that he usually finds senseless, excessive or repugnant, and that he tries to resist or neutralise. Obsessions are ego-dystonic: experienced as alien to the person's values and wishes, unlike the overvalued idea, which is ego-syntonic, in keeping with the person's sense of himself. They take several forms: obsessional thoughts, doubts ("Did I lock the door?"), images, impulses, fears and endless ruminations on unanswerable questions. A young mother says:

"When I hold a knife in the kitchen, the thought comes that I might stab my baby. I would never do it. I know it is my own mind, it is my own stupid thought, but it comes again and again and I cannot push it away. Now I don't go into the kitchen."

The obsession is often followed by a compulsion, a repetitive behaviour or mental act (washing, checking, counting, praying a set number of times) that the person feels driven to perform to reduce the anxiety or to prevent a feared event; compulsions are observed as behaviour and recorded in Section 2.4 as well. Notice how firmly the mother insists that the thought is her own. That is exactly what separates an obsession from thought insertion (next subsection), in which the patient insists that the thought is not his own.

A phobia is a persistent, excessive and irrational fear of a specific object, activity or situation, out of proportion to any real danger, that leads to avoidance; adults usually recognise that the fear is excessive. "I know the lift is perfectly safe, but I would rather climb eleven floors than get into it." It is recorded under thought content because what the patient fears and avoids is part of what occupies his mind; the phobic disorders are in the Psychiatric Disorders book and their treatment in Chapter 4.

The rest of the content of thought is recorded as preoccupations: the themes the patient's thinking keeps returning to. In depression they are the depressive cognitions of worthlessness, hopelessness, helplessness and guilt (the negative cognitive triad of Chapter 5); excessive or inappropriate guilt is one of the symptoms of a depressive episode, and when it is out of all proportion and unshakeable it becomes a delusion of guilt. Other preoccupations concern illness (hypochondriacal ideas), the precipitating event, money, religion or philosophy. One more term belongs here. Magical thinking is the belief that one's thoughts, words or actions can cause or prevent events in ways that defy ordinary cause and effect: "If I tap the door four times before I leave, my father will not have an accident." It is normal in young children, common in obsessive-compulsive disorder, and one of the criteria of schizotypal personality disorder, where it appears alongside ideas of reference. Practices that a whole culture shares, such as wearing a lucky thread before an examination, are not counted as magical thinking in the clinical sense.

Finally, every MSE records suicidal and homicidal ideation under thought content, whether or not the patient seems depressed. The clinician asks, step by step as Section 2.2 described, about passive death wishes ("I wish I would not wake up"), active thoughts of ending one's life, plans, intent, preparations and past attempts, and about any thoughts of harming others, and records the answers in the patient's words. Ravi said, "Sometimes I think everyone would be better off without me. I have not thought how. I would not do that to my mother." What the clinician does with such answers, the weighing of risk and protective factors, is the subject of Section 2.7.

Table 2.11 Telling apart a delusion, an overvalued idea and an obsession.
FeatureDelusionOvervalued ideaObsession
Recognised as own thought?Yes (but held as fact)YesYes
Held with conviction?Full, unshakeableStrong, short of delusionalNo: seen as senseless or excessive
Resisted?NoNo; defendedYes
Ego-syntonic or dystonic?SyntonicSyntonicDystonic
Understandable from background?No (primary) or partly (secondary)YesContent often repugnant to him
Typical example"My neighbours poison the well""Above 38 kilos I will be disgusting""I might stab my baby"
Exam trap

An obsession is recognised as one's own thought and resisted; thought insertion is experienced as someone else's thought put into the mind. An overvalued idea (Wernicke) is understandable and defended but not as fixed as a delusion. Excessive guilt is thought content; when it becomes fixed and out of all proportion, it is a delusion of guilt. Magical thinking and ideas of reference are features of schizotypal personality disorder (and magical thinking of obsessive-compulsive disorder), not of post-traumatic stress disorder. Suicidal and homicidal ideas are recorded under thought content in every MSE.

Disorders of possession: thought alienation, passivity and the first-rank symptoms

You do not normally wonder whether your thoughts are your own. The sense that thoughts belong to the person thinking them, and are under his control, is so basic that it is noticed only when it fails. In thought alienation the patient experiences his thoughts as under the control of an outside agency, or feels that others are taking part in his thinking. It has three classic forms.

  • In thought insertion the patient experiences thoughts that are not his own being put into his mind from outside: "These are not my thoughts. They are the thoughts of the man next door. He puts them into my head with a ray through the window."
  • In thought withdrawal he experiences his thoughts being taken out of his mind by an outside agency, and the mind is left empty: "My thoughts are sucked out by the satellite. That is why my mind suddenly goes blank when I talk to you."
  • In thought broadcasting (thought diffusion) he experiences his thoughts as escaping from his mind so that other people know them, or as being broadcast to everyone: "Everyone on the bus knows what I am thinking. My thoughts go out of my head like a radio."

Closely related are the passivity phenomena (made experiences), in which the patient experiences his feelings, impulses, actions or bodily sensations as imposed or controlled by an outside agency. In made feelings an emotion is experienced as not his own: "I am not angry. The anger is put into me by the computer at the police station." In made impulses the drive to act comes from outside, though he carries out the act. In made volitional acts the action itself is controlled: "My hand picks up the glass, but it is the swami who moves it. I just watch." And in somatic passivity he is the passive recipient of bodily sensations imposed from outside: "They are sending electric currents into my stomach from the tower."

Two further points about this heading. First, British texts, Fish's among them, also list obsessions under disorders of possession, because the obsessional patient feels that his thinking has escaped his control, even though, unlike the patient with thought insertion, he knows the thought is his own; American texts put obsessions under content. Second, thought echo (hearing one's own thoughts spoken aloud) sounds as if it belongs here, but it is a perception, recorded under perception (next subsection).

These experiences have a special place in the history of psychiatry because of Kurt Schneider (1887 to 1967), a German psychiatrist at Heidelberg. Schneider was looking for symptoms that could be identified reliably in an interview and that would point to schizophrenia. He picked out a group which, when present without any organic brain disease, he regarded as strongly suggestive of schizophrenia, and called them first-rank symptoms; the list is best known in English from the translation of his Clinical Psychopathology (1959). As usually taught, it has eleven items, set out in Table 2.12. (Some books split or merge them slightly differently.)

Table 2.12 Schneider's first-rank symptoms, grouped by MSE heading.
MSE headingFirst-rank symptom
Perception: auditory hallucinations1. Audible thoughts (thought echo); 2. voices arguing or discussing the patient in the third person; 3. voices giving a running commentary on his actions
Possession: thought alienation4. Thought insertion; 5. thought withdrawal; 6. thought broadcasting
Possession: passivity7. Made feelings; 8. made impulses; 9. made volitional acts; 10. somatic passivity
Content: primary delusion11. Delusional perception

The first-rank symptoms were enormously influential, partly because they are easy to rate reliably, and they shaped the ICD-10 criteria for schizophrenia. Later research, however, showed that they are neither necessary nor specific: many people with schizophrenia never have them, and they also occur in mania and in other psychoses, and DSM-5-TR notes that experiences closely resembling them are reported in dissociative identity disorder. DSM-IV still gave them special weight (one symptom was enough for criterion A if the voices kept up a running commentary or conversed with each other, or if the delusions were bizarre); DSM-5 (2013) removed that special weight. They remain essential knowledge for the MSE, because they tell the clinician what to ask about and how to name what he hears.

Exam trap

The first-rank symptoms are thought insertion, withdrawal and broadcasting, made feelings, impulses and acts, somatic passivity, delusional perception and three kinds of auditory hallucination (audible thoughts, voices arguing or discussing, running commentary). They are not: avolition and flat affect (negative symptoms), memory deficits and disorientation (cognitive impairments, which point to organic disease and which Schneider's rule actually excluded), catatonic excitement (a motor sign), depersonalisation (end of this section), second-person voices, or delusional mood. Thought broadcasting (others know my thoughts) is not thought echo (I hear my thoughts aloud) and not ideas of reference (others are talking about me).

Disorders of perception

Perception is the process of becoming aware of what is presented through the senses. Its disturbances fall into two families. In a sensory distortion a real object is perceived, but perceived as changed: in intensity (sounds unbearably loud, hyperacusis; the world dulled and colourless in depression), in quality (everything tinged yellow, xanthopsia, as in digoxin toxicity; or red, erythropsia) or in size and shape (objects appearing smaller, micropsia, or larger, macropsia, as in temporal lobe epilepsy, migraine and some drug states). In a sensory deception something new is perceived, which may or may not be triggered by a real stimulus: illusions and hallucinations. Figure 2.5 lays out the family tree.

An illusion is a misperception of a real external stimulus. The French psychiatrist Jean-Étienne Dominique Esquirol (1772 to 1840) was the first to separate it clearly, in the early nineteenth century, from a hallucination, which has no external object at all. Descriptive psychopathology recognises three kinds. Completion illusions depend on inattention: you read a misprinted word as the correct one, or hear your name in a hubbub. Affect illusions arise from a strong emotion: in a dark, frightening ward, a patient says, "The drip stand turned into a man standing over my bed"; on a village path at night, a rope becomes a snake. Pareidolic illusions are vivid images that the mind makes out of vague stimuli, such as faces in clouds, animals in the cracks of a wall or Ganesha in the shape of a coconut; they persist even when the person attends closely, and they are entirely normal. Illusions are common when perception is degraded (darkness, fatigue, fear) and increase greatly in delirium.

A hallucination is a perception-like experience that occurs without an external stimulus. DSM-5-TR describes hallucinations as "vivid and clear, with the full force and impact of normal perceptions, and not under voluntary control". Three features separate them from imagination: they are experienced as coming from outside, in external objective space; they have the substantial, real quality of normal perceptions; and the person cannot summon or dismiss them at will. Jaspers added that they occur alongside real perceptions, not instead of them: the patient hears the voice and the clinician at the same time.

Hallucinations are classified first by sensory modality.

  • Auditory hallucinations are the commonest in schizophrenia and related disorders. They may be elementary (bangs, whistles, music) or complex (voices). Voices are described by person. Second-person voices speak to the patient ("You are useless. You should die."), and are common in severe depression, where their content is mood-congruent. Third-person voices speak about the patient ("He is a fool. Look, now he is getting up."), and when they argue or discuss him, or keep up a running commentary on what he is doing, they are first-rank symptoms. In audible thoughts (thought echo) the patient hears his own thoughts spoken aloud, as he thinks them or just after. A command hallucination is a voice that tells the patient to do something, and the clinician must always ask what it commands and whether he obeys, because commands to harm oneself or others raise risk (Section 2.7).
  • Visual hallucinations may be elementary (flashes of light) or complex (people, animals, scenes). They are less common in schizophrenia than auditory ones and should always raise the possibility of an organic cause: delirium (including alcohol withdrawal delirium, with its frightening animals and insects and its tiny Lilliputian people), drug intoxication, epilepsy, some dementias and eye disease.
  • Olfactory and gustatory hallucinations (smells and tastes, often together: burning rubber, rotting flesh, a metallic taste) suggest temporal lobe epilepsy, but they also occur in severe depression ("I can smell my body rotting") and in schizophrenia, where the "taste of poison" in food may feed a persecutory delusion.
  • Tactile (haptic) hallucinations are false sensations of touch on or just under the skin. Formication, the sensation of insects crawling on or under the skin, is typical of cocaine use and alcohol withdrawal.
  • Somatic (visceral or coenaesthetic) hallucinations are false sensations inside the body ("My liver is being twisted"), and kinaesthetic hallucinations are false sensations of movement ("My bed is rising off the floor"). When the patient attributes the bodily sensation to an outside agency, it is somatic passivity, a first-rank symptom.

Several special varieties are named for the circumstances in which they occur, and they are collected in Table 2.13. A functional hallucination is triggered by a real stimulus in the same modality and is perceived together with it: a woman hears voices only when the ceiling fan is running, and hears them inside its whirr. A reflex hallucination is triggered by a stimulus in a different modality: a man feels a stab of pain in his head every time a door slams. An extracampine hallucination is experienced outside the limits of the sensory field: seeing someone standing behind one's back, or hearing voices from Delhi while sitting in Mysuru. In autoscopy the person sees himself, as a double. Hypnagogic hallucinations occur while falling asleep and hypnopompic hallucinations while waking up: hearing one's name called, seeing a figure at the foot of the bed. They are common in healthy people, and DSM-5-TR treats them as within the range of normal experience; they are also frequent in narcolepsy. Finally, the Swiss naturalist Charles Bonnet (1720 to 1793) described in 1760 the vivid visual hallucinations (people, patterns, buildings) of his grandfather, who was going blind from cataracts and knew perfectly well that the visions were not real. Charles Bonnet syndrome, complex visual hallucinations with preserved insight in a person with visual loss, is common in older people with eye disease, and recognising it spares them an unnecessary diagnosis of psychosis.

Table 2.13 Special varieties of hallucination.
VarietyDefining featureThe patient says
FunctionalTriggered by a real stimulus in the same modality, heard with it"The voices come only in the noise of the fan."
ReflexTriggered by a stimulus in another modality"When the door slams, I feel a pain in my head."
ExtracampineOutside the limits of the sensory field"I can see a man standing behind me."
HypnagogicWhile falling asleep (normal)"As I dozed off, I heard my name called."
HypnopompicWhile waking up (normal)"I woke and saw my grandmother at the door."
CommandInstructs the patient to act"The voice tells me to jump from the terrace."
PseudohallucinationInner space, or recognised as unreal"I hear his voice inside my head, not with my ears."

That leaves the most confusing term in the subject. A pseudohallucination is, roughly, a perception-like experience that falls short of a true hallucination, and the confusion comes from the fact that the term is used in two different senses. In the European tradition of the Russian psychiatrist Victor Kandinsky (1849 to 1889), who described pseudohallucinations in the 1880s partly from his own psychotic illness (the word itself is usually credited to the German psychiatrist Friedrich Wilhelm Hagen, in 1868), and of Jaspers, a pseudohallucination is experienced in inner, subjective space ("in my head", "in my mind's eye") rather than in the outside world; it is vivid and involuntary like a perception but lacks the substantial quality of one. A widow who hears her husband's voice calling her, inside her head, in the weeks after his death is having a pseudohallucination in this sense. In the other, more British and American sense, a pseudohallucination is an experience located in outer space but recognised by the person as unreal, so that insight is preserved. In either sense pseudohallucinations are common in grief, trauma, severe stress and personality disorders as well as in psychosis, and the clinician should say which sense he means.

Asking about hallucinations takes tact, because many patients fear being called mad. Barlow and Durand suggest a normalising introduction: "Let me ask you a couple of routine questions that we ask everybody. Do you ever see things or maybe hear things when you know there is nothing there?" If the answer is yes, the clinician records the modality, the content in the patient's words, whether a voice speaks to him or about him, where it seems to come from, how clear, how frequent and at what times (only when falling asleep?), what the patient does in response, and whether he believes it is real.

Exam trap

An illusion misperceives a real stimulus; a hallucination has no stimulus; a delusional perception perceives a real stimulus accurately but gives it a delusional meaning (so it is a disorder of content, not of perception). A functional hallucination is triggered in the same modality and heard alongside the trigger; a reflex hallucination crosses modalities. Hypnagogic is falling asleep, hypnopompic is waking (remember "pomp" as the fanfare at the start of the day); both are normal. Formication is tactile (cocaine, alcohol withdrawal). Lilliputian hallucinations (tiny people) point to delirium. Visual hallucinations suggest an organic cause until proved otherwise.

Depersonalisation and derealisation

The last items under perception describe a change not in what is perceived but in how real it feels. In depersonalisation the person feels unreal, detached, or like an outside observer of his own thoughts, feelings, body or actions; he feels that he is no longer his normal, natural self. In derealisation it is the surroundings that feel unreal: dreamlike, foggy, lifeless, flat, like a stage set or a painting. The word depersonalisation was brought into use in 1898 by the French philosopher Ludovic Dugas. Patients struggle to describe these experiences and nearly always use the words "as if":

"I feel as if I am not really here, as if I am watching myself in a film. I know I am me, but it does not feel like me." (depersonalisation)

"Mysuru looks like a painting, as if there is a glass wall between me and everything." (derealisation)

The "as if" is the key to recording them. Throughout the experience the person knows that he is real and that the world is real; DSM-5-TR's phrase is that "reality testing remains intact". That separates depersonalisation from the nihilistic delusion of Cotard syndrome, in which the patient believes, without any "as if", that he is actually dead. Depersonalisation and derealisation are common and often brief in healthy people who are exhausted, sleep-deprived or frightened; they are frequent during panic attacks, in depression and anxiety, after trauma, in temporal lobe epilepsy and with cannabis and other drugs; and when they are persistent and distressing in their own right, they form depersonalisation-derealisation disorder (Psychiatric Disorders book). Two neighbouring experiences are sometimes recorded here as well: déjà vu, the feeling that a new situation has been experienced before, and jamais vu, the feeling that a familiar situation has never been experienced; both are disturbances of recognition memory, normal when occasional and frequent in temporal lobe epilepsy.

Exam trap

A change in awareness of one's own identity, the feeling of no longer being one's normal, natural self, is depersonalisation; the feeling that the outside world is unreal is derealisation. Neither is stress or depression, although both can accompany them. In both, reality testing is intact (the "as if" quality), which separates them from a delusion. And depersonalisation is not one of Schneider's first-rank symptoms, which makes it the favourite "all except" option.

The second half of Ravi's MSE

Here is how the thought and perception headings of Ravi's MSE were written. Notice that the most important findings are negative ones, carefully asked about and recorded.

Ravi's MSE (thought and perception)

Thought. Stream: retarded; replies slow and brief; no thought block. Form: no formal thought disorder; relevant, coherent and goal-directed. Content: preoccupied with his examination failure and with being a burden: "I am useless. They spent my mother's gold on me and I gave them nothing back." Feelings of worthlessness, guilt and hopelessness ("Nothing will change"); the guilt is related to real events and he accepts, with some reluctance, that his parents do not blame him, so it is not held with delusional conviction. Passive death wishes ("Sometimes I think everyone would be better off without me"); denies any plan or intent, cites his mother as a reason to live. No homicidal ideas. No delusions, overvalued ideas, obsessions or phobias. Possession: no thought alienation or passivity experiences. Perception. No hallucinations in any modality; no illusions; occasional feeling that "things seem far away" in the early mornings, recognised as "just my tiredness" (mild derealisation, insight preserved).

Two headings remain: whether Ravi's cognition is intact (his complaint that "nothing goes into my head" makes it essential to test), and whether he understands that he is ill. Those are the subject of Section 2.6, which also shows how the whole examination is written up, and his passive death wishes are weighed in the risk assessment of Section 2.7.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The descriptive method of understanding the patient's subjective experience from the inside was set out by Karl Jaspers in General Psychopathology in .
  2. Holding a posture that the examiner has placed the patient in is ; feeling a slight, even resistance as the examiner moves the limb is flexibility.
  3. The sustained emotional state the patient reports is his ; the moment-to-moment emotion the clinician observes is his .
  4. In order of increasing severity, reduced emotional expression is described as restricted, and flat.
  5. Rapid, abrupt shifts of emotion out of proportion to events are called affect.
  6. Markedly diminished interest or pleasure in nearly all activities is , a term introduced by Ribot in 1896.

  9. Experiencing one's thoughts as being put into the mind by an outside agency is thought , one of Schneider's first-rank symptoms.

Answers: (1) 1913, (2) catalepsy; waxy, (3) mood; affect, (4) blunted, (5) labile, (6) anhedonia, (7) circumstantiality; tangentiality, (8) Capgras, (9) insertion, (10) functional.

2.6 The Mental Status Examination III: Cognition, Judgement, Insight and the Written MSELower yield · 2 Qs

Sections 2.4 and 2.5 examined how a person looks, moves, speaks, feels, thinks and perceives. Almost all of that came from watching and listening. The last part of the mental status examination is different in kind: the clinician now asks the client to do things. Tell me today's date. Repeat these numbers after me. Take seven away from a hundred. Remember these three words; I will ask you for them again in a few minutes. What do people mean when they say this proverb? For the first time in the interview the client is being tested, and many clients notice the change. Some feel insulted ("Do you think I am mad?"), some become anxious, and a few, who have been chatting fluently for half an hour, suddenly cannot say what month it is. That last group is the reason the cognitive examination exists.

Why test at all, if the client has been talking sensibly? Because conversation hides a great deal. A sociable retired schoolteacher in the early stage of dementia can talk about cricket for an hour on well-worn phrases and still not know the year. A man recovering from a head injury, or an older woman in a delirium caused by a urinary infection, may answer social questions politely while being unable to hold a thought for ten seconds. The cognitive examination is the clinician's quick check that the machinery behind the conversation, alertness, attention, memory and reasoning, is working. When it is not, the whole meaning of the rest of the MSE changes: hallucinations in a clouded, disoriented patient point towards a medical cause, not a primary psychosis.

Why cognition is examined in a fixed order

The cognitive functions are traditionally examined, and written up, in a fixed sequence: consciousness, orientation, attention and concentration, memory, intelligence and general information, and abstract thinking, with judgement and insight closing the examination. The order is not a matter of habit. Each function depends on the ones before it. A person who is drowsy cannot attend; a person who cannot attend cannot register new information, and so cannot later recall it, and will therefore appear disoriented, because knowing today's date depends on having registered it this morning; and a person who cannot hold a question in mind cannot be expected to interpret a proverb. Figure 2.6 shows this hierarchy. The practical rule that follows from it is simple and examinable: test from the bottom up, and interpret every higher failure in the light of the lower ones. A poor score on the recall of three words means "memory impairment" only if the client was alert and attentive when the words were given.

Two more principles govern the bedside examination. The first is preparation. Clients do better, and feel less humiliated, if the clinician introduces the tests honestly: "I am going to ask some questions that we ask everyone, to check memory and concentration. Some are easy and some are quite hard, so don't worry if you can't do them all." The second is calibration.
