Every result must be read against the person's education, occupation, language and culture, which is why Section 2.3 insisted that education be recorded in the identifying data. Failing to subtract sevens means one thing in a chartered accountant and something quite different in a woman who left school in the second standard to look after her siblings.

Finally, remember what these tests are. They are bedside tests: quick, free, done with nothing but a pencil and a watch, and crude. They screen; they do not measure. When the bedside examination suggests a problem, the clinician refers the client for formal neuropsychological assessment, whose instruments (the Wechsler scales, the PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction, the NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery and many others) are taught in the Assessment book. Older case notes, and many Indian case records, group consciousness and orientation (sometimes with attention and memory) under the single heading sensorium, and write "sensorium clear" when they are intact.

Exam trap

The cognitive part of the MSE begins with consciousness, not with orientation or memory, because every later test assumes an alert patient. A patient who fails a recall test while drowsy or inattentive has not shown a memory deficit; he has shown that the lower levels failed. And do not confuse the bedside cognitive examination, which is part of the MSE, with formal neuropsychological testing, which is a separate assessment done with standardised tests.

Consciousness and its levels

In the MSE, consciousness means the level of alertness or wakefulness: the degree to which the person is awake and aware of himself and his surroundings. (The deeper question of what consciousness is belongs to General Psychology.) Most clients in an outpatient clinic are fully alert, and the clinician simply writes "conscious and alert". The headings exist for the patients who are not, and they are found most often in emergency departments, medical wards and old-age homes, where a psychologist is asked to see someone who is "confused".

Impairment of consciousness is a continuum, and Table 2.14 marks the usual points along it. The mildest and the most easily missed is clouding of consciousness: the person is awake and may talk, but thinking is slowed and muddled, attention drifts, perception is inaccurate (shadows become figures, the drip stand becomes a man), and memory for the period is patchy. Clouding is the hallmark of delirium, an acute confusional state caused by a medical illness, a drug or its withdrawal, in which the level of consciousness typically fluctuates through the day and worsens in the evening and at night, a pattern nurses call "sundowning". (Delirium itself is taught in the Psychiatric Disorders book.) A clinician who sees a confused patient once, at ten in the morning, may find him lucid and miss the delirium entirely; the night nurse's report is part of the assessment. Beyond clouding lies drowsiness (somnolence, sometimes called lethargy), in which the person falls asleep unless stimulated but can be roused by voice; then stupor, in which only vigorous or painful stimulation produces a brief response; and finally coma, in which the person cannot be roused at all. Some texts insert obtundation, a reduced alertness with slowed responses to stimulation, between drowsiness and stupor. At the opposite end from clouding is hypervigilance, an abnormally heightened alertness seen in anxiety, stimulant intoxication, alcohol withdrawal and paranoid states.

Table 2.14 Levels of consciousness, from fully alert to coma.
LevelWhat the clinician seesResponse to stimulation
AlertAwake, attentive, responds normallyNormal
Clouding of consciousnessAwake but muddled; attention drifts; often fluctuatesResponds, but slowly and inaccurately
Drowsiness (somnolence)Falls asleep unless stimulatedRoused by voice, then drifts off
StuporLies unresponsiveRoused only briefly by vigorous or painful stimuli
ComaEyes closed, unrousableNo purposeful response, even to pain

In emergency and neurological settings the level of consciousness is rated on the Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS), published in 1974 by the Glasgow neurosurgeons Graham Teasdale and Bryan Jennett. The GCS adds the scores for three responses: eye opening (1 to 4), the best verbal response (1 to 5) and the best motor response (1 to 6). The total therefore runs from 3, a patient who shows no response of any kind, to 15, a fully alert and oriented one; a score of 8 or less is generally taken to mean coma. You will not be asked to use the GCS in a psychology clinic, but you will read it in every head-injury file you are sent.

One word in this list causes trouble. In neurology, stupor is a level of consciousness. In psychiatry the same word is also used for a state of mutism and akinesia (the person neither speaks nor moves, and may not eat or drink) in which consciousness is often fully preserved. This is seen in catatonic stupor, in severe depressive stupor and in dissociative stupor. Such patients may later recall everything that was said at the bedside, which is why nobody should ever discuss a stuporous patient carelessly in his presence. How do you tell the two kinds apart at the bedside? The eyes help: a patient in a psychiatric stupor often keeps his eyes open and follows movement, or resists attempts to open his eyelids, and his reflexes and pupils are normal.

Whatever the level, record it in plain words ("drowsy, rouses to his name, falls asleep mid-sentence"), and then read every later finding in its light. A drowsy patient's poor memory says nothing about dementia.

Exam trap

The lowest possible Glasgow Coma Scale score is 3, not 0, and the highest is 15 (eyes 4, verbal 5, motor 6); 8 or below is taken as coma. Clouding of consciousness is the mildest impairment and the core of delirium, and it fluctuates. Stupor has two meanings: a deep level of impaired consciousness in neurology, and mutism with immobility, often with clear consciousness, in catatonic, depressive and dissociative states. If an option says a stuporous patient "cannot be aware of his surroundings", be suspicious.

Orientation to time, place and person

In the MSE, orientation is the person's awareness of himself in relation to time, place and other people: knowing when it is, where he is and who he and the people around him are. Its absence, disorientation, is one of the most reliable signs that something is wrong with the brain rather than with the mind alone, because orientation is preserved in most functional psychiatric disorders, even severe ones. A man with florid schizophrenia who believes that the television is broadcasting his thoughts will usually still tell you, correctly, that it is a Tuesday morning in the outpatient department of the district hospital.

Each sphere is tested with simple questions. For time, the clinician asks the day of the week, the date, the month, the year and the approximate time of day ("Is it morning, afternoon or evening?"), and, for an inpatient, how long he has been in hospital. For place, he asks where the patient is now, what kind of place it is ("What sort of building is this?") and which town or city it is in. For person, he asks the patient to identify the people around him (his relative, the nurse, "Who do you think I am?") and, last, to state his own name and age. The questions are only half the test. The other half is judging whether an error matters. Patients in hospital, where every day is the same, often get the date wrong by a day or two, and this is not disorientation. Many rural Indians, farmers and older women in particular, do not follow the calendar of months and dates at all but mark time by the agricultural season, the lunar calendar and the festivals; for them the right questions are "Which season is it?", "Which festival went by most recently?" and "Is it morning or evening?" A clinician who scores such a person "disoriented to time" because she cannot name the date has tested her schooling, not her brain.

Orientation is lost in a characteristic order: time first, then place, then person, and the patient's own identity last of all. Why this order? Because the spheres differ in how much continuous new learning they demand. The date changes every day and must be updated constantly, so it is the first thing to go when attention and recent memory fail; one's location changes less often; and the identity of familiar people, and of oneself, is overlearned over a lifetime and is the most resistant. As a delirium clears or a patient emerges from the confusion that follows a head injury, orientation usually returns in the reverse order, person first and time last. Figure 2.7 sets this out.

The order gives the clinician a diagnostic clue. Disorientation that follows it, time before place before person, is the pattern of organic conditions: delirium, dementia, intoxication and head injury. A patient who has forgotten who he is while knowing perfectly well the date and the name of the hospital has broken the order, and that pattern suggests a dissociative condition such as dissociative amnesia or fugue rather than brain disease (these disorders are taught in the Psychiatric Disorders book). A second curiosity is double orientation, seen in some patients with chronic schizophrenia: the patient states correctly that he is in the psychiatric ward of the district hospital and, in the same breath, that the ward is a palace in which he is being crowned. The real and the delusional orientation run side by side, something the Swiss psychiatrist Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939) called "double bookkeeping". Finally, apparent disorientation in a severely depressed or acutely psychotic patient is often not disorientation at all but a failure of attention or cooperation: he did not answer because he did not attend, or did not care to.

The written entry is brief: "Oriented to time, place and person" (many clinicians write "oriented in all three spheres"), or, when there is a deficit, the exact errors: "Gave the year as 2019 and said the hospital was his office; recognised his wife."

Exam trap

Orientation is lost time, then place, then person, and recovered in the reverse order. Loss of personal identity with intact orientation for time and place points to a dissociative cause, not dementia. Orientation is usually preserved in functional disorders such as schizophrenia and depression, so marked disorientation should make you think of delirium or another organic condition. And orientation is a cognitive function in its own right: it is not insight, which is the patient's awareness of being ill (Section 2.6.9).

Attention and concentration

The words are often used together, but they name two different things. In clinical usage, attention is the ability to direct the mind to a stimulus or task, to turn towards it and take it in, while concentration is the ability to sustain that focus over time, against distraction and fatigue. Indian case records capture the difference in a standard phrase: "attention is easily aroused and sustained". A patient may show one problem without the other. A man in a manic episode turns instantly to every sound in the corridor (his attention is easily aroused, too easily) but cannot keep it on anything (it is not sustained); this is distractibility. A depressed woman may attend to a question but lose the thread halfway through a long task. Inattention, the inability to direct and hold attention at all, is the cardinal feature of delirium, and in DSM-5-TR a disturbance of attention and awareness is the first criterion for it.

Much of the evidence comes from the interview itself: whether the client follows questions, loses the thread, needs questions repeated or is pulled away by noises. The bedside tests then put a number on it.

  • The digit span test asks the client to repeat a string of digits. The clinician reads them at about one per second, in an even voice, without grouping them as one groups a telephone number, starting with three digits and adding one each time until the client fails twice at the same length. Then the client is asked to repeat new strings backwards. Most adults of average education repeat six or seven digits forwards and four or five backwards, and backwards is usually one or two less than forwards; a forward span below five is usually abnormal. Digits forward depends mainly on attention and the immediate registration of information; digits backward also requires holding and manipulating the digits, a working-memory task. This is why some textbooks list digit span under attention and others under immediate memory: it measures the point at which the two meet.
  • The test of serial sevens asks the client to take 7 away from 100 and keep taking 7 from each answer: 93, 86, 79, 72, 65. The clinician records the answers, the errors and the time taken. One slip followed by correct subtraction from the wrong number (93, 85, 78, 71) counts as a single error, because the test is of sustained concentration, not of arithmetic; even healthy people make an occasional slip, so the pattern matters more than a single mistake.
  • Because serial sevens depends on arithmetic, it is unfair to people with little schooling. The alternatives are serial threes (take 3 from 20, and keep going), counting backwards from 20 to 1, saying the days of the week backwards, saying the months of the year backwards, or spelling a five-letter word such as WORLD backwards. The clinician chooses the hardest task that the client's education makes fair; months backwards, for example, assumes that the client knows the months in order forwards.
  • A simple test of vigilance asks the client to tap the table every time he hears the letter A in a long string of letters read aloud; missed A's and taps on other letters are counted.

How a person fails is as informative as whether he fails. The depressed patient tends to do the tasks slowly, with effort and complaint ("I can't, my head doesn't work") but often accurately in the end. The manic patient starts quickly and then abandons the task for something more interesting. The anxious patient makes errors he would not make at home, and improves when reassured. The delirious patient cannot keep the instruction in mind long enough to begin. These patterns matter because poor performance on these tests is not specific to any one condition: it tells you that attention is impaired, not why.

Exam trap

Attention is aroused; concentration is sustained. Digit span tests attention and immediate memory (registration), not long-term memory and not intelligence. Serial sevens (100, 93, 86, 79, 72, 65) tests concentration; for a person with little schooling use serial threes, counting backwards, or days or months backwards. The ability to do serial subtraction is a test of concentration: it has nothing to do with insight, which is a distractor in questions about what "insight" means. Easy distractibility is the attentional sign of mania; inattention is the core of delirium.

Memory: immediate, recent and remote

Clinicians divide memory, for bedside purposes, by how long ago the information was learned. The divisions are practical rather than theoretical, and they do not map neatly onto the stores of cognitive psychology (General Psychology): what the clinician calls "recent memory" is mostly what a cognitive psychologist would call long-term memory for recent events.

  • The first division, immediate memory (immediate retention and recall, or registration), is the ability to take in information and reproduce it within seconds. It is tested with the digit span, or by asking the client to repeat three unrelated words straight away (for example, "mango, chair, blue") until he has them all. If he cannot register the words, there is no point asking for them later: the problem lies in attention, not in memory proper.
  • The second, recent memory, is memory for events of the last few minutes, hours and days. The standard test is delayed recall: after about five minutes filled with other tasks, the client is asked for the three words again, with a category cue ("one was a fruit") if he cannot recall them unaided, and then, if needed, a choice ("was it apple, mango or banana?"). Recognition with cues but not free recall suggests a retrieval problem; failure even with choices suggests that the words were never stored. Recent memory is also tested with questions about the last day or two: what he ate for breakfast, how he travelled to the clinic, who visited him yesterday. The answers must be checked against an informant or the ward staff, because a fluent answer can be invented. Some Indian formats add a separate heading, recent past memory, for events of the past few months (the last festival, a recent family wedding, news of the past weeks).
  • The third, remote memory, is memory for events of years ago: personal history (where he went to school, the year of his marriage, his children's names and ages) and public events that a person of his background would know. Again, answers must be verifiable: the clinician who asks the year of a man's marriage must know it from his wife.

The three are lost unequally. In dementia, in the amnesia that follows head injury, and in many other brain diseases, recent memories are lost before remote ones: the grandfather who cannot remember that his son visited this morning can describe in detail the day Indira Gandhi visited his village decades ago. This gradient was described by the French psychologist Théodule-Armand Ribot (1839 to 1916) in Les maladies de la mémoire (1881), and it is still called Ribot's law: the newest memories, least consolidated, are the most vulnerable, and the oldest, most rehearsed, the most resistant. (Families often take preserved remote memory as proof that "his memory is fine"; it is not.)

Two further phenomena belong to this heading. The first is confabulation: the filling of gaps in memory with false memories that the patient produces fluently and believes. Asked what he did yesterday, an inpatient with severe amnesia may describe a trip to the market and a meeting with friends that never happened. Confabulation is not lying, because there is no intent to deceive (Section 2.3 contrasted it with malingering), and it is classically seen in the amnesic syndrome described by the Russian psychiatrist Sergei Sergeievich Korsakoff (1854 to 1900) in 1887, which results from thiamine deficiency, most often in chronic heavy drinking (the Psychiatric Disorders book). The second is the difference between complaining of poor memory and having it. Depressed people often complain bitterly that "nothing stays in my head", yet perform reasonably when tested; people with early dementia often deny or minimise memory problems that their families describe and the tests confirm. When depression in an older adult produces a picture that looks like dementia, it is called pseudodementia, a term popularised by the psychiatrist Leslie Kiloh in a paper of 1961, although others had used it before him (today it is often called the dementia syndrome of depression).

Clinical link

Is this older person depressed or dementing? At the bedside, a few contrasts help. The depressed patient tends to have a fairly clear onset, often after a loss; complains of memory loss in detail; gives up quickly with "I don't know" answers; and has low mood that came first. The patient with dementia usually has a gradual onset that the family cannot date; minimises or is unaware of the problem; tries hard and gives near-misses or confabulated answers; and has deficits that are consistent across tests. None of these contrasts is decisive (depression and dementia often coexist, and late-life depression can be an early sign of dementia), so the answer comes from treatment, follow-up and formal testing. But the distinction matters, because the depression is treatable.

Exam trap

Immediate memory is registration within seconds (digit span, repeating three words at once); recent memory is minutes to days (three words after five minutes, yesterday's events); remote memory is years (personal and public past). By Ribot's law recent memories are lost before remote ones. Confabulation fills memory gaps without intent to deceive (Korsakoff syndrome); malingering is deliberate. Pseudodementia (Kiloh, 1961) is depression that looks like dementia, typically with "don't know" answers and complaints that exceed the measured deficit.

Intelligence and general information

The MSE does not measure intelligence; it makes a rough clinical estimate of whether the person's intellectual functioning is in keeping with his education and background, and whether it appears to have declined. The estimate rests on several sources. The first is the interview itself: vocabulary, the complexity of the person's sentences, how well he understands questions and how organised his account was. The second is general information, the person's fund of knowledge, tested with questions pitched at what someone of his background would know: the name of the Prime Minister and of the Chief Minister, the capital of the state, a recent event in the news, the winner of a recent cricket series, the price of a kilo of rice. The third is calculation, tested with simple, practical sums ("If you buy two kilos of tomatoes at 30 rupees a kilo and pay with a 100-rupee note, how much change will you get?"). The fourth is comprehension, the ability to understand a short story or a practical situation.

The interpretation, again, is everything. A farmer who left school at ten may not know the name of the state's capital and may still calculate the price of a cartload of sugarcane in his head faster than the clinician can on a phone. A failure matters when it falls clearly below what the person's education and occupation predict, because then it suggests either lifelong intellectual disability (when the history shows that he was always so) or deterioration (when the history shows that he used to do better). The written entry reads, for example, "intelligence appears average, consistent with educational and occupational background". When a numerical estimate is needed, the clinician refers for formal testing with an intelligence scale (the Wechsler scales, the Bhatia Battery, the Binet-Kamat test and others, taught in the Assessment book).

Abstract thinking: proverbs and similarities

In the MSE sense, abstract thinking is the ability to move beyond the particular and literal to the general: to grasp categories, concepts, metaphors and the principle behind a concrete example. Its loss, concrete thinking, is thinking tied to the literal, immediate and physical features of things. The German neurologist Kurt Goldstein (1878 to 1965), working with soldiers who had suffered brain injuries in the First World War, described the loss of the "abstract attitude" as a basic effect of brain damage, and with Martin Scheerer published a set of tests of abstract and concrete behaviour in 1941. The bedside examination uses two tasks.

Proverb interpretation asks the client what a familiar proverb means. A Kannada-speaking client might be asked about haasige iddashtu kaalu chaachu ("stretch your legs only as far as your bed goes"), a Hindi speaker about its twin jitni chadar ho, utne hi pair phailao, and an English speaker about "People who live in glass houses should not throw stones". An abstract answer captures the principle ("live within your means"; "don't criticise others for faults you have yourself"). A concrete answer stays with the objects ("if your legs go off the bed they will touch the cold floor"; "the glass will break"). Proverbs work only if they are familiar, so the clinician uses proverbs from the client's own language and asks first whether he has heard the proverb before. (A standardised version, the Gorham Proverbs Test of 1956, belongs to the Assessment book.)

Similarities ask what two things have in common: an orange and a banana, a table and a chair, a dog and a lion, a poem and a statue. Answers fall into three levels. The abstract answer names the category ("both are fruits"; "both are furniture"). The functional answer names a use or action ("you can eat both"; "you use both in a dining room"), a partly abstract level. The concrete answer names a physical feature ("both have skins"; "both have four legs"), or the client simply insists that they are different ("an orange is round and a banana is long"). Differences can be asked too: what is the difference between a lie and a mistake? (The abstract answer turns on intention.) Similarities are easier than proverbs and depend less on culture, so they are the better test for a client with little schooling.

Concrete thinking is found in organic brain disease (especially frontal lobe damage and dementia), in intellectual disability, in some patients with schizophrenia, and in young children, for whom it is normal (Jean Piaget's concrete operational stage is in the Developmental Psychology book). Psychiatric texts describe two further abnormalities. In over-abstraction the answer goes too far, generalising far beyond relevance ("an orange and a banana are both manifestations of the cosmic energy"), and in bizarre or idiosyncratic interpretation the answer reflects the patient's thought disorder or delusions ("glass houses means that the government is watching through my windows"); both are seen in schizophrenia and point back to the thought disorder of Section 2.5.

Table 2.15 gathers the bedside tests of this section in one place, with a rough guide to what a healthy adult of average education does.

Table 2.15 The bedside tests of the cognitive functions, in the order they are examined.
FunctionBedside testHealthy adult (rough guide)
ConsciousnessObservation; GCS if impairedAlert; GCS 15
OrientationTime, place and person questionsCorrect, apart from small slips of date
AttentionDigit span forwards and backwardsAbout 6 to 7 forwards, 4 to 5 backwards
ConcentrationSerial sevens; serial threes; days or months backwardsCompleted, with at most an occasional slip
Immediate memoryRepeat three words at onceAll three registered
Recent memoryThree words after five minutes; events of the last dayAll three; events verified
Remote memoryVerifiable personal and public pastAccurate
IntelligenceGeneral information, calculation, comprehensionIn keeping with education
Abstract thinkingProverbs; similarities and differencesAbstract or category answers
Exam trap

"An orange and a banana are both fruits" is abstract; "you can eat both" is functional; "both have skins" is concrete. Proverb interpretation is culture-bound: a concrete answer to an unfamiliar proverb, or from a person with little schooling, is not by itself a sign of illness, which is why similarities are the fairer test. Concrete thinking is normal in young children and abnormal in adults with organic brain disease, intellectual disability or schizophrenia; over-abstraction and bizarre interpretations are the signs that point to schizophrenic thought disorder.

Judgement: personal, social and test

In the MSE, judgement is the ability to assess a situation correctly, weigh the likely consequences of different courses of action and act appropriately. It is the cognitive function closest to everyday life, and the one whose impairment most often brings a patient to hospital: a man in a manic episode who resigns from a secure job to start five businesses at once, an older woman with dementia who gives her savings to a stranger at the door, a young man who drives home drunk. Clinicians assess it in three ways, summarised in Table 2.16.

Personal judgement concerns the person's own life: whether his plans, expectations and decisions about his health, work, money and relationships are realistic. It is assessed by asking about his plans ("What will you do when you leave the hospital?", "How will you manage the loan?") and by comparing what he says with what the history shows he has actually done. Social judgement concerns his conduct with other people: whether he behaves appropriately in the interview, on the ward and at home, and whether he can anticipate how others will react to him. It is assessed by observation (an overfamiliar remark to a stranger, undressing in a public place, shouting in a temple) and from the informants. Finally, test judgement asks how the person would act in a standard hypothetical situation: "What would you do if you found a sealed, stamped and addressed envelope lying on the road?" (post it), "What would you do if you saw smoke and fire in a crowded cinema hall?" (alert the staff and the people near you and leave calmly, rather than shout "fire" and start a stampede), "What would you do if you smelled gas when you entered your kitchen?" (turn off the cylinder, open the windows and not light anything).

Table 2.16 The three kinds of judgement assessed in the MSE.
KindQuestion it answersHow it is assessed
Personal judgementAre his plans and decisions about his own life realistic?Questions about plans; comparison with what the history shows he has done
Social judgementDoes he behave appropriately with other people?Observation in the interview and on the ward; informants' reports
Test judgementWhat would he do in a standard hypothetical situation?Problems such as the found envelope, the fire in the cinema, the smell of gas

The three are not equally informative, and this is the single most important point about judgement. Test judgement measures whether the person knows what a sensible person would do; it does not measure whether he does it. A man in the middle of a manic episode will usually tell you, correctly and with some impatience, that he would post the envelope, and then go home and spend the family's savings on a car. A patient with frontal lobe damage may give textbook answers to every hypothetical problem while his life falls apart through impulsive decisions. Personal and social judgement, drawn from the history and from behaviour, therefore carry much more weight than test judgement, and an entry that reads only "judgement intact" on the strength of the envelope question tells the reader almost nothing.

Judgement is impaired in mania, in acute psychosis (where decisions follow from delusions), in dementia, in frontal lobe disease, in intoxication and in intellectual disability. In depression it is more often distorted than lost: a hopeless person may decide, with what seems to him clear logic, to abandon his studies or resign from his job, and the clinician's advice to postpone major decisions until the depression lifts is a judgement made on his behalf.

Exam trap

The three kinds are personal (own plans and life decisions), social (conduct with others) and test (hypothetical situations such as the found envelope or the fire in a cinema). Intact test judgement does not mean intact judgement: a manic or frontal patient can answer the envelope question correctly and still behave recklessly, so personal and social judgement from the history and behaviour matter more. Do not confuse judgement (deciding and acting appropriately) with insight (awareness of one's own illness), although the two often fail together.

Insight and its six grades

The last heading of the MSE asks a question that sounds simple and is not: does the patient know that he is ill? In the MSE, insight is the patient's awareness that he has an illness, his understanding of its nature and causes, and his acceptance that he needs treatment. The classic definition was given in 1934 by Aubrey Lewis (1900 to 1975), the Australian-born psychiatrist who later led the Institute of Psychiatry in London: insight is "a correct attitude to a morbid change in oneself". Each word of the definition does work. "Correct" means that the patient's view matches reality; "morbid change" means that what must be recognised is a change from his usual self, the kind of change the case history was designed to document; and "in oneself" places the change inside the person, not in the neighbours, the government or the evil eye.

Insight is assessed by a short sequence of questions, asked without argument: "Do you think anything is wrong with you?" "What do you think it is?" "What has caused it?" "Do you think you need treatment? What kind?" "Will you take it?" The answers are then graded. The six grades most often taught, set out in Kaplan and Sadock's Synopsis of Psychiatry and in most Indian MSE formats, are shown in Table 2.17 with the kind of statement a patient at each grade might make.

Table 2.17 The six grades of insight, with typical statements.
GradeDefinitionA patient might say
1Complete denial of illness"Nothing is wrong with me. My family brought me here for nothing."
2Slight awareness of being sick and needing help, while denying it at the same time"Maybe I need a tonic for sleep, but I am not ill."
3Awareness of being sick, but blaming it on others, on external events or on physical factors"I am like this because my neighbours did black magic", or "It is all the gas in my stomach."
4Awareness that the illness is due to something unknown in oneself"Something has gone wrong inside my mind. I don't know what."
5Intellectual insight: admits the illness and that symptoms come from his own irrational feelings or disturbances, but does not apply this knowledge to future experience"The voices are part of my illness and the tablets help", yet he stops the tablets as soon as he feels better.
6True emotional insight: emotional awareness of his own motives and feelings and those of important people in his life, leading to basic changes in behaviour"When the old suspicion starts, I now tell my wife and see the doctor before it takes hold."

The step that students find hardest is the one from grade 5 to grade 6. What is called intellectual insight is knowledge: the patient can say, correctly, that he has an illness and that his symptoms are part of it, but the knowledge does not change what he does. By contrast, true emotional insight is knowledge that has been felt and used: it reaches his feelings, his understanding of himself and the people around him, and his behaviour. The distinction comes from the psychotherapeutic tradition (in psychodynamic therapy, Chapter 3, intellectual insight without emotional insight is a familiar form of resistance), and it explains a common clinical paradox: a patient who can lecture you on his diagnosis and still relapse every year because he stops treatment.

Modern research treats insight as a set of dimensions rather than a single ladder. In an influential paper of 1990, the British psychiatrist Anthony David proposed three: recognising that one has a mental illness, accepting the need for treatment (and adhering to it), and being able to relabel unusual mental events, such as hallucinations and delusions, as abnormal. A patient can have one without the others: a woman may take her tablets faithfully "because my husband insists" while denying that anything is wrong, and a man may accept that he is ill while insisting that his voices are real. Rating scales built on this approach, such as the Scale to Assess Unawareness of Mental Disorder of Xavier Amador and colleagues (1993), belong to the Assessment book.

Key study: lack of insight in the International Pilot Study of Schizophrenia (WHO, 1973)

In the late 1960s the World Health Organization ran the International Pilot Study of Schizophrenia, in which psychiatrists in nine centres (Aarhus, Agra, Cali, Ibadan, London, Moscow, Prague, Taipei and Washington) examined 1,202 patients with the Present State Examination that you met in Section 2.1. The researchers wanted to know which symptoms of schizophrenia were common to every culture. The answer surprised many people. The single most frequent finding was not hallucinations or delusions but lack of insight, present in about 97 percent of the patients diagnosed with schizophrenia.

The finding has two lessons for the MSE. Poor insight is not a rare complication of psychosis but nearly its rule, so the clinician must expect it and plan engagement around it. And because Agra was one of the nine centres, the result holds for Indian patients too.

Why does insight matter so much? Because it predicts behaviour. Poor insight is one of the strongest predictors of non-adherence to treatment, of relapse and of compulsory admission, and in risk assessment (Section 2.7) lack of insight is an item on the best-known violence risk scheme. Yet the relationship is not simple. In schizophrenia, patients who gain insight are, on average, more likely to become depressed and to think of suicide, because to see clearly that one has a serious, long-term illness is painful; this is sometimes called the insight paradox, and it is a reason to watch for depression as insight improves. At the neurological end, unawareness of a deficit can be a direct effect of brain damage: the French neurologist Joseph Babinski (1857 to 1932) coined the word anosognosia in 1914 for the failure of some patients with right-hemisphere strokes to recognise that the left side of the body is paralysed. Some researchers, Amador among them, argue that much of the poor insight of schizophrenia is closer to anosognosia than to psychological denial.

Indian context

Insight must be judged against the patient's culture. Many Indian families explain illness in terms of karma, planetary influences, the evil eye, spirit possession or sorcery (often called maata or banamathi), and Section 2.2 showed why the clinician should ask about this explanatory model rather than dismiss it. Holding a belief that is shared by one's family and community is not, by itself, poor insight. What the clinician grades is narrower: does the patient recognise that a change has taken place in himself, and will he accept help for it? A woman who says, "My mother-in-law thinks it is a spirit, and perhaps it is, but I know I have not been myself and the tablets help me sleep" has more insight than her words about spirits suggest. Many clients hold a medical and a spiritual explanation side by side, and use the doctor and the temple together.

Exam trap

In the MSE, insight is the patient's awareness and understanding of his own illness. It is not orientation to time, place and person, not the level of consciousness, not immediate retention and recall, not the ability to do serial subtraction and not the presence of hallucinations: those are all separate MSE headings, and they are the usual distractors. Know the six grades: 1 complete denial; 2 slight awareness while denying; 3 aware but blames external or physical factors; 4 aware that the cause is something unknown in himself; 5 intellectual insight; 6 true emotional insight, which changes behaviour. Do not confuse the MSE's insight with Wolfgang Köhler's "insight learning" in chimpanzees (General Psychology).

Bedside screening: the MMSE and its relatives

The bedside examination described so far is flexible: the clinician chooses the questions and judges the answers. For some purposes, following a patient's cognition over months, comparing patients across clinics or deciding quickly who needs a full assessment, a standard, scored version is more useful. The best known is the Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE), published in 1975 by Marshal F. Folstein, Susan Folstein and Paul McHugh under the title "'Mini-mental state': a practical method for grading the cognitive state of patients for the clinician". They wanted something a busy doctor could give in five to ten minutes, and that is what it remains. Table 2.18 shows how its 30 points are distributed; notice how many of the tasks are the ones you have just met.

Table 2.18 The structure of the Mini-Mental State Examination (Folstein, Folstein and McHugh, 1975).
DomainTaskPoints
Orientation to timeYear, season, date, day, month5
Orientation to placeState, county (district in India), town, hospital, floor5
RegistrationRepeat three named objects3
Attention and calculationSerial sevens (five subtractions), or WORLD spelled backwards5
RecallThe three objects, a few minutes later3
LanguageName two objects (2); repeat a phrase (1); three-stage command (3); read and obey "Close your eyes" (1); write a sentence (1)8
Visuospatial constructionCopy two intersecting pentagons1
Total30

A score of 23 or less is commonly taken to suggest cognitive impairment, with 24 to 30 regarded as normal, although clinicians adjust the cut-off for age and especially for education. That adjustment is the MMSE's great weakness in India. Several items (writing a sentence, reading a command, serial sevens) assume literacy and schooling, so an illiterate but cognitively healthy villager may score in the "impaired" range, while a retired professor in the early stages of dementia may still score 29. For this reason Mary Ganguli and her colleagues developed the Hindi Mental State Examination (HMSE, 1995) for a largely illiterate rural elderly population near Ballabgarh in Haryana, replacing the literacy-dependent items with tasks that do not require schooling. The MMSE is also insensitive to mild impairment and to problems of executive (frontal) function, and it says nothing about the cause of a low score. The Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA), published by Ziad Nasreddine and colleagues in 2005, was designed to fill that gap: it is also scored out of 30, but adds tasks of executive function (a short trail-making task, drawing a clock, copying a cube, verbal fluency), uses a harder memory test, takes a cut-off of 26 and is more sensitive to mild cognitive impairment.

Keep these instruments in proportion. They are screening tests: a low score says "look further", never "this is dementia". The diagnosis comes from the history, the full examination, investigations and formal neuropsychological assessment, and the tests themselves, their norms and their psychometric properties are taught in the Assessment book.

Exam trap

The MMSE (Folstein, Folstein and McHugh, 1975) is a 30-point cognitive screening test; the MSE is the clinician's whole description of the mental state. They are not the same thing, and the MMSE is not a diagnostic interview (the SCID is, Section 2.1). The usual cut-off is 23 or below; the MMSE is education-sensitive and insensitive to mild and frontal impairment. The MoCA (Nasreddine, 2005) is more sensitive to mild cognitive impairment, cut-off 26. The HMSE (Ganguli, 1995) is the Hindi adaptation for illiterate rural elderly people.

Writing the MSE: a model you can copy

An MSE that stays in the clinician's head is of no use to anyone. It has to be written so that a colleague who has never met the patient can picture him, compare him with how he will be next week, and see how the clinician reached the conclusions. A few rules make the difference between a useful MSE and a list of jargon.

  • Describe, then label. Write what you saw and heard, with the patient's own words in quotation marks, and add the technical term afterwards. "Persecutory delusion" alone is an opinion; "believes firmly that his neighbours have fitted a device in his phone to record his thoughts, and was not shaken when shown that the phone was ordinary (persecutory delusion)" is evidence.
  • Record the present, not the past. The MSE is the photograph, not the film. "Has not slept well for three months" belongs to the history; "looks tired and yawned repeatedly" belongs to the MSE.
  • Write every heading, including the normal ones, and the important negatives: "no suicidal ideation", "no hallucinations elicited". An absent heading leaves the reader unsure whether the function was normal or never examined.
  • Record the conditions: the date and time, the setting, the language used, who was present, how long the interview lasted and how cooperative the patient was, since all of these affect the findings.
  • Close with a short summary and a statement of risk. The summary pulls out the significant findings in three or four lines; the risk statement (Section 2.7) records what was asked about harm to self and others and what was found.

Table 2.19 shows the commonest faults beside better versions.

Table 2.19 Weak and better entries in a written MSE.
HeadingWeak entryBetter entry
Mood"Patient is depressed."Says "I feel useless all the time"; sad face, tearful twice when speaking of his father.
Thought content"Delusions present."Believes he has been chosen to run all transport in the city; unshaken by contrary evidence (grandiose delusion).
Memory"Memory normal."Registered three words; recalled 3 of 3 at five minutes; described breakfast and journey correctly (confirmed by wife).
Judgement"Judgement intact."Test judgement intact; personal judgement impaired (plans a large loan to buy vehicles he cannot pay for).
Insight"Insight poor.""Nothing is wrong with me, my brother is jealous"; refuses medicines (grade 1 insight).

Here, then, is a complete written MSE. The patient is a composite, Mr Manjunath, a 34-year-old auto-rickshaw driver from Mysuru, brought by his wife and his younger brother after two weeks of sleeping two or three hours a night, spending money he does not have and quarrelling with everyone who disagreed with him; six years earlier he had a depressive episode. He has been chosen deliberately because almost every heading of the MSE shows something, so the model shows you how abnormal findings are recorded. Read it heading by heading against Sections 2.4 to 2.6.

Model mental status examination: Mr Manjunath (a composite case)

Conditions. Examined in the outpatient department on 12 March at 11 a.m., in Kannada, for about 45 minutes; his brother was present for the first ten minutes. General appearance and behaviour. A well-built man who looks his stated age, wearing a new, brightly coloured shirt, three gold chains and dark glasses, which he kept on indoors until asked to remove them; hair freshly dyed; hygiene adequate. Eye contact excessive. Overfamiliar and jocular, calling the examiner "boss", and briefly irritable when interrupted. Rapport established easily but difficult to sustain. Psychomotor activity: increased; got up from his chair four times, walked to the window and handled the objects on the desk; no abnormal involuntary movements. Speech: spontaneous; rate and volume increased; pressured and difficult to interrupt; reaction time shortened (began answering before questions were finished); relevant and coherent at first, less goal-directed as the interview went on; frequent rhymes and puns. Mood: subjectively, "I have never felt better in my life, I am on top of the world." Affect: elated, with irritability when contradicted; labile (tearful for a few seconds when speaking of his late father, laughing again within a minute); reactive; congruent with the content of his thought.

Thought. Stream and form: pressure of thought with flight of ideas; a sample: "I will buy ten autos, ten autos, ten toes, my toes are faster than Usain Bolt, bolt the door, the door to success is open." Content: a grandiose delusion that the Chief Minister has personally chosen him to run all the transport in Mysuru, held with full conviction and not shaken when his brother pointed out that he has never met the Chief Minister; no persecutory ideas; denies suicidal ideation; no homicidal ideation, but said of his brother, who has taken away his ATM card, "I will teach him a lesson"; no obsessions. Possession of thought: no thought insertion, withdrawal or broadcasting. Perception: denies hallucinations; none inferred from his behaviour; no illusions, depersonalisation or derealisation.

Cognitive functions. Conscious and alert. Oriented to time, place and person. Attention easily aroused but not sustained; distracted by traffic noise and by objects on the desk. Digit span 6 forwards and 4 backwards; abandoned serial sevens after 86 ("I don't need mathematics, I will have accountants"); months backwards with two errors from distraction. Immediate memory: registered three words at the first attempt. Recent memory: recalled 3 of 3 at five minutes; described the morning's journey correctly. Remote memory intact (gave the year of his marriage and his children's ages, confirmed by his wife). Intelligence: average, consistent with his education to the tenth standard; general information adequate (named the Prime Minister and the Chief Minister, and the price of petrol). Abstract thinking: explained haasige iddashtu kaalu chaachu as "live within your means", then added, laughing, "but my bed has no end"; similarities abstract. Judgement: personal judgement impaired (plans to borrow fifteen lakh rupees to buy ten auto-rickshaws and sees no difficulty in repaying it); social judgement impaired (overfamiliar with the women at the registration counter, on his brother's report and as observed); test judgement intact. Insight: grade 1, complete denial: "Nothing is wrong with me. My brother is jealous; I came only because my wife was crying." Summary and risk. A 34-year-old man with increased psychomotor activity, pressured speech, elated and labile affect, flight of ideas and a grandiose delusion, with distractible attention, intact orientation and memory, impaired personal and social judgement and no insight. No suicidal ideation; risk of financial harm to himself and his family; verbal threat towards his brother, with no history of violence, requiring assessment (Section 2.7).

Notice three things about the model. Every abnormal finding is backed by an example or a quotation; the cognitive section separates a problem of attention (he gave up serial sevens because he was distracted) from problems of memory and orientation (both intact), which is exactly the pattern expected in mania; and the judgement entry shows intact test judgement sitting beside impaired personal and social judgement, the trap from Section 2.6.8 in a real patient. What the MSE does not do is give a diagnosis. That comes in the formulation (Section 2.9), which joins the photograph to the film.

What did the same headings show for Ravi? His cognitive examination was close to normal, which is itself informative. He was alert and fully oriented; his digit span was 6 forwards and 4 backwards; he completed serial sevens slowly and with visible effort, making two slips that he corrected himself; he recalled 3 of 3 words at five minutes, although he insisted beforehand that he would not remember any. His complaint that "nothing goes into my head" was therefore far worse than his measured performance, the pattern of depression described in Section 2.6.5. His intelligence was above average and his abstraction intact; his social and test judgement were intact, but his personal judgement was coloured by hopelessness ("There is no point in applying again"). His insight was grade 3: he agreed that he could not sleep or study and accepted help for his sleep, but blamed everything on the examination and his father and said that "no tablet can change my result". And the MSE recorded one more finding, under thought content, that the clinician could not leave there: a passive wish to be dead, and the sentence his mother had already reported, "everyone would be better off without me". What a clinician does with that sentence is the subject of the next section.

2.7 Risk Assessment: Suicide and ViolenceLower yield · not asked yet

"Everyone would be better off without me." Ravi said it to his mother a week before he came to the clinic, and he said something like it again in the interview. What should the clinician do with that sentence? Two reactions are common in trainees, and both are wrong. One is to panic: to stop the assessment, telephone the family and arrange an admission on the strength of a single sentence. The other is to let it pass, because Ravi is polite, has no plan and "doesn't seem the type". The right response lies between them, and it is a skill: a calm, systematic inquiry into whether this person is likely to harm himself, or anyone else, in the near future, and what can be done to make that less likely. That inquiry is risk assessment.

Risk assessment is not a separate test bolted onto the end of the interview. It runs through everything you have read in this chapter: the history (previous attempts, family suicides, substance use, past violence, Section 2.3), the mental state (hopelessness, delusions, command hallucinations, insight, Sections 2.5 and 2.6) and the formulation (Section 2.9). It also needs an honest starting point. No clinician, and no scale, can predict whether a particular person will die by suicide or commit a violent act. What a clinician can do is identify the people and the moments at which risk is raised, understand why, and act on the factors that can be changed. Prevention at the level of services and populations (means restriction, helplines, gatekeeper training, safety planning) is taught in Section 9.7, and dialectical behaviour therapy for chronic self-harm in Section 5.8. This section is about the assessment of one person in one room.

Asking about suicide: the words and the ladder

First, the vocabulary, because exam questions and case notes depend on it. The term suicidal ideation means thoughts of ending one's life. It may be passive (a wish to be dead, to not wake up, to be killed in an accident, without any thought of acting) or active (thoughts of killing oneself). By contrast, suicidal intent is the strength of the person's wish to die and of his expectation that he will act. A suicide plan specifies the method, the place and the time. Preparatory acts are steps taken to carry the plan out: buying tablets, collecting pesticide, writing a note or a will, giving away belongings, saying goodbyes. Rehearsal is a practice run, either in imagination or in action: standing at the edge of the terrace, holding the tablets, tying the rope. A suicide attempt is a self-injurious act carried out with at least some intent to die; it is interrupted when someone else stops it and aborted when the person stops himself before harm is done. The broader term self-harm, the one used in British and Indian guidelines, covers any intentional self-poisoning or self-injury, whatever its motive, while non-suicidal self-injury (NSSI) is deliberate damage to one's own body, such as cutting or burning, without any intent to die, usually to relieve unbearable tension or to punish oneself. (DSM-5-TR codes current and past suicidal behaviour and NSSI among the conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention, and proposes a disorder of each for further study; these belong to the Psychiatric Disorders book.)

Second, the principle. You ask, directly and in plain words. Section 2.2 introduced the fear that underlies most avoidance, the belief that asking about suicide puts the idea into someone's head, and the evidence that it does not. The study that settled the question for many clinicians is worth knowing in detail.

Key study: does asking about suicide plant the idea? (Gould and colleagues, 2005)

Madelyn Gould and her colleagues at Columbia University tested the fear directly. In six high schools in New York State, between 2002 and 2004, 2,342 students taking part in a screening programme were randomly assigned, class by class, to complete one of two versions of the screening questionnaires: one that included questions about suicidal thoughts and behaviour, and one identical except that those questions were left out. Two days later all the students were asked about distress and suicidal ideation.

If asking plants the idea, the students who had been asked should have reported more suicidal thinking. They did not: the two groups did not differ. The students at highest risk (those who were depressed, using substances or had attempted suicide before) were not harmed either: if anything, the depressed students who had been asked were less distressed, and the previous attempters who had been asked less suicidal, than comparable high-risk students who had not. Later systematic reviews (Dazzi and colleagues, 2014, Section 2.2) reached the same conclusion for adults and adolescents. Asking is safe; not asking leaves risk undiscovered.

Third, the method. The inquiry climbs a ladder, one step at a time, from the general to the specific, as Figure 2.8 shows. It starts with a normalising lead-in that gives permission to answer honestly: "When people are as low as you have been, they sometimes feel that life is not worth living. Have you felt like that?" A "yes" leads to the next rung: thoughts of ending life, then intent, then plan and means, then preparation and rehearsal; and, whatever the answers, the clinician asks about any past attempt. Use clear words such as "kill yourself" or "end your life", not euphemisms like "do something silly", which allow both of you to misunderstand. In Kannada, Hindi or any other language, learn the plain local phrase and use it. And see the client alone for part of the interview: an adolescent will not tell you about last week's tablets with her father in the room.

Each rung has its own follow-up questions. For ideation: how often the thoughts come, how long they last, how intense they are, whether he can push them away, and what stops him from acting on them (his children, his religion, fear of pain, the thought of his mother finding him). For intent: "How likely do you think it is that you will act on these thoughts?" and "What do you expect would happen?" For the plan: the method, the place and the time, and whether the plan includes avoiding discovery. For the means: whether the method is available, a question with special force in India, where pesticides stored in a farmhouse, a stock of tablets, a rope, a high terrace or a railway line near the house may be within easy reach. For preparation and rehearsal: whether he has bought or saved anything, written a note, settled debts, given away possessions or "practised". Rehearsal matters because each practice run makes the act less frightening and more possible, an idea developed by the interpersonal theory in Section 2.7.2.

A past attempt is explored in detail, because it is the best guide to what the person might do again. The clinician asks what happened, step by step, and listens for the signs of high intent that Aaron Beck and his colleagues built into the Suicide Intent Scale (1974): was the person alone, was the act timed so that discovery was unlikely, did he take precautions against being found, did he leave a note or make final arrangements, did he tell anyone beforehand, and did he believe that the method would kill him? The clinician adds one more question of his own: is he now sorry or relieved to have survived? Notice that the last questions concern belief, not the medical facts. The lethality of an act, its medical danger, is not the same as intent. A 15-year-old girl who swallows five paracetamol tablets believing they will kill her has shown high intent with low lethality; a man who swallows a cupful of a highly toxic pesticide in a fit of anger at his wife, meaning only to frighten her, has shown low intent with very high lethality. Both are serious, for different reasons, and many people are wrong about how dangerous a method is. (Beck's Suicide Intent Scale, his Scale for Suicide Ideation and the Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale are taught in the Assessment book.)

Exam trap

Asking directly about suicide does not increase suicidal thinking (Gould and colleagues, 2005; Dazzi and colleagues, 2014); it is the standard of care. The steps run ideation (passive, then active), intent, plan, means, preparation and rehearsal, with past attempts asked about in every case. Lethality (medical danger) is not intent (the wish and expectation of dying). Self-harm covers all intentional self-poisoning and self-injury whatever the motive; non-suicidal self-injury has no intent to die; an interrupted attempt is stopped by someone else, an aborted one by the person himself.

Risk factors, warning signs and protective factors

The answers to the ladder of questions are weighed against what is known, from studies of large populations, about who dies by suicide. A risk factor is a characteristic that is associated with a higher probability of suicide across a population. A protective factor is one associated with a lower probability. Table 2.20 sets out the main ones, and a few deserve comment.

Table 2.20 The main risk and protective factors for suicide.
DomainRisk factorsProtective factors
ClinicalDepression, bipolar disorder, schizophrenia, substance use (especially alcohol), personality disorder; insomnia, agitationEffective treatment, adherence and a good therapeutic alliance
HistoryPrevious attempt (the strongest single predictor); family history of suicide; childhood abusePast crises survived with help
PsychologicalHopelessness; perceived burdensomeness; impulsivity; shame and humiliationReasons for living; hope and future plans; problem-solving skills
SocialLiving alone; widowed, separated or divorced; isolation; recent loss or conflictFamily support; responsibility for children; community and religious ties, including moral objection to suicide
SituationalAccess to lethal means; recent discharge from a psychiatric ward; debt, job loss, chronic painful illnessRestricted access to means; easy access to care
DemographicMale sex (for death by suicide); age bands that differ by countryNone in particular

A previous suicide attempt is the strongest single predictor of eventual suicide: in follow-up studies of people treated after self-harm, roughly 1 to 2 percent die by suicide within a year, a risk many times that of the general population, and the risk remains raised for years. Mental disorder is present in the great majority of suicides in Western psychological autopsy studies, around 90 percent, with depression the commonest; studies in China have found a lower proportion and Indian studies give mixed figures, and in both countries acute interpersonal and financial crises, and impulsive acts with highly lethal poisons, play a larger part. Hopelessness, the expectation that the future holds nothing good and that nothing can change it, is the psychological factor with the strongest evidence. In a ten-year follow-up of patients who had been admitted to hospital with suicidal ideation, Beck and his colleagues (1985) found that, of all the measures taken on admission, only the Beck Hopelessness Scale (Chapter 5) and the pessimism item of the Beck Depression Inventory predicted who would later die by suicide; hopelessness mattered more than how depressed the patient was. Sex works in opposite directions for different outcomes: in almost every country men die by suicide more often than women, while women report more suicidal ideation and make more attempts, a pattern called the gender paradox of suicide. Even here India is unusual: Indian women have some of the highest suicide rates of women anywhere in the world.

Why do some people with these risk factors die while most do not? Two theories are worth knowing, one old and one new. The French sociologist Émile Durkheim (1858 to 1917), in Le Suicide (1897), argued that suicide rates reflect how well a society integrates and regulates its members, and described four types: egoistic suicide, from too little integration (the isolated person with no ties); altruistic suicide, from too much (the person who dies for the group); anomic suicide, from too little regulation, when sudden change, such as a financial collapse or the loss of a high-status job, leaves people without norms; and fatalistic suicide, from too much regulation, when the future is blocked by oppressive control. (Durkheim's sociology is taught more fully in the Social Psychology book.)

The newer theory is the interpersonal theory of suicide of the American psychologist Thomas E. Joiner, set out in Why People Die by Suicide (2005) and refined with Kimberly Van Orden and colleagues (2010). Joiner asked why so few of the people who want to die actually kill themselves, and answered that suicide requires two separate things. The desire for death arises when two painful states occur together: perceived burdensomeness, the belief that one's existence is a burden on the people one loves, so that they would be better off without one, and thwarted belongingness, the feeling of being alienated and cut off from others. The capability for suicide, which Joiner called acquired capability, is the lowered fear of death and raised tolerance of pain that people acquire through repeated exposure to painful and frightening experiences: previous attempts, self-injury, rehearsal, violence. Only when desire and capability are present together does a lethal attempt become likely. Listen to Ravi again with the theory in mind: "everyone would be better off without me" is perceived burdensomeness in its purest form, and a young man who has stopped going home at weekends and barely leaves his rented room is losing his sense of belonging. The theory also explains why rehearsal and previous attempts are so ominous: they build capability.

Risk factors describe who is at risk over months and years. By contrast, warning signs describe who may be at risk now, in the coming hours or days. A working group of the American Association of Suicidology condensed them into the mnemonic IS PATH WARM: Ideation, Substance abuse; Purposelessness, Anxiety, Trapped, Hopelessness; Withdrawal, Anger, Recklessness, Mood change. Some warning signs are behaviours that family members notice before clinicians do: talking or writing about death, searching for methods, giving away prized possessions, saying goodbye, and a sudden, unexplained calm in a person who has been deeply depressed, which may mean that a decision has been made.

Protective factors are the other side of the balance. Marsha M. Linehan (1943 to 2026), the creator of dialectical behaviour therapy, and her colleagues built the Reasons for Living Inventory (1983) on the observation that what keeps many suicidal people alive is not the absence of pain but the presence of reasons: responsibility to family and children, fear of the act, moral or religious objection, plans for the future and belief in one's ability to cope. In India, family ties and religious belief are often powerful protective factors, although the family can also be the source of the crisis, as Ravi's is. Two cautions apply. Protective factors do not cancel risk factors arithmetically: a man with a loving wife is still at high risk if he has a rope in his room and a plan for tonight. And a protective factor can disappear overnight, with a quarrel, a bereavement or a failed examination.

Finally, the factors differ in whether they can change. The static risk factors are fixed, or change only in one direction: sex, age, a history of previous attempts, a family history of suicide, childhood abuse. They set the person's baseline level of risk. The dynamic risk factors can change, and can be changed: hopelessness, the severity of depression, insomnia, agitation, alcohol use, isolation, access to means. They determine the person's current level of risk, and they are the targets of every intervention. The distinction, as you will see in Section 2.7.5, is equally central to the assessment of violence.

Indian context

The National Crime Records Bureau records more than 1.7 lakh suicides a year in India (Section 9.7 gives the figures). Most occur between the ages of 18 and 45, so the Western picture of suicide as chiefly a problem of older men does not fit. Family problems and illness are the two commonest reasons recorded, and hanging and poisoning (much of it with pesticides) are the commonest methods. Groups that appear repeatedly in the records include daily-wage earners, housewives, farmers and agricultural labourers, and students, among whom examination results and coaching-centre pressure are recurring triggers, exactly Ravi's situation. Because many suicides in India follow an acute crisis with a highly lethal poison close at hand, asking about access to means is not a formality.

Exam trap

The strongest single predictor of suicide is a previous attempt; the strongest psychological predictor is hopelessness (Beck and colleagues, 1985), more than the severity of depression. Men die by suicide more often, women attempt more often (the gender paradox). Joiner's interpersonal theory (2005): desire = perceived burdensomeness + thwarted belongingness; capability = acquired capability (habituation to pain and fear). Durkheim (1897): egoistic, altruistic, anomic, fatalistic. Risk factors are long-term and population-based; warning signs (IS PATH WARM) are near-term. Static factors cannot be changed; dynamic factors can, and they are what treatment targets.

The SAD PERSONS mnemonic and its limits

Faced with a long list of risk factors, busy doctors wanted something they could remember in an emergency department at three in the morning. In 1983 William Patterson and his colleagues Henry Dohn, Julian Bird and Gary Patterson, writing in the journal Psychosomatics, offered one. The SAD PERSONS scale turns ten risk factors into an acronym, scores one point for each factor present and links the total to a recommended action (Table 2.21). It was devised as a teaching aid, to help medical students and non-psychiatrists remember what to ask, and in Patterson's own report medical students who had been taught it were better than untaught students at judging a low-risk and a high-risk patient correctly and recommending what should be done, as rated by three experienced psychiatrists.

Table 2.21 The SAD PERSONS scale (Patterson, Dohn, Bird and Patterson, 1983). One point for each factor present.
LetterRisk factor
SSex: male
AAge: under 19 or over 45
DDepression
PPrevious attempt
EEthanol (alcohol) abuse
RRational thinking loss (psychosis, confusion)
SSocial supports lacking
OOrganised plan
NNo spouse (single, widowed, separated or divorced)
SSickness (chronic, debilitating illness)
Suggested action0 to 2: send home with follow-up; 3 to 4: close follow-up, consider admission; 5 to 6: strongly consider admission; 7 to 10: admit, involuntarily if necessary

Five years later Hockberger and Rothstein (1988) produced a modified SAD PERSONS scale for emergency physicians, which gives two points instead of one to four items that seemed to matter more (depression or hopelessness, rational thinking loss, an organised or serious attempt, and a stated intent to repeat it) and changes several others. Both versions are still widely taught, and as a checklist they are useful: a clinician who runs through the letters will not forget to ask about alcohol or a previous attempt.

As a way of deciding what to do with a patient, however, the scale has been heavily criticised, and an examiner who asks you to "use and criticise" it expects you to know why. First, it weights every item equally, so that being over 45 counts as much as having an organised plan, although the evidence says that plans, previous attempts and hopelessness matter far more. Second, it leaves out factors with strong evidence: hopelessness (in the original version), access to lethal means, recent discharge from hospital, impulsivity, a family history of suicide, childhood abuse and every protective factor. Third, its demographic items come from Western data and travel badly. In India, where most suicides occur between 18 and 45 and young women are at high risk, a 25-year-old married woman scores no points for sex, age or marital status. Fourth, most of its items are static, so the score barely changes as the patient improves or deteriorates. Fifth, and most important, when it has been tested as a predictor it has failed. In a study of 4,019 consecutive referrals to the emergency psychiatric services of the two largest hospitals in Manitoba, Canada, both in Winnipeg, James Bolton and his colleagues (2012) found that the scale predicted later suicide attempts no better than chance and, at its usual high-risk cut-off, missed about four in five of the patients who went on to make an attempt, and a systematic review by Warden and colleagues (2014) found that none of the studies that tested it showed that it predicted suicidal behaviour accurately, and concluded that there was insufficient evidence to support its use in assessing or predicting suicidal behaviour.

Behind these particular faults lies a problem that no scale can solve: suicide is rare. Even in a group at raised risk, only a small proportion of people die by suicide in any given year, and when an outcome is rare, any test, however good, produces many more false alarms than true alarms. A false positive is a person whom the test flags as high risk who would not have died; a false negative is a person whom it calls low risk who does. The proportion of an outcome in the population being tested is its base rate. A worked example shows the arithmetic. Suppose a risk scale, far better than any that exists, correctly flags 80 percent of the people who will die by suicide (its sensitivity) and correctly clears 80 percent of those who will not (its specificity), and suppose it is used on 1,000 patients of whom 10 (1 percent) will die by suicide within a year. It catches 8 of the 10 and misses 2. But it also flags 20 percent of the 990 who would have lived, which is 198 people. Of the 206 patients labelled "high risk", only 8, fewer than 4 percent, would have died. If the label decides admission, the ward fills with people who were never going to die, and 2 of the 10 who were are sent home with a "low risk" note in the file.

Key study: Pokorny's attempt to predict suicide (1983)

The American psychiatrist Alex Pokorny asked the obvious question: if we know the risk factors, can we use them to pick out, in advance, the patients who will die? He assessed about 4,800 consecutive patients admitted to the psychiatric service of a Veterans Administration hospital in Houston, Texas, using the best risk factors and scales of the day, and then followed them for about five years. Sixty-seven of them died by suicide.

His prediction did identify 35 of those 67 people, about half. It also identified 1,206 people who did not die by suicide: for every person correctly flagged, there were about 35 false positives, and the 32 people it missed included some who had seemed to be at low risk. Pokorny concluded that the prediction of suicide in individual patients was not feasible with the tools available. Four decades later a meta-analysis of 365 studies covering 50 years of research (Franklin and colleagues, 2017) reached essentially the same conclusion: known risk factors predict suicidal thoughts and behaviour only slightly better than chance, and prediction had not improved over the half-century.

The lesson has been taken into clinical guidance. The 2022 guideline on self-harm of the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) in England advises clinicians not to use risk assessment tools and scales to predict future suicide or repetition of self-harm, and not to use a global classification of patients into low, medium or high risk to decide who should be offered treatment or who should be discharged. Instead it asks for a thorough psychosocial assessment of each person's needs and of the factors that can be changed. That does not make risk assessment pointless; it changes its purpose, from prediction to understanding and management.

Exam trap

SAD PERSONS was published by Patterson and colleagues in 1983 as a teaching mnemonic for ten risk factors (Sex, Age, Depression, Previous attempt, Ethanol, Rational thinking loss, Social supports lacking, Organised plan, No spouse, Sickness), one point each; the modified version (Hockberger and Rothstein, 1988) double-weights four items. Its main limits: equal weights, missing factors (hopelessness, means, protective factors), Western demographics, static items and poor predictive validity. Prediction fails mainly because of the low base rate, which guarantees many false positives (Pokorny, 1983: 35 of 67 suicides identified, with 1,206 false positives). Use the mnemonic as a checklist, never as a score that decides admission or discharge.

From prediction to formulation: Ravi's risk assessment

If the score cannot decide, what does? The clinician's job is to build a risk formulation: a short, reasoned account of this person's risk that pulls together the static and dynamic factors, the warning signs, the answers to the ladder of questions and the protective factors, and explains them. A good risk formulation answers five questions. How high is this person's risk compared with other people (his chronic, baseline risk, set mostly by static factors)? How high is it now compared with his own usual level (his acute risk, set mostly by dynamic factors and warning signs)? Anthony Pisani and his colleagues (2016) call these two judgements risk status and risk state. Why is the risk raised now? What could make it rise suddenly? And what can be done, today, to bring it down? Notice that the last three questions are about understanding and action, not prediction: they are the 5 Ps of case formulation (Section 2.9) applied to one outcome.

Here is how the clinician worked through it with Ravi. On the ladder, Ravi described daily passive thoughts that he would be better off dead and, on direct questioning, occasional thoughts over the past fortnight of "just ending it" by drinking the pesticide kept in the shed at his parents' farm. He said he had no intention of acting, because "it would kill my mother", had made no plan beyond that passing thought, had done nothing to prepare and had never rehearsed. He had never harmed himself; the episode at 17 involved no self-harm.

  • Static factors: male; a possible previous depressive episode (the month of untreated sadness at 17); a family history of depression (his maternal uncle); no previous attempt; no family history of suicide.
  • Dynamic factors: a moderately severe depressive episode with insomnia and weight loss; hopelessness ("there is no point in applying again"); perceived burdensomeness ("better off without me") and guilt about the pledged gold; withdrawal from his family (he has stopped going home and barely leaves the shared room he rents in Mysuru); access to a highly lethal method whenever he visits the farm.
  • Warning signs: hopelessness, withdrawal, feeling trapped; no sudden calm, no farewells, no giving away of possessions.
  • Protective factors: his strong bond with his mother, who has been staying with him for the past ten days, and his explicit reason for living ("it would kill my mother"); his religious faith; no alcohol or drug use; a good, if new, relationship with the clinician; and his willingness to come back.

The formulation that followed was written in plain sentences. Ravi's baseline risk is modestly raised by his sex, a possible earlier depressive episode (so that his depression may prove to be recurrent) and his family history. His current risk is raised above his own usual level by a depressive episode with hopelessness and a strong sense of being a burden, but he has active ideation without intent, plan, preparation or rehearsal, and real protective factors, so the risk at present is not imminent. It could rise quickly with a further humiliation (another result, an angry scene with his father), with alcohol, or if he were alone at the farm with the pesticide while feeling trapped.
