The plan follows from the formulation and targets the dynamic factors: treatment of the depression; a collaborative safety plan written in his own words (the Stanley and Brown method taught in Section 9.7); with his agreement, a conversation with his mother about locking the pesticide away and about the warning signs to watch for; a follow-up appointment within a week, with the risk reviewed at every visit; and a crisis number he could ring.

Notice what the plan does not contain: a no-suicide contract, which has no evidence behind it (Section 9.7), or an admission justified only by a score. For a patient at high and imminent risk, someone with intent, a plan and the means at hand, the response is proportionately stronger: he is not left alone, the means are removed, the family is involved and an urgent psychiatric assessment, often an admission, is arranged. And whatever the level, the clinician documents what was asked, what the patient said, the reasoning and the plan. A clear note is both good care and the clinician's best protection if things go wrong.

One more question arises when risk is high: can the clinician tell the family without the patient's consent? Confidentiality is the rule, but protecting a person from serious and imminent harm is a recognised exception in every professional code and in Indian law; the rules and their limits are set out in Chapter 1. In practice the clinician tries first to obtain the client's agreement, as with Ravi, and explains honestly that safety comes before secrecy.

Indian context

For most of its history Indian law treated a suicide attempt as a crime, under section 309 of the Indian Penal Code. Section 115 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 changed this: notwithstanding section 309, a person who attempts suicide is presumed, unless the contrary is proved, to have severe stress and is not to be tried or punished, and the government has a duty to provide that person with care, treatment and rehabilitation to reduce the risk of another attempt. (The fuller legal story, including the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita that replaced the Penal Code in 2024, is in Section 9.7 and Chapter 1.) For the assessing clinician the change matters in a practical way. Fear of the police once kept many families from bringing a person to hospital after an attempt, and kept many patients from admitting what they had done. Telling a frightened patient that a suicide attempt is now treated as a sign of distress needing care, not as an offence, is often what makes an honest account possible.

Exam trap

The modern aim of suicide risk assessment is formulation and management, not prediction. Chronic (baseline) risk is set largely by static factors; acute risk by dynamic factors and warning signs (Pisani's risk status and risk state). Management targets dynamic factors. A safety plan (Stanley and Brown) is recommended; a no-suicide contract is not. Under section 115 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, a person who attempts suicide is presumed to have severe stress and is not to be tried or punished, and the government must provide care, treatment and rehabilitation.

Assessing the risk of violence

The second half of risk assessment asks whether the person may harm someone else. Mr Manjunath's MSE in Section 2.6.11 ended with a verbal threat against his brother; the same question arises with a man who hears voices telling him that his neighbour is poisoning him, a husband convinced that his wife is unfaithful, a young man brought in drunk after a fight. Before looking at how the assessment is done, it helps to clear away a myth that distorts it.

The myth is that mental illness makes people dangerous. The great majority of people with mental illness are never violent, and people with severe mental illness are far more likely to be the victims of violence than its perpetrators. There is a modest association between some disorders and violence, but most of it is accounted for by other factors, above all substance use. The clearest evidence came from the MacArthur Violence Risk Assessment Study, led by Henry Steadman and John Monahan, which followed 1,136 patients for a year after their discharge from acute psychiatric wards in three American cities and, at one of the sites (Pittsburgh), compared them with people living in the same neighbourhoods (Steadman and colleagues, 1998). Discharged patients who did not misuse alcohol or drugs were no more violent than their neighbours who did not; substance misuse raised the rate of violence in both groups, and raised it more in the patients. In a Swedish national study, Seena Fazel and Martin Grann (2006) estimated that only about 5 percent of violent crime could be attributed to people with severe mental illness.

Within that picture, some factors do raise risk, and they are what the clinician asks about. The most powerful by far is the oldest rule in the field: the best predictor of future violence is past violence, especially if it was recent, frequent, serious or unprovoked. Others are being young and male, substance misuse, antisocial personality traits and psychopathy, a history of childhood abuse or of witnessing violence, poor adherence to treatment and lack of insight. Among symptoms, attention has fallen on what Bruce Link and Ann Stueve (1994) called threat/control-override symptoms: the belief that one is being threatened or persecuted by others combined with the experience that one's mind is being controlled by outside forces, which together seem to override the usual restraints against violence. (Later analyses of the MacArthur data found the link between delusions and violence weaker than expected, so the evidence is mixed.) Command hallucinations instructing harm, persecutory delusions accompanied by anger, and morbid (delusional) jealousy, a well-known danger to the spouse, are each asked about specifically. Intoxication, withdrawal states, delirium, the irritability of mania and the agitation of dementia account for much of the short-term violence seen on wards and in emergency departments.

Key study: the Baxstrom patients and the limits of clinical prediction (1966 to 1981)

In 1966, in Baxstrom v. Herold, the United States Supreme Court ruled that Johnnie Baxstrom, a prisoner kept in a New York hospital for the criminally insane after his sentence had ended, had been detained without the legal protections given to other people facing civil commitment. As a result nearly a thousand patients who had been held in secure hospitals, because clinicians had judged them too dangerous for ordinary ones, were transferred to civil hospitals or released. Henry Steadman and Joseph Cocozza followed them up (their book Careers of the Criminally Insane appeared in 1974). In the first four years after the transfers only a minority, about one in five, were assaultive in the civil hospitals or the community, and by 1970 only about 3 percent had been returned to secure hospitals. Most of the people predicted to be dangerous were not.

Reviewing this and similar studies, John Monahan concluded in The Clinical Prediction of Violent Behavior (1981) that psychiatrists and psychologists were accurate in no more than one out of three predictions of violence over several years, even in institutionalised populations with a history of violence. His review launched the search for better methods that produced the instruments described next.

Three broad approaches to violence risk assessment have grown out of that research, and Table 2.22 compares them. The first, unstructured clinical judgement, is what clinicians did before the research: weighing whatever seemed relevant, with no fixed list and no rules. It is flexible, but it is unreliable and, as Monahan showed, inaccurate over the long term. The second, actuarial risk assessment, combines a fixed set of factors by a statistical formula derived from follow-up studies, in the way insurance companies calculate premiums. The best known, the Violence Risk Appraisal Guide (VRAG) of Grant Harris, Marnie Rice and Vernon Quinsey (1993), scores twelve mostly historical items, of which the Psychopathy Checklist-Revised (PCL-R) score carries the most weight. Actuarial tools are reliable and outperform unaided judgement, but they rely mainly on static factors, ignore anything not in the formula and say little about what to do. The third, structured professional judgement (SPJ), is the compromise now preferred in most services: the clinician rates every item on a fixed, evidence-based list, including dynamic factors, and then uses professional judgement to reach a final risk formulation and, above all, a management plan.

Table 2.22 Three approaches to assessing the risk of violence.
ApproachHow it worksStrengthWeakness
Unstructured clinical judgementClinician weighs whatever seems relevantFlexible, individualUnreliable; poor long-term accuracy
Actuarial (VRAG, COVR)Fixed factors combined by a statistical formulaReliable; beats unaided judgementMostly static; little guidance on management
Structured professional judgement (HCR-20)Fixed list of evidence-based factors, then a reasoned clinical judgementIncludes dynamic factors; guides managementNeeds training; final step remains a judgement

The best-known SPJ scheme is the HCR-20, developed in Canada by Christopher Webster, Kevin Douglas, Derek Eaves and Stephen Hart (version 2, 1997) and revised as version 3 in 2013 by Douglas, Hart, Webster and Belfrage. Its name gives its structure: twenty factors grouped into Historical, Clinical and Risk management scales (Table 2.23). The Historical items are largely static and describe the past; the Clinical items are dynamic and describe the present; the Risk management items look forward to the situation the person will face. Notice that insight, the last heading of the MSE, appears as the first Clinical item: the MSE and the risk assessment are the same examination seen from two angles.

Table 2.23 The structure of the HCR-20 (version 3, 2013).
ScaleItemsTime focusExamples of factors
Historical10 (H1 to H10)Past; largely staticHistory of violence, substance use, major mental disorder, personality disorder, traumatic experiences
Clinical5 (C1 to C5)Present; dynamicInsight, violent ideation or intent, symptoms of major mental disorder, instability, response to treatment
Risk management5 (R1 to R5)Future; dynamicProfessional services and plans, living situation, personal support, stress and coping

A psychologist in an outpatient clinic will rarely complete an HCR-20; that belongs to forensic services (Chapter 10). But the clinical interview asks the same questions, directly and without embarrassment, just as it does for suicide. "Have you had thoughts of hurting anyone? Who? Have you thought about how? Do you have a weapon, or access to one? What is the most violent thing you have ever done? When did you last hit someone? Had you been drinking?" The answers about past violence are explored like a past suicide attempt: the victim, the circumstances, the trigger, the injuries, the consequences, and how the person feels about it now. Informants and records matter even more than usual, because people minimise their own violence. The result is formulated in the same way as suicide risk, with static and dynamic factors, triggers and protective factors, and a plan that targets what can change. For Mr Manjunath the static factors were few (no past violence, no substance use), while the dynamic ones, the irritability of mania, poor insight and a quarrel over money with the brother who had taken his card, were all treatable; the plan was treatment of the mania, a calmer arrangement over the family's finances, and a review of the threat at every contact. When a client makes a credible threat against an identifiable person, the clinician also faces the legal and ethical duty to protect that person, the subject of the Tarasoff rulings of 1974 and 1976 discussed in Chapter 1.

Clinical link

Assessing an agitated patient safely begins before you enter the room: read the file, tell a colleague where you are, and use a room in which you have a clear path to the door without blocking the patient's own way out (a patient who feels trapped is more likely to lash out). Remove objects that could be thrown, know where the alarm is, and do not interview a threatening patient alone. Watch for the signs of escalation: pacing, clenched fists, a rising voice, swearing, staring, moving into your space, threats and banging objects (short checklists such as the Brøset Violence Checklist rate six such behaviours on wards: confusion, irritability, boisterousness, verbal threats, physical threats and attacks on objects). The skills of de-escalation are the interviewing skills of Section 2.2 under pressure: a calm, low voice; standing or sitting at more than arm's length and at an angle rather than face to face; acknowledging the feeling ("I can see you are very angry about being brought here"); not arguing with delusions; offering choices and something to drink; and ending the interview, without heroics, if the situation keeps escalating.

Exam trap

The best single predictor of future violence is past violence. Most people with mental illness are not violent and are more often victims; the modest association is driven largely by substance misuse (MacArthur study, Steadman and colleagues, 1998). Monahan (1981): clinicians' long-term predictions were right at most one time in three. Actuarial tools (VRAG) use a statistical formula of mostly static factors; structured professional judgement (HCR-20) uses a fixed list plus clinical judgement and includes dynamic factors. HCR-20 = Historical (10 items), Clinical (5), Risk management (5), 20 in all. Threat/control-override symptoms are Link and Stueve's (1994). The duty to protect a threatened third party comes from Tarasoff (Chapter 1).

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The mildest impairment of consciousness, which fluctuates and is the hallmark of delirium, is of consciousness.
  2. The Glasgow Coma Scale runs from a minimum of to a maximum of 15.
  3. Orientation is usually lost in the order time, then , then person.
  4. Taking 7 away from 100 repeatedly is the serial sevens test of .
  5. The rule that recent memories are lost before remote ones is law.
  6. Filling gaps in memory with false memories, without intent to deceive, is .
  7. Saying how a person would act on finding a stamped, addressed envelope on the road tests judgement.
  8. A patient who accepts that he is ill but blames his illness on black magic or on external events has grade insight; grade 6 is true insight.
  9. The SAD PERSONS mnemonic was published by Patterson and colleagues in ; the strongest single predictor of suicide is a previous .
  10. In Joiner's interpersonal theory, the desire for suicide arises from perceived burdensomeness and thwarted ; in the HCR-20, the letters stand for Historical, Clinical and .

2.8 Behavioural AssessmentLower yield · 1 Q

Every tool you have met so far in this chapter asks, in the end, the same question: what is wrong with this person? The interview gathers the complaints, the case history sets them in a life, the mental status examination describes the mind at this moment and the risk assessment weighs the danger. Behavioural assessment asks a different question, and it is worth stating it plainly because everything in this section follows from it: what exactly does this person do, in which situations, and what keeps him doing it? A diagnosis of depression tells you that Ravi belongs to a class of people who share certain symptoms. It does not tell you that he ends every phone call with his father within a minute, that he lies in bed with his phone for three hours every evening, or that each of these acts makes him feel better for ten minutes and worse for a week. Those are the facts a behaviour therapist needs, because they are the facts that can be changed.

Behavioural assessment grew up alongside behaviour therapy in the 1960s (Chapter 4), and for a while it defined itself mainly by what it rejected: the projective test, the search for hidden conflicts and the psychiatric label. It has since become part of ordinary clinical practice, used by cognitive-behavioural therapists, child psychologists, rehabilitation teams and anyone who needs to know whether a treatment is working. This section teaches its logic, its central method (functional analysis) and its tools: the behavioural interview, self-monitoring, direct observation and the ways of recording it, role play and, briefly, psychophysiological measurement. It also teaches the errors to which each tool is prone, because a measure is only as useful as the clinician's knowledge of how it can mislead.

Samples, not signs: the logic of behavioural assessment

Why did behaviour therapists need a new kind of assessment at all? Part of the answer came from a small book that shook personality psychology. In Personality and Assessment (1968), Walter Mischel (1930 to 2018) reviewed decades of research on whether people behave consistently across situations, and concluded that they do so far less than trait theories assumed. Correlations between a trait measured by a questionnaire and the same trait observed in a particular situation rarely rose above about .30, a ceiling Mischel called the "personality coefficient". A child who is honest at home may cheat at school; a man who is assertive at work may be meek with his mother. Behaviour, Mischel argued, is far more specific to the situation than a trait label implies. (The debate that followed belongs to the Personality book.)

Behaviour therapists drew a practical conclusion. If behaviour depends heavily on its situation, then the most useful way to assess it is to sample it in the situations that matter. In 1972 Marvin R. Goldfried and Ronald Kent put the contrast in a phrase that is still quoted: traditional assessment treats a response as a sign, behavioural assessment treats it as a sample. When a boy sees fighting animals on a Rorschach card, a traditional assessor reads the response as a sign of something underlying (an aggressive drive, an unresolved conflict) that is assumed to cause his behaviour everywhere. A behavioural assessor would rather count how often the boy hits other children in the playground, note what happens just before and just after, and treat that count as a sample of the very behaviour that brought him to the clinic. The inference from a sign to an inner cause is long and uncertain; the inference from a sample to the behaviour it samples is short.

From this starting point come the principles that distinguish behavioural assessment, the direct measurement of specific behaviours and of the environmental and personal variables that control them, in order to plan and evaluate treatment. Table 2.24 sets them against traditional assessment.

Table 2.24 Traditional and behavioural assessment compared.
QuestionTraditional assessmentBehavioural assessment
What is a response taken to be?A sign of an underlying trait, conflict or disorderA sample of behaviour in a situation
Where are the causes sought?Inside the person, often in the pastIn current antecedents and consequences, plus the person's history and biology
How important is the situation?Minor: traits are assumed to be consistentCentral: behaviour is assumed to be situation-specific
Comparison madeMainly nomothetic (with norms for a population)Mainly idiographic (with the same person over time)
When is assessment done?Mostly before treatmentBefore, during and after treatment, continuously
Link to treatmentIndirect: diagnosis first, then treatmentDirect: the analysis names the targets and the techniques

Two features deserve emphasis. First, behavioural assessment is idiographic: it compares a person mainly with himself (his behaviour this week against his behaviour before treatment) rather than with a population norm. Second, it is continuous. The behaviour is measured before treatment to establish the baseline (Section 4.1), measured again throughout treatment, and measured at follow-up, so that the clinician can see whether the intervention is doing what it was meant to do and change course if it is not. In this sense assessment and treatment are never separate stages.

Behavioural assessment is also multimethod. No single source is trusted on its own: what the client says in the interview is checked against what he records in a diary, what a parent or teacher observes, how he behaves in a role play and, where relevant, how his body responds. As the cognitive revolution reached behaviour therapy in the 1970s, the assessment widened to include thoughts, images and expectations, treated as covert behaviours to be sampled in the same specific, situation-bound way ("What went through your mind just before you put the phone down?"). Modern texts therefore speak of cognitive-behavioural assessment; the logic is unchanged.

Exam trap

"Signs versus samples" is Goldfried and Kent (1972): traditional assessment reads a response as a sign of something underlying; behavioural assessment treats it as a sample of the behaviour itself. Behavioural assessment is idiographic, situation-specific, continuous and focused on current maintaining factors; it is not a one-time, norm-referenced test of traits. The "personality coefficient" of about .30 is Walter Mischel's (1968), the starting point of the person-situation debate.

Functional analysis: the ABC, Kanfer and Saslow, and SORKC

Section 4.1 introduced functional analysis as the first step of every behaviour therapy case, and showed it at work on Arjun's homework tantrums. Here we take it apart properly, because it is the heart of behavioural assessment and, as you will see in Section 2.9, a formulation in its own right.

The word "functional" needs explaining first. It comes from Burrhus Frederic Skinner (1904 to 1990), who wrote in Science and Human Behavior (1953) that the external variables of which behaviour is a function provide for a "causal or functional analysis". A behaviour is a function of the variables that control it, in the mathematical sense that it changes when they change. The word also carries its everyday meaning: the function of a behaviour is what it does for the person, what it gets him or lets him escape. Two children may bang their heads against a wall in exactly the same way. For one, head-banging brings an adult running (it is maintained by attention); for the other, it ends a demand to sit at a desk (it is maintained by escape). The topography of the behaviour, its form, is identical; its function is not, and the treatments that work are opposite. Ignoring the first child's head-banging (extinction of attention) might help; ignoring the second child's would change nothing, because attention was never the point. Functional analysis exists to find the function before choosing the treatment.

The ABC. The simplest form of functional analysis is Skinner's three-term contingency, which clinicians write as the ABC: the Antecedent, the Behaviour and the Consequence.

  • The antecedent is what happens just before the behaviour and sets the occasion for it. In Skinner's language an antecedent that signals that a particular consequence is now available is a discriminative stimulus: the sight of the mother's handbag signals to a child that a tantrum may now produce a sweet. Antecedents also include more distant conditions that make the behaviour more or less likely without directly triggering it: a bad night's sleep, hunger, a quarrel earlier in the day, a headache. Behaviour analysts call these setting events (a term that Sidney Bijou and Donald Baer took from the interbehavioural psychology of Jacob Robert Kantor), and Jack Michael in 1982 described a related class, establishing operations, which alter how valuable a consequence is at the moment: a child who has had no adult attention all afternoon finds attention more rewarding than one who has had plenty.
  • The behaviour is described in observable terms (how to do that is the subject of the next subsection). In cognitive-behavioural assessment the B may also be a covert behaviour, such as a thought or an urge, provided it can be specified and recorded.
  • The consequence is what follows the behaviour and changes its future probability. A consequence that increases the behaviour is a reinforcer, positive if something pleasant is added (praise, a sweet, attention) and negative if something unpleasant is removed (a demand withdrawn, anxiety relieved). A consequence that decreases the behaviour is a punisher. When a previously reinforced behaviour stops being reinforced, it extinguishes. (These principles of operant conditioning are taught in General Psychology; their clinical uses are in Chapter 4.)

The consequences that matter most are often the immediate ones. Much of human misery is maintained by behaviour that pays off in the short term and costs in the long term. The American learning theorist Orval Hobart Mowrer (1907 to 1982) called this the neurotic paradox (1948): why do people persist in behaviour that is plainly self-defeating? Because the relief comes at once and the cost comes later, and immediate consequences control behaviour more powerfully than delayed ones. A good functional analysis therefore always separates short-term from long-term consequences.

Notice which kind of learning the ABC describes. A behaviour that is controlled by its consequences is, by definition, an operant. Functional analysis is therefore grounded in operant conditioning. A full analysis does not ignore classical conditioning: an antecedent may be a conditioned stimulus that elicits fear (the smell of a hospital for a man who watched his father die there), and the avoidance that follows is then maintained operantly by the relief it brings, exactly as Mowrer's two-factor theory proposes (Section 4.1). But the analysis of what maintains behaviour through its consequences is operant at its core.

Kanfer and Saslow's behavioural analysis. The ABC is enough for a child's tantrum, but adult outpatients bring tangled lives. In 1965 the psychologist Frederick H. Kanfer (1925 to 2002) and the psychiatrist George Saslow published a paper in the Archives of General Psychiatry whose title stated its ambition: "Behavioral analysis: an alternative to diagnostic classification." Psychiatric diagnosis, they argued, was unreliable and told the clinician almost nothing about what to do. They proposed instead a systematic analysis of the specific behaviours to be changed, the conditions currently maintaining them and the resources available to change them, and they expanded the scheme in a 1969 chapter on "behavioral diagnosis". Their first step was to sort the client's problems into three kinds:

  • behavioural excesses: behaviours that occur too often, too intensely or for too long, or in situations where they are not appropriate (checking the door thirty times, drinking, outbursts of anger, compulsive scrolling at night);
  • behavioural deficits: behaviours that occur too rarely, too weakly or not at all (making requests, initiating conversations, studying, self-care);
  • behavioural assets: the person's non-problematic behaviours, skills and strengths, which treatment can build on.

The rest of their outline covers the areas set out in Table 2.25. Read it as a checklist that stops the clinician from analysing one behaviour in a vacuum.

Table 2.25 The areas of Kanfer and Saslow's behavioural analysis (1965; expanded 1969).
AreaWhat the clinician asks
1. Initial analysis of the problem situationWhat are the behavioural excesses, deficits and assets?
2. Clarification of the problem situationWho objects to the behaviour, who supports it, what would change if it stopped?
3. Motivational analysisWhat reinforces this person? What does he work for or avoid?
4. Developmental analysisWhat biological, social and behavioural changes in his history bear on the problem?
5. Analysis of self-controlIn which situations can he control the behaviour, and how?
6. Analysis of social relationshipsWho are the important people, and how do they reward or punish him?
7. Analysis of the social, cultural and physical environmentWhat norms and settings support or limit the behaviour and its change?

SORKC. Kanfer's behavioural analysis is usually summarised in a five-term formula, SORKC, set out in the 1969 chapter: Kanfer and Saslow added the O to a four-term chain of stimulus, response, contingency and consequence that the behaviour analyst Ogden Lindsley (whom you will meet again below) had described in 1964. S is the stimulus, the antecedent situation. O is the organism: everything the person brings to the situation, from his biological state (illness, fatigue, drugs, hunger) to his learning history and, in later cognitive versions, his expectations, beliefs and appraisals. R is the response, the behaviour, which may be motor, verbal, cognitive or physiological. K is the contingency, the relation between the response and its consequence: does the consequence follow every time (continuous reinforcement) or only sometimes (intermittent), immediately or after a delay? It is written with a K so that it is not confused with the C. C is the consequence, positive or negative, added or removed, short term or long term. Many textbooks use the shorter SORC, which drops the contingency term; some credit that four-term version to Marvin Goldfried and Joyce Sprafkin's chapter "Behavioral personality assessment" (1976). What the O adds to the ABC is the recognition that the same stimulus does not produce the same response in everyone: a father's comment about a cousin's job is a trigger only for a son who already believes his worth lies in results. Figure 2.9 pushes Ravi's evenings through the model.

From description to function. How does a clinician actually discover the function of a behaviour? Behaviour analysts distinguish three levels of functional assessment, in increasing order of rigour. Indirect assessment asks people about the behaviour: the behavioural interview, and rating scales such as the Motivation Assessment Scale (V. Mark Durand and Daniel Crimmins, 1988), whose 16 questions sort self-injurious behaviour into four functions (attention, escape, tangible rewards and sensory stimulation). Descriptive assessment observes the behaviour in its natural setting and records antecedents and consequences as they happen, in an ABC chart or in a scatter plot (Touchette, MacDonald and Langer, 1985), a grid of days against times of day that shows at a glance when the behaviour clusters (every day between 4 and 5 p.m., say, when homework starts). Experimental functional analysis, the method of Brian Iwata and his colleagues (1982) described in Section 4.1, deliberately arranges conditions (attention after the behaviour, escape after the behaviour, play, alone) and sees which one raises the behaviour. Only the experimental method shows cause and effect; the others generate hypotheses. In outpatient work with adults the analysis is mostly indirect and descriptive, and its conclusions are held as hypotheses to be tested by the response to treatment.

For Ravi, the hypothesis that emerges from Figure 2.9 is that his withdrawal is maintained by negative reinforcement (escape from shame and from his father's comparisons) and that it is costing him almost every source of positive reinforcement he used to have. This is precisely the pattern that Peter Lewinsohn's behavioural account of depression describes (Section 5.10), and it points straight to a treatment: scheduling small, achievable activities that bring back reinforcement (behavioural activation), planning shorter and more structured contacts with his father rather than abrupt escapes, and working later on the belief in the O box. The function decided the treatment.

Exam trap

"Functional analysis is a therapy (or method) based on ..." Answer: operant conditioning. It examines the antecedents, the behaviour and above all the consequences that maintain it, which is Skinner's three-term contingency. Classical conditioning explains how a fear may be acquired; humanistic and psychodynamic principles have nothing to do with the ABC. Do not confuse the behavioural ABC (Antecedent, Behaviour, Consequence) with Albert Ellis's ABC of rational emotive behaviour therapy (Activating event, Belief, Consequence, Section 5.2): in Ellis's model the B is a belief and the C is the emotional consequence of that belief, not a reinforcer that follows the behaviour. SORKC is Kanfer and Saslow's (1969, building on their 1965 behavioural analysis and on Lindsley's 1964 contingency term): Stimulus, Organism, Response, Kontingency (contingency), Consequence. SORC is the four-term version without the K. Excesses, deficits and assets are also Kanfer and Saslow's.

The behavioural interview and the target behaviour

Most functional analyses begin, as most assessments do, with an interview. The behavioural interview uses the microskills of Section 2.2 but points them at a narrower goal: to identify the problem behaviours, specify them precisely, find their antecedents and consequences, note the client's assets, agree on goals and plan the rest of the assessment. Its style is concrete, specific and present-focused. Where a traditional interviewer might ask "Why do you think you avoid your father?", a behavioural interviewer asks "When did this last happen? Where were you? What happened just before? What did you do? What happened next? How did you feel ten minutes later, and the next morning?" The question "why" invites a theory; the questions "what, when, where, with whom and how often" invite data.

The single most useful request in a behavioural interview is "Take me through the last time it happened, step by step." General statements ("I always fall apart when he calls") are summaries the client has already interpreted; a recent, specific episode can be analysed. Here is how part of Ravi's behavioural interview went.

From Ravi's behavioural interview

Clinician: Take me through last Sunday evening, from the moment the phone rang. Ravi: It was Appa. He asked if I had started the new mock tests. Then he said Mahesh has joined the branch in Hassan. Clinician: What did you do then? Ravi: I said the signal was bad and cut the call. Clinician: And straight after? Ravi: I felt ... lighter, for a few minutes. Then I lay down. I didn't eat dinner. I watched videos till midnight. Clinician: Have there been evenings when he called and you didn't cut the call? Ravi: When Amma is on the line too. She talks about the village, and he doesn't bring up the exam.

Notice how much the last question bought. Asking about the situations in which the problem does not occur is as informative as asking about those in which it does: here it reveals that the mother's presence changes the antecedent, which is a clue for treatment.

Defining the target. The output of the interview is a short list of target behaviours (Section 4.1), each with an operational definition: a description of the behaviour in terms so observable and specific that two people watching independently would agree on when it starts, when it stops and whether it occurred. "Ravi is withdrawn" is not an operational definition; neither are "lazy", "aggressive", "low self-esteem" or "attention-seeking", which are traits or inferences. "Ravi watches videos on his phone in bed after 9 p.m." and "Ravi ends a phone call within two minutes of a mention of examinations" are. The behaviour analyst Ogden R. Lindsley (1922 to 2004) offered a famous rule of thumb, the dead man test: if a dead man can do it, it is not a behaviour. "Not leaving his room" and "lying in bed" fail the test (a corpse can do both); "watching videos on his phone in bed after 9 p.m." passes.

Which targets come first? Clinicians usually choose behaviours that are dangerous to the client or others before anything else (self-harm, violence), then behaviours that most impair daily functioning or most distress the client, then those most likely to change quickly and bring natural rewards that will keep the improvement going. A target is also defined by the dimension that matters. Ravi's problem is not that he ends phone calls (everyone does) but how quickly he ends them; a smoker's problem is how many cigarettes; a child's tantrum may matter most for how long it lasts. The main dimensions are frequency, duration, latency and intensity, and each has its own recording method, described later in this section.

Exam trap

A behavioural interview asks what, when, where, with whom and how often, and asks for a specific recent episode; it avoids "why" questions. Trait words ("lazy", "aggressive", "insecure") are not target behaviours until they are operationally defined. The dead man test (Lindsley) rejects any "behaviour" a dead man could perform, such as "not talking" or "staying still".

Self-monitoring and its reactivity

Many of the behaviours that matter most happen when nobody is watching. No observer can follow Ravi into his room at midnight, count the urges a person with bulimia resists, or sit beside an anxious student as worries crowd in before sleep. For these the client must become his own observer. Self-monitoring is the systematic observation and recording, by the client, of his own behaviour, thoughts, feelings or physiological states, usually together with their antecedents and consequences, as they occur.

The methods range from the simple to the sophisticated. A diary or log records each episode with its time, situation and outcome; an ABC diary uses three columns, and the cognitive therapist's thought record (Section 5.4) is a diary with columns for situations, automatic thoughts and emotions. A counter (a tally on a card, a click counter, beads moved from one pocket to the other) suits high-frequency behaviours such as hair-pulling or nail-biting. An activity schedule divides each day into hours and records what the client did, often with ratings of mastery and pleasure on a scale of 0 to 10. A sleep diary records bedtime, time to fall asleep, awakenings and rising time. Today many records are kept on mobile phones that prompt the client at random or fixed times during the day, a method that Arthur Stone and Saul Shiffman in 1994 named ecological momentary assessment (EMA): ecological because it happens in the person's real environment, momentary because it records the present moment rather than asking him to remember.

Why not simply ask at the next session? Because memory is a poor recorder. Asked "How many panic attacks did you have this month?", a client answers from his current mood and from the most vivid episodes; asked how depressed he was last week, a depressed client remembers the worst day. Recording at the time avoids most of this retrospective bias, gives access to private events that no observer can see, costs almost nothing and makes the client an active partner in his own treatment.

Self-monitoring has two well-known weaknesses. The first is accuracy. Clients forget to record, record selectively, or fill in the whole week's diary in the waiting room. That last habit is more common than clinicians like to think. In a study published in 2002, Stone, Shiffman and their colleagues gave patients with chronic pain paper diaries fitted, without their knowledge, with a sensor that recorded when the diary was opened. The patients reported completing about 90 percent of their entries on time; the sensor showed actual compliance of about 11 percent, and on almost a third of the days (32 percent) the diary binder had never been opened, although the entries for those days were filled in. Electronic diaries that time-stamp each entry largely solved the problem. Accuracy improves when the recording is simple, immediate, practised in the session, and checked occasionally by another person.

The second feature, reactivity of self-monitoring, is a weakness for measurement and a strength for treatment. Section 4.1 introduced reactivity as the change in a behaviour produced by the act of observing it. When people begin to record their own behaviour, it tends to move in the desired direction: smokers who count cigarettes smoke fewer, students who record study time study more, and dieters who record every meal eat less. Research in the 1970s, much of it by Alan E. Kazdin and by Rosemery O. Nelson, identified the factors that make self-monitoring more reactive: the valence of the behaviour (desirable behaviours tend to increase and undesirable ones to decrease), the client's motivation to change, the timing of the recording (recording an urge before acting on it changes the behaviour more than recording it afterwards), the obtrusiveness of the device (a counter worn on the wrist is a constant reminder), and whether the client has a goal and receives feedback. Two further facts matter. Reactive effects are frequently short-lived unless other treatment follows. And reactivity and accuracy are independent: an inaccurate record can still change behaviour, and an accurate one need not.

For Ravi the clinician asked for a simple hourly activity and mood record for one week: what he was doing, with whom, and his mood from 0 to 10. The record showed what the interview had only suggested. His mood was lowest (2 out of 10) in the late evening in bed with his phone and highest (6) during a single morning when he walked to the market with his roommate. He had studied for a total of 40 minutes in the week. He also noticed, without being told, that the evenings he spent in bed were his worst, and on the sixth day he went for a walk instead: reactivity at work, and a first step in treatment.

Clinical link

Adapt the record to the client. A client who cannot read and write well can use pictures (a smiling, neutral or sad face for mood), coloured stickers, beads or a voice note on the phone; a child can use a sticker chart; an older adult may prefer a large-print card with three boxes a day. A record that is too long will not be kept. Always look at the record at the start of the next session and use it: a client whose diary is never read soon stops keeping one.

Exam trap

When self-recording changes the behaviour being recorded, the term is reactivity. Its usual direction is therapeutic: desirable behaviours rise and undesirable ones fall. It is greater when the behaviour is valued, the client is motivated, recording happens before the behaviour, and the device is obtrusive. Reactivity is not the same as inaccuracy. Ecological momentary assessment (Stone and Shiffman, 1994) is real-time, repeated self-monitoring in the natural environment, usually by phone.

Direct observation: naturalistic, analogue and participant

Self-report, even in a diary, is still the client's account. The most direct way to know what a person does is to watch. Behavioural observation is the systematic recording of a person's behaviour by an observer, using predefined categories and recording rules. "Systematic" is the important word. Every parent and teacher observes children all day; what turns watching into assessment is a written definition of what counts as the behaviour, a rule for when and how to record it, and some check that the record can be trusted. Observation is used most with children, with people who cannot give a good account of themselves (people with intellectual disability, dementia or severe psychosis on a ward) and with problems that show themselves in interaction, such as a family's quarrels or a couple's arguments.

Observations differ in where they take place and in who does the observing.

Naturalistic observation records behaviour in the setting where it naturally occurs (home, classroom, playground, ward or workplace) without arranging anything. Its great strength is ecological validity: what is seen is the behaviour the referral is about, in the context that maintains it. The psychologist Gerald Patterson and his colleagues at the Oregon Social Learning Center built much of the modern science of child conduct problems on observers who sat in families' homes in the busy hour before the evening meal and coded every exchange between parent and child, which is how the escalating "coercive" cycles of nagging, defiance and giving in were first documented. The costs of naturalistic observation are equally real. It is expensive and slow, the behaviour of interest may not occur while the observer is there (a child who hits his sister twice a week may not do it during a two-hour visit), the setting cannot be controlled, and the observer's presence changes what happens, a problem taken up below.

Analogue observation (also called controlled or structured observation) records behaviour in a situation that the clinician sets up to resemble the real one, usually in the clinic or laboratory. A mother and child may be observed through a one-way mirror in a clinic playroom while the mother is asked, in turn, to let the child lead the play, to direct the play and to get the child to tidy up. A socially anxious student may be asked to start a conversation with a stranger who is in fact a trained assistant. Analogue methods make rare behaviours occur on demand, standardise the situation so that clients can be compared and progress measured, and save time. Their weakness is the mirror image of naturalistic observation's strength: behaviour in a simulation may not generalise to life. A man who can refuse an unreasonable request from an assistant in a clinic may still be unable to refuse his elder brother at home.

Key study: the behavioural avoidance test (Lang and Lazovik, 1963)

How do you measure a phobia objectively? In a study of systematic desensitisation published in 1963, Peter J. Lang and A. David Lazovik recruited university students who were intensely afraid of snakes and gave them a simple test. A harmless snake was placed in a glass case at the end of a room. Each student was asked to walk towards it and go as far as he or she could: approach the case, look at the snake, touch it and, finally, pick it up. The score was simply how far along that sequence the student got. Students treated with desensitisation came closer after treatment than untreated controls, and the improvement held at follow-up.

The behavioural avoidance test (BAT) became the standard analogue measure of phobic avoidance, adapted for spiders, dogs, heights, lifts, injections and crowded buses. Its lesson is the lesson of behavioural assessment: measure the behaviour you want to change, in a situation that samples it.

Participant observation is observation by someone who is a natural part of the setting and records the behaviour while carrying on with his ordinary role: a parent who notes each tantrum on a chart on the fridge, a teacher who tallies a pupil's out-of-seat episodes, a nurse who records a patient's social contacts on the ward, a husband who records his wife's checking rituals. (The term has a related meaning in anthropology and qualitative research, where the researcher joins a community and takes part in its life in order to study it; Sullivan's "participant observer" in Section 2.1 is a third, looser use.) Participant observers are cheap, present when the behaviour actually occurs, and less reactive than a stranger with a clipboard, because the people observed are used to them. They are also untrained, busy with their own duties, and personally involved: a mother exhausted by her son's tantrums may record them more faithfully than his good behaviour. In non-participant observation, by contrast, an outside observer records the behaviour and takes no part in the activity.

Observation can also be overt (the people observed know they are being watched) or covert (they do not, as with a concealed camera). Covert observation reduces reactivity but raises obvious ethical problems; in clinical work consent is the rule, and reactivity is managed in other ways.

Indian context

In India much behavioural observation is necessarily participant observation. A child psychologist in a Mysuru clinic will rarely be able to visit a government school, but the class teacher can keep a simple tally sheet, and a grandmother who looks after the child every afternoon may be the best observer of all. Training these observers takes ten minutes and pays for itself: show them the operational definition, practise on one or two examples in the clinic, give them a sheet with large boxes, and ask them to record only one or two behaviours. In inpatient units, nurses' observation charts (sleep charts, ward behaviour ratings) are the participant observation of the ward.

Exam trap

Naturalistic observation happens in the real setting with nothing arranged (high ecological validity, low control). Analogue (controlled) observation happens in a simulated setting such as a clinic playroom, a role play or the behavioural avoidance test (high control, uncertain generalisation). In participant observation the observer is part of the setting (parent, teacher, nurse); in non-participant observation he is an outsider. The BAT is Lang and Lazovik's snake test (1963), an analogue measure of avoidance.

Recording methods: event, duration, latency, interval and time sampling

Deciding where and by whom a behaviour will be observed is half the plan. The other half is deciding what exactly the observer will write down. Recording methods fall into two families. Continuous methods capture every occurrence during the observation period; discontinuous methods divide the period into intervals or moments and record only a sample, trading completeness for practicality. To see how much the choice matters, follow one child through all of them.

Meena is eight and in Class 3 at a school in Mysuru. Her teacher complains that she "never sits still". The operational definition agreed with the teacher is: out of seat, meaning that Meena's bottom has left the chair or bench for more than two seconds without permission. A trained observer sits at the back of the class.

The first and most open method is narrative recording, also called anecdotal or ABC recording: the observer writes a running account of what happens just before each episode, what Meena does and what follows. It is the natural tool of descriptive functional assessment and of the first visit, when the clinician does not yet know what to count. Once the target is clear, more precise methods take over.

Event recording (frequency recording; observation that records every occurrence of a defined event in this way is also called event sampling, as opposed to the time sampling described below) counts each occurrence of the behaviour: Meena left her seat four times in the observation period. Dividing the count by the time gives the rate (four times in 100 seconds, or, over a whole period, perhaps 12 times an hour), which allows observations of different lengths to be compared. Event recording is ideal for discrete behaviours with a clear beginning and end that occur at a countable rate: hitting, swearing, questions asked, cigarettes smoked, panic attacks. It suits poorly behaviours that occur so fast that counting becomes impossible (rocking, finger-tapping) and behaviours that last a long time, where one "event" may be a ten-second episode or a twenty-minute one.

Duration recording measures how long the behaviour lasts, either per episode or in total: Meena was out of her seat for 48 seconds in all. Duration is the right measure when the length of the behaviour is the problem (crying, tantrums, time spent in the toilet performing rituals) or the goal (time on task, minutes of study, time spent talking to others on a ward). It needs a clear start and stop and an observer who is free to watch with a stopwatch.

Latency recording measures the time between a cue and the start of the behaviour: Meena first left her seat three seconds after the teacher said "Open your books". Latency matters for compliance ("How long after I ask does he start?"), for social behaviour (how long a socially anxious client takes to respond to a question) and for sleep (sleep-onset latency, the time from lights-out to falling asleep). The related interresponse time is the time between the end of one episode and the start of the next.

Two further dimensions complete the set. Intensity (or magnitude) records the force or severity of the behaviour: the loudness of a scream, the damage done in a tantrum, or the client's own rating of anxiety on a 0 to 100 scale of subjective units of distress (SUDS, Section 4.2). Permanent product recording measures the lasting result of a behaviour rather than the behaviour itself: worksheets completed, cigarette butts in the ashtray, kilograms lost, hairs collected after a trichotillomania session.

Interval recording. Continuous methods are exhausting. An observer cannot keep a stopwatch on one child for an hour, let alone on four. Interval recording solves the problem by dividing the observation period into short, equal intervals (often 10 seconds, sometimes with a few seconds after each for writing) and recording, for each interval, only whether the behaviour occurred. The result is the percentage of intervals scored. There are two versions, and their errors run in opposite directions.

  • In whole-interval recording an interval is scored only if the behaviour continued throughout the entire interval. A brief episode inside an interval is not scored, and neither is a long episode that starts a few seconds after the interval began. Whole-interval recording therefore underestimates the true occurrence of the behaviour. It is used for behaviours that the clinician wants to increase and that should be sustained, such as on-task behaviour or cooperative play, because it gives credit only for sustained performance.
  • In partial-interval recording an interval is scored if the behaviour occurred at any time during it, however briefly. A four-second episode earns the whole ten-second interval. Partial-interval recording therefore overestimates the true occurrence. It is used for behaviours the clinician wants to decrease, such as hitting or calling out, because a reduction in the score is then a conservative sign of real improvement, and because even a brief occurrence counts.

Momentary time sampling scores an interval only if the behaviour is occurring at the exact moment the interval ends: the observer looks up at the beep and asks, "Is Meena out of her seat right now?" Its errors are not systematically in one direction, and with enough sampled moments its estimate approaches the true proportion of time the behaviour occupies, which makes it the best of the discontinuous methods for continuous, long-lasting behaviours. It misses brief behaviours almost entirely. Because the observer need only glance at the moment of sampling, one observer can sample several children in turn, and the group version, in which the observer counts how many members of a group are engaged in an activity at each sampled moment, is called the planned activity check (PLACHECK). (Be warned that some textbooks use "time sampling" loosely for all the interval methods; strictly, it means momentary sampling.)

Figure 2.10 shows all three discontinuous methods applied to the same 100 seconds of Meena's morning.

Table 2.26 brings the methods together.

Table 2.26 Methods of recording observed behaviour.
MethodWhat is recordedBest forMain error or limit
Narrative (ABC)Running account of A, B and CFirst visits; generating hypothesesSubjective; hard to quantify
Event (frequency)Number of occurrences; rateDiscrete behaviours with clear start and endPoor for very fast or long-lasting behaviours
DurationHow long the behaviour lastsContinuous behaviours: crying, time on taskNeeds clear start and stop; demanding
LatencyTime from cue to start of behaviourCompliance; sleep onsetNeeds a clear cue
IntensityForce or severity, often ratedTantrums; anxiety (SUDS)Subjective without an instrument
Permanent productLasting result of the behaviourWork completed; weight; cigarette buttsDoes not show how the product arose
Whole-intervalBehaviour present throughout the intervalBehaviours to increase (on-task)Underestimates
Partial-intervalBehaviour present at any time in the intervalBehaviours to decrease (hitting)Overestimates
Momentary time samplingBehaviour present at the instant the interval endsLong, continuous behaviours; several clientsMisses brief behaviours
Exam trap

Whole-interval recording underestimates and suits behaviours you want to increase; partial-interval recording overestimates and suits behaviours you want to decrease. Momentary time sampling looks only at the instant the interval ends. Event recording is frequency (how often), duration is how long, latency is how soon after the cue. A stem asking for the method that "counts the number of times a discrete behaviour occurs" wants event (frequency) recording; one asking about "time from the instruction to the start of the behaviour" wants latency.

Can the observations be trusted? Agreement and the errors of observation

An observation is only as good as the observer. How do we know that what one observer recorded is what happened? The first check is whether a second, independent observer, watching the same behaviour at the same time with the same definitions, records the same thing. The degree to which they do is inter-observer agreement (IOA), also called inter-observer or inter-rater reliability. It is the main form of reliability in behavioural observation, the counterpart of test-retest and internal-consistency reliability for tests (Assessment book).

There are several ways to calculate it, and they do not give the same answer. Suppose a second observer scored Meena's ten partial intervals at the same time as the first and missed the brief episode at 32 seconds, so that her record read 1, 1, 0, 0, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 0.

  • Interval-by-interval agreement compares the two records interval by interval: agreements divided by agreements plus disagreements, times 100. The observers agree on 9 of the 10 intervals, so agreement is 9 / (9 + 1) × 100 = 90 percent.
  • Total count agreement compares only the totals: the smaller count divided by the larger, times 100. If the first observer counted four episodes by event recording and the second counted three, agreement is 3 / 4 × 100 = 75 percent. Total count agreement is easy to compute but can hide disagreement: two observers who both count four episodes agree perfectly even if they counted different ones.
  • Occurrence agreement counts only the intervals in which at least one observer scored the behaviour. It is used for rare behaviours, because when a behaviour seldom occurs two observers can agree on almost every interval simply by both scoring nothing, which inflates the ordinary index.

By convention agreement should be at least 80 percent, and 90 percent or more is preferred. Simple percentage agreement does not correct for the agreement two observers would reach by chance alone, which is high when a behaviour is very common or very rare. Cohen's kappa does, and it is described in Section 2.10.

Notice that agreement is not the same as accuracy. Two observers can agree perfectly and both be wrong, for example if they were trained together and share the same misunderstanding of the definition. Accuracy is the agreement between an observer's record and an incontestable standard, such as a videotape coded slowly and repeatedly by an expert. Research programmes therefore check observers against such criterion recordings as well as against each other.

What goes wrong? The errors of observation fall into two groups: changes in the people observed, and changes in the observers.

Reactivity of the observed. People behave differently when they know they are being watched. Children are better behaved when a stranger sits at the back of the class; parents are more patient when an observer sits in their living room; staff on a ward are more attentive on the day of an inspection. This is reactivity again (Section 4.1 and the self-monitoring subsection above), now produced by an outside observer. Its best-known name comes from industry. Between 1924 and 1932 researchers at the Western Electric Company's Hawthorne Works near Chicago varied the lighting, breaks and hours of factory workers, and productivity seemed to rise whatever they changed, apparently because the workers knew they were being studied. The sociologist Henry Landsberger named this the Hawthorne effect in 1958 (later reanalyses have questioned how large the effect in the original studies really was). Clinicians reduce reactivity by letting the people observed get used to the observer before recording begins (a habituation period), by making the observer as unobtrusive as possible, by using participant observers or video, and by observing over several sessions so that the novelty wears off.

Observer drift is a gradual, usually unnoticed change in the way an observer applies the definitions over time. At the start of a study an observer trained to code "aggression" may score only hitting and kicking; three weeks later she may be scoring pushing and loud teasing too, without anyone having changed the definition. Drift is dangerous because it looks like change in the client. It is especially hard to detect when two observers who work together drift together, so that their agreement stays high while both move away from the definition (sometimes called consensual drift). The remedies are regular retraining, periodic checks against criterion recordings, and rotating observer pairs.

Observer expectancy bias is the tendency to see what one expects to see. An observer who knows that a child has just started treatment, or who has been told that a treatment works, may record improvement that has not happened. The classic demonstration is described in the box below. A related and slightly surprising finding is that observers are more careful when they know their agreement is being checked: in a study published in 1970, John Reid found that observers' accuracy (their agreement with a criterion record) fell sharply when they believed they were no longer being checked. The remedy is to make checks unannounced and frequent.

Key study: expectation and observation (Kent, O'Leary, Diament and Dietz, 1974)

Ronald Kent, K. Daniel O'Leary and their colleagues trained observers to code disruptive classroom behaviour from videotapes. Half the observers were told that the children in the tapes were receiving a treatment expected to reduce their disruptive behaviour; the other half were told that no change was expected. In fact both groups watched the same tapes. When the observers recorded specific, operationally defined behaviours, their records did not differ: expectation did not bias the behavioural codes. But when they were asked for their overall impression, the observers who expected improvement reported that the children had improved.

The lesson is precise and practical. Expectancy bias contaminates global impressions far more than it contaminates counts of specifically defined behaviours, which is one of the strongest arguments for operational definitions. Keeping observers blind to the phase of treatment protects both.

Observers also tire, and their accuracy falls in long sessions and with complicated coding systems that ask them to track many categories at once; short sessions, few categories and breaks help. And all the rater errors of Section 2.2 apply to observers too: a halo effect may lead an observer who finds a child charming to under-record his misbehaviour. Table 2.27 summarises the errors and their remedies.

Table 2.27 Errors in behavioural observation and how to reduce them.
ErrorWhat happensRemedy
Reactivity (Hawthorne effect)The people observed change because they know they are watchedHabituation period; unobtrusive or participant observers; video
Observer driftObservers gradually change how they apply the definitionsRegular retraining; checks against criterion recordings; rotate pairs
Expectancy biasObservers record what they expect, especially in global ratingsKeep observers blind; record specific behaviours, not impressions
Reactivity of agreement checksObservers are more careful only when they know they are checkedFrequent, unannounced checks
Fatigue and complex codesAccuracy falls over long sessions and many categoriesShort sessions; few categories; breaks
Halo and other rater errorsA global impression colours specific ratingsOperational definitions; trained observers
Exam trap

Keep the three classic errors apart. Reactivity is a change in the person observed caused by being observed (the Hawthorne effect, named by Landsberger in 1958). Observer drift is a gradual change in the observer's use of the definitions over time. Expectancy bias is the observer recording what he expects; Kent and colleagues (1974) showed it affects global impressions more than specific behaviour codes. Inter-observer agreement of 80 percent is the usual minimum, and agreement is not the same as accuracy.

Role play and psychophysiological measures

Some behaviours are hard to observe because they happen in private moments that no observer can reach: refusing a request from a boss, asking a girl for coffee, answering a hostile question at a job interview, saying no to a friend who offers a drink. For these the clinician can bring a sample of the situation into the room. In a role play assessment the client acts out a problem situation with the clinician or a trained assistant playing the other person, and the clinician observes and rates his behaviour. Role play is therefore a form of analogue observation, and it is the standard way of assessing social skills, assertiveness, anger control and interview performance.

Role plays range from the informal ("Let's try it. I'll be your father. The phone rings ...") to standardised tests. In the Behavioral Assertiveness Test described by Richard Eisler, Peter Miller and Michel Hersen in 1973, a narrator describes a series of everyday scenes (someone pushes ahead of you in a queue; a waiter brings the wrong order), an assistant delivers a prompt, and the client's reply is recorded and later rated on specific components: eye contact, duration of the reply, latency, loudness, fluency, compliance with the unreasonable request and requests for new behaviour. Notice that the components are themselves operationally defined behaviours, scored with the dimensions you have just met.

Role play has the analogue method's strengths (it is quick and controlled and can be repeated to measure change) and its weakness: people do not always behave in a role play as they do in life, and the evidence that role-play performance predicts real-world behaviour is mixed. The assessment is also only the beginning. The same procedure, with feedback, modelling and repetition, becomes behaviour rehearsal, one of the main techniques of social skills and assertiveness training (Chapter 4), and role play has older roots in Moreno's psychodrama (Section 2.3). For Ravi, a two-minute role play of his father's Sunday call showed the clinician more than any description: Ravi's voice dropped, his eyes went to the floor, and within forty seconds he was looking for a way to end the call.

Three response systems. Why should a behaviour therapist care about the body? Because fear, anger and craving are not only things people say and do. In an influential 1968 chapter, Peter Lang argued that an emotion such as fear is expressed in three partly independent response systems: the verbal-cognitive (what the person says he feels and thinks), the overt behavioural or motor (what he does, such as avoiding or escaping) and the physiological (what his body does: heart rate, sweating, breathing, muscle tension). The three are only loosely coupled. A man may avoid lifts while insisting that he is not afraid of them; a student may say she is terrified of a presentation and give it fluently, with her heart pounding. Stanley Rachman and Ray Hodgson (1974) called this lack of correspondence desynchrony, and showed that the systems can also change at different rates in treatment: avoidance may fall before reported fear does, or heart rate may settle before the client feels any calmer. The practical conclusion is the multimethod principle met at the start of this section, now with a reason behind it: measure all three systems, because improvement in one does not guarantee improvement in the others.

Psychophysiological assessment measures the physiological system directly. The commonest measures are heart rate and blood pressure; electrodermal activity (skin conductance, which rises with sweating and arousal); muscle tension recorded by electromyography (EMG), used in assessing tension headache and in biofeedback; respiration; brain electrical activity (EEG); and, in sleep laboratories, polysomnography, which records brain waves, eye movements, muscle tone and breathing through the night. Specialised measures include penile plethysmography, which records changes in penile blood volume in response to sexual stimuli and is used in the assessment of sexual dysfunction and of sexual offenders, and hormonal measures such as salivary cortisol in stress research. These measures are objective and hard to fake, but they are expensive, sensitive to movement and to the laboratory itself, and they vary greatly between individuals, so a single reading means little without a baseline. Their technology belongs to the Physiological Psychology and Assessment books, and their main therapeutic use, biofeedback, is described in Section 9.4.

Exam trap

Lang's (1968) three response systems are the verbal-cognitive, the overt behavioural (motor) and the physiological. Their lack of agreement is desynchrony (Rachman and Hodgson, 1974). A role play is an analogue observation, not a naturalistic one, and its validity depends on whether the behaviour generalises to real life. Electrodermal activity (skin conductance) measures sweating and arousal; EMG measures muscle tension; polysomnography records sleep.

Step back and look at what behavioural assessment has given Ravi's clinician. It has turned "he barely leaves his room" into a handful of defined behaviours, each with a baseline; it has found what triggers them and what keeps them going; it has measured how his mood moves through the day; and it has shown, in a role play, what happens to him when his father calls. The SORKC analysis in Figure 2.9 is already a small explanation of his problem: a hypothesis about why he does what he does. The next step is to build that kind of explanation for the whole person and the whole problem. That is case formulation.

2.9 Case FormulationLower yield · 1 Q

By now Ravi's file is thick. It holds an intake note, a case history of several pages, a genogram, a mental status examination, a risk assessment, a week of activity and mood records and a functional analysis of his evenings. Every page is accurate. And yet, laid side by side, the pages do not tell the clinician what to do on Monday morning. A pile of facts is not an understanding. Somewhere the facts have to be turned into an explanation: an account of why this young man, with this history, became unwell in this way at this time, why he has not recovered on his own, and what is most likely to help. That explanation is the case formulation, and making it is the point at which assessment becomes treatment.

What a formulation is, and what it is for

The most widely quoted definition comes from the American psychologist Tracy D. Eells, editor of the Handbook of Psychotherapy Case Formulation (1997): a psychotherapy case formulation is a hypothesis about the causes, precipitants and maintaining influences of a person's psychological, interpersonal and behavioural problems. Each word of that definition does work, so take them one at a time. A formulation is a hypothesis: tentative, testable and revisable, held only until the evidence suggests a better one. It is explanatory: it says not just what the problems are but why they arose and why they persist. It is individual: it is about this person, not about people with depression in general. And it covers causes, precipitants and maintaining influences, the long-standing vulnerabilities, the recent triggers and the current processes that keep the problem going.

A case formulation (called a case conceptualisation in much of the cognitive-behavioural literature; the two terms mean the same thing) can therefore be defined as an individualised, theory-informed explanation of a client's difficulties that links his history, his current circumstances and his problems into a coherent account, and from which a plan of treatment follows. In Britain the psychologists Lucy Johnstone and Rudi Dallos, in Formulation in Psychology and Psychotherapy (2006), emphasised that formulation is both an event (a written summary at a particular point, such as the end of assessment) and a process (an understanding developed with the client and revised throughout therapy). The British Psychological Society's Division of Clinical Psychology, which regards formulation as one of the core skills of the clinical psychologist, published good practice guidelines on its use in 2011.

Why go to the trouble? A good formulation does several jobs at once.

  • It integrates information from every source (interview, history, MSE, tests, observation) into one account, and in doing so shows which pieces of information matter and which do not.
  • It guides treatment: it tells the clinician which problems to tackle first and which interventions fit, and it explains why a standard treatment might need to be adapted for this person.
  • It predicts difficulties: a formulation that identifies perfectionism predicts that the client will struggle with "imperfect" homework; one that identifies a fear of being judged predicts that he may hide setbacks from the therapist.
  • It explains failure: when treatment stalls, the formulation is the first thing to revisit, because a treatment aimed at the wrong maintaining factor will not work however skilfully it is delivered.
  • It helps the client: a shared formulation turns a frightening, shameful experience into an understandable one ("This is how it happened, and this is what keeps it going"), which reduces self-blame and gives a sense that the problem can be changed.
  • It helps the team: in a hospital or clinic, a written formulation lets the psychiatrist, the nurse, the social worker and the psychologist work from the same understanding.

Two things a formulation is not are worth saying at once. It is not a diagnosis: a diagnosis classifies the problem under a category in a manual, whereas a formulation explains it in this person (the comparison is developed later in this section). And it is not a summary: restating the history in fewer words explains nothing. The test of a formulation is whether it answers the question "why?" in a way that tells you what to do.

Exam trap

The standard definition is Eells's (1997): a formulation is a hypothesis about the causes, precipitants and maintaining influences of a person's problems. It is explanatory, individual, tentative and linked to treatment; a shortened retelling of the history is a summary, not a formulation. Case conceptualisation and case formulation are synonyms. Johnstone and Dallos (2006) stress that formulation is both an event (a written summary) and a process (revised with the client throughout therapy). Its contrast with diagnosis is set out below.

The 5 Ps

How do you begin to build an explanation? The most widely taught framework, in psychiatry and clinical psychology training across the English-speaking world and in India, organises the factors under five headings, all beginning with P. The four explanatory Ps are usually credited to the "multiperspective" grid of the Canadian psychiatrist Priyanthy Weerasekera (in a paper of 1993 and a book of 1996), which elaborated a grid already in use in psychiatric residency training; adding the presenting problem gives the 5 Ps. Each P answers a different question.

  • The presenting problem answers what is the problem? It is the client's difficulties stated specifically, in his own words where possible and in clinical description, including what he wants to change. (It grows out of the chief complaints of Section 2.3, but it is wider: it includes problems the client has not complained of, such as the risk the clinician has identified.)
  • Predisposing factors answer why this person? They are the long-standing vulnerabilities that made him more likely than others to develop this problem: genetic loading, temperament, early experiences, attachment, personality traits, deep beliefs, and long-term social disadvantage.
  • Precipitating factors answer why now? They are the recent events or changes that triggered the onset or the present episode: a loss, a failure, a move, an illness, childbirth, starting or stopping a drug (Section 2.3 showed how to record them in the history of present illness).
  • Perpetuating factors (maintaining factors) answer why does it continue? They are the processes that keep the problem going now: avoidance, rumination, poor sleep, unhelpful beliefs, criticism at home, substance use, debt, isolation, a lack of access to treatment, and sometimes the unintended rewards of being ill.
  • Protective factors answer what helps? They are the strengths, resources and supports that reduce the risk, soften the impact and offer a way out: supportive relationships, skills, intelligence, faith, a job, previous recovery, the client's own motivation.

Two features of the 5 Ps are easily missed. The first is that the Ps are not equally useful for treatment. Predisposing factors explain, but they cannot be undone: nobody can change Ravi's genes or his childhood. Precipitating factors are usually in the past by the time the client is seen. Perpetuating factors are where treatment acts, because they operate now and can be changed, and protective factors are the levers it pulls. A formulation that lists six predisposing factors and one vague perpetuating factor has its emphasis upside down. The second feature is that the same fact can appear under more than one P. Ravi's father's comparisons were part of the climate in which he grew up (predisposing), sharpened into a trigger in the week of the result (precipitating) and continue every Sunday (perpetuating). That is not a mistake; it is the formulation showing how a pattern runs through a life.

If the scheme reminds you of the diathesis-stress idea you met with Beck's cognitive model (Section 5.3), it should: predisposing factors are the diathesis, precipitating factors the stress. The 5 Ps add what that idea leaves out, namely what keeps a disorder going once it has started and what protects against it. Table 2.28 applies the scheme to Ravi.

Table 2.28 Ravi's 5 Ps.
PQuestionRavi
PresentingWhat is the problem?Three months of low mood, lost interest, early waking, weight loss, guilt, hopelessness and withdrawal; stopped studying; thoughts of death and of suicide, without intent
PredisposingWhy this person?Maternal uncle's depression; perfectionistic, self-critical personality; worth measured by marks since childhood; a month of depression at 17
PrecipitatingWhy now?Third failure in the bank examination, in the week his cousin was selected; his father's comparisons since
PerpetuatingWhy does it continue?Avoidance and withdrawal (Figure 2.9); loss of rewarding activity; rumination at night; poor sleep; father's criticism; guilt over the pledged gold
