ProtectiveWhat helps?Warm mother who brought him; sister; faith; good academic and work record; no substance use; earlier episode recovered; came for help

A table is a worksheet, not a formulation. The formulation proper is written as a short narrative in which the factors are connected by "because", "so" and "which": the connections are the explanation. Here is how the 5 Ps read when they are written up.

Ravi's 5 Ps, written as a formulation

Ravi is a 24-year-old man who presents with a three-month history of low mood, loss of interest, early morning waking, weight loss, guilt, hopelessness and withdrawal, with daily thoughts that he would be better off dead and occasional thoughts of suicide, without intent or plan. He may have been vulnerable to depression because of a family history of the illness, a perfectionistic and self-critical personality, and a childhood in which his worth in the family seemed to depend on his examination results; he had a brief depressive reaction to an academic setback at 17. The present episode was precipitated by his third failure in the bank examination, made more painful by his cousin's success in the same week and by his father's comparisons. It is maintained by a cycle in which he escapes shame by withdrawing to his room and his phone, which brings short-term relief but removes him from study, cricket, family and friends, worsens his sleep and gives him more time to ruminate on having failed his parents; his father's continued criticism and the family's debt keep the cycle supplied. In his favour are a warm and engaged mother, his intelligence and conscientiousness, a good record of study and work, his faith, the absence of substance use, his recovery from the earlier episode and his willingness to come for help.

Exam trap

Predisposing = why this person (long-standing vulnerability). Precipitating = why now (the recent trigger). Perpetuating = why it continues (maintaining factors, the main target of treatment). Protective = what helps (strengths and supports). Presenting = the problem itself. A stem describing "factors that maintain the problem" wants perpetuating; one describing "a stressful event shortly before onset" wants precipitating. Do not confuse perpetuating (operating now) with predisposing (a vulnerability from the past): both are "causes", but only the first is the main target of treatment.

The biopsychosocial formulation

The 5 Ps organise the factors in time: long ago, recently, now. A second question is at what level each factor operates. The answer comes from the biopsychosocial model (its place among the models of psychopathology is covered in Chapter 1, and Section 9.1 applies it to physical illness) that the American psychiatrist George L. Engel (1913 to 1999) proposed in a 1977 paper in Science, "The need for a new medical model": biological, psychological and social systems are nested inside one another, so that illness is never only a matter of the body, or only of the mind, or only of circumstances, and a change at one level ripples into the others. Applied to formulation, the model becomes a simple and powerful device. Cross the four explanatory Ps with the three levels and you get a grid of twelve cells, each a question: what are the biological predisposing factors? the psychological precipitants? the social perpetuating factors? A biopsychosocial formulation is an explanation of the client's difficulties that fills in that grid and then shows how the levels interact. Table 2.29 is Ravi's.

Table 2.29 Ravi's biopsychosocial formulation grid.
FactorBiologicalPsychologicalSocial
PredisposingDepression in maternal uncle (genetic loading)Perfectionism, self-criticism; depressive month at 17Worth measured by marks; father's comparisons
PrecipitatingNone found yet: check thyroid and haemoglobinThe meaning of the failure: "I have failed them"Third failure; cousin selected the same week
PerpetuatingBroken sleep, poor appetite, weight lossAvoidance, rumination, hopelessnessFather's criticism; debt on pledged gold; living away from home
ProtectiveGood physical health; no alcohol or tobaccoIntelligent, conscientious; earlier episode recoveredWarm mother; sister; faith; supportive roommate

The grid earns its place in three ways. First, it exposes gaps. The empty-looking cell for biological precipitants is not a statement that there are none; it is a reminder to check, because tiredness and weight loss in a young man can come from hypothyroidism, anaemia or an infection as easily as from depression (Section 2.3 flagged this). A grid that is full in the psychological column and empty in the other two usually means the clinician has looked only where his training points.

Second, it shows how the levels feed each other, which is what turns a list into an explanation. Ravi's broken sleep (biological) worsens his concentration and mood (psychological), which makes study impossible, which makes the next examination less likely to go well (social), which confirms his belief that he is a failure (psychological), which keeps him awake at 3 a.m. (biological). Draw that circle for the client and he sees, perhaps for the first time, that his depression is not simply a weakness of character.

Third, every cell suggests an intervention at its own level. At the biological level: investigations, a review by a psychiatrist to consider an antidepressant (the severity, the biological symptoms and the thoughts of death all point that way), and a regular sleep routine. At the psychological level: the cognitive-behavioural work described in the next subsection. At the social level: a family session in which the father's comparisons can be discussed without blame, a conversation about the debt, a reconsideration of whether the bank examination is the only route to a respectable job, and a plan for Ravi to spend more time with his roommate and less alone.

The biopsychosocial model has its critics. The American psychiatrist S. Nassir Ghaemi, in The Rise and Fall of the Biopsychosocial Model (2010), argued that in practice it has often become a vague eclecticism that licenses a clinician to mention everything and commit to nothing, without any principle for deciding which level matters most in a given case. The criticism is fair of bad formulations, and it points to the answer: a good biopsychosocial formulation does not merely list factors at three levels but says which ones carry most weight in this person and how they interact.

Indian context

In Indian formulations the social column is usually the richest, and the most often neglected by trainees taught from Western textbooks. It includes the structure of the family (joint or nuclear, who decides), the family's honour (maryade, in Kannada) and the shame attached to failure, unemployment and mental illness, the pressure of competitive examinations and the coaching economy that has grown around them, debt, migration from village to town, marriage expectations, caste and gender, and the family's explanatory model, including any faith healer or temple already consulted. These are not background details. For many clients they are the precipitating and perpetuating factors.

Exam trap

The biopsychosocial model is George Engel's (1977, Science). It is not Adolf Meyer's psychobiology (Section 2.3), although Meyer's idea of the whole person in his life circumstances anticipated it. A biopsychosocial formulation crosses the predisposing, precipitating, perpetuating and protective factors with the biological, psychological and social levels. Its main criticism (Ghaemi) is that it can become an unprincipled eclectic list.

The cognitive-behavioural formulation

The 5 Ps and the biopsychosocial grid are frameworks: they tell you where to look, but not what mechanism links the factors. A cognitive-behavioural formulation supplies a mechanism, taken from cognitive and learning theory. Its pioneer as a formal method was the American psychologist Jacqueline B. Persons, whose Cognitive Therapy in Practice: A Case Formulation Approach (1989) argued that the therapist should treat each case as a hypothesis about the psychological mechanisms producing the client's problems, derive the treatment plan from that hypothesis, and test it as therapy proceeds, revising it when the client does not improve. Persons' formulation lists the problems, proposes the mechanisms (the beliefs and behaviours that produce them), identifies the precipitants and the origins of the mechanisms, and builds the treatment plan from them.

Two decades later Willem Kuyken, Christine A. Padesky and Robert Dudley, in Collaborative Case Conceptualization (2009), described formulation with the image of a crucible, a vessel in which the client's experience and the clinician's theory and research are combined and transformed, with collaborative empiricism (Section 5.4) providing the heat. They made three principles central. The formulation is built at increasing levels as therapy proceeds: first a descriptive level (the problems in cognitive-behavioural terms), then a cross-sectional explanatory level (the triggers and maintaining cycles of the problems now), then, if needed, a longitudinal explanatory level (the predisposing and protective factors in the client's history). It is built collaboratively, with the client as co-author. And it incorporates strengths, not just deficits, because strengths are what the client will use to recover.

At the cross-sectional level, the simplest tool is the five-part model that Padesky and Kathleen Mooney described in 1990, sometimes called the "hot cross bun" because of the shape in which it is drawn: the environment or situation, and four interacting parts of the person's response to it (thoughts, emotions, physical reactions and behaviours), each affecting the others. Ravi's version is quickly drawn. Situation: he opens his exam guide. Thought: "What's the point? I'll fail again." Emotion: hopelessness. Body: heaviness, fatigue. Behaviour: he shuts the book and lies down. And the behaviour feeds back: another day without study makes failure more likely and gives the thought fresh evidence. Notice that this cycle and the SORKC analysis of Figure 2.9 describe the same evenings from two angles, one emphasising the belief and the other the reinforcement. A good cognitive-behavioural formulation uses both.

For the longitudinal level, the standard tool is the cognitive conceptualisation diagram (CCD) of Judith S. Beck, Aaron Beck's daughter, set out in Cognitive Therapy: Basics and Beyond (1995; later editions are titled Cognitive Behavior Therapy: Basics and Beyond). It is built on Aaron Beck's three levels of cognition, which Section 5.3 and Figure 5.3 have already taught: core beliefs, intermediate beliefs (attitudes, rules and assumptions) and automatic thoughts. What the diagram adds is a way of tying these to one particular person's history and to his typical problem situations, on a single page. Its lower half is cross-sectional. For three typical situations in which the client became upset, it records the situation, the automatic thought, the meaning of the automatic thought to the client (often found by the downward arrow technique of Section 5.4), the emotion and the behaviour. Its upper half is longitudinal. It records the relevant childhood data (the experiences that help explain how the core belief arose), the core belief itself, the conditional assumptions, beliefs or rules that grew from it, and the compensatory strategies, the behaviours the client developed to cope with the core belief and to keep it from being activated. The diagram is filled in by working upwards from the situations and asking, at each step, what belief would make sense of the thought. Figure 2.11 is Ravi's.

Read the diagram from the top and Ravi's story acquires a mechanism. A boy whose family's pride rested on his marks came to believe that his worth lay in achievement; in Judith Beck's three families of negative core beliefs (Section 5.3) "I am a failure" is a helpless belief. For years his compensatory strategy, relentless hard work, kept that belief quiet, because it worked: he was the topper. The bank examinations are the first challenge that hard work has failed to overcome again and again (the setback at 17 was a single result), and three failures have broken the strategy and activated the belief. Now every situation that touches achievement or family (a phone call, a book, a photograph) produces an automatic thought that confirms it. This explains, in cognitive terms, both why Ravi was vulnerable and why the illness began when it did; the 5 Ps said the same thing in a less specific language.

The formulation then writes the treatment plan. Because Ravi is depressed, withdrawn and short of energy, the first step is behavioural: graded activity scheduling to restore reinforcement, using the activity record he has already kept. Next come thought records and behavioural experiments directed at the automatic thoughts and at the rule "I must never let my parents down" (what happens, for instance, if he tells his father plainly how he is feeling?). Work on the core belief itself, with a positive data log of evidence that his worth does not rest on results, comes last (Section 5.4). And the formulation predicts the obstacles: a perfectionist may treat homework as another examination to pass or fail, and a young man who believes he must never disappoint may hide his setbacks from the therapist. Forewarned, the therapist praises effort rather than results and asks regularly what has not gone well.

Exam trap

The cognitive conceptualisation diagram is Judith S. Beck's (1995), built on Aaron Beck's cognitive model. Its parts, from the top: relevant childhood data, core beliefs, conditional assumptions or rules, compensatory strategies; then, for each typical situation, the automatic thought, its meaning, the emotion and the behaviour. Compensatory strategies are behaviours (overworking, avoiding, seeking approval), not beliefs. Persons (1989) founded the case formulation approach to cognitive therapy; Kuyken, Padesky and Dudley (2009) gave the crucible metaphor and the three levels (descriptive, cross-sectional, longitudinal). The five-part ("hot cross bun") model is Padesky and Mooney (1990).

The psychodynamic formulation

The cognitive-behavioural formulation explains Ravi's depression through his beliefs and the behaviour that maintains them. A psychodynamic clinician listening to the same story hears something else: a son who has never been able to be angry with his father. A psychodynamic formulation explains the client's present difficulties in terms of enduring, largely unconscious patterns (conflicts between wishes and fears, the defences used against them, and internalised templates of self and others) that took shape in development and are now being repeated in his current life, and it predicts how those patterns will appear in the relationship with the therapist.

The classic structure comes from a 1987 paper in the American Journal of Psychiatry by the psychiatrists Samuel Perry, Arnold Cooper and Robert Michels, "The psychodynamic formulation: its purpose, structure, and clinical application". They proposed that a written formulation should be brief (about 500 to 750 words) and should have four parts.

  1. A summary statement that describes the patient and the presenting problem and places the problem in the context of his life.
  2. A description of the nondynamic factors that contribute to the problem: genetic, constitutional, medical and traumatic influences. Perry and his colleagues insisted that the presence of such factors does not make psychodynamic understanding irrelevant, and that a psychodynamic formulation that ignores them is a poor one.
  3. A psychodynamic explanation of the central conflicts: what they are, how they developed and how they are repeated in the patient's current life and relationships.
  4. A prognostic assessment of how these conflicts are likely to show themselves in treatment, as transference and resistance, and what this implies for the choice and conduct of therapy.

A more recent teaching scheme, from Deborah Cabaniss and her colleagues at Columbia University (Psychodynamic Formulation, 2013), reduces the task to three verbs. Describe the problems and the patterns (in the person's sense of self, relationships, characteristic ways of adapting and defending, thinking and working). Review the developmental history. Link the two, using one or more organising ideas: trauma, early difficulties, conflict and defence, relationships with others, the development of the self, or attachment.

The ingredients of the explanation come from the theories of Chapter 3. The clinician identifies the central conflict, usually a wish that is opposed by a fear (of punishment, of loss of love, of guilt) and kept out of awareness; the principal defences (Chapter 3 and the Personality book); the object relations, meaning the internal templates of self and other that the person brings to every relationship; the way he regulates self-esteem; and his ego strengths, such as the capacity to tolerate painful feelings, to reflect on himself (psychological mindedness) and to form a working relationship, which decide whether exploratory therapy is suitable. Several brief psychodynamic therapies have turned this kind of formulation into structured tools, notably David Malan's triangles of conflict and of person and Lester Luborsky's core conflictual relationship theme (CCRT), with its three components of the wish, the response of the other and the response of the self; they are taught with the brief therapies in Section 3.6.

Ravi: a psychodynamic formulation (abridged)

Summary. Ravi, 24, the eldest son of a farming family, presents with a three-month depression that followed his third failure in the bank examinations and his cousin's success. Nondynamic factors. A family history of depression and the biological features of his illness suggest a constitutional vulnerability and call for a medical review alongside psychotherapy.

Central conflict. Ravi has longed all his life to be valued by his father for himself, and has learned that his father's regard is given for achievement. His wish to be loved unconditionally is opposed by the fear that, if he fails, he will lose that love altogether. Alongside the longing there is anger at a father who compares and belittles him, but for a dutiful eldest son in a family that depends on him this anger is unthinkable. It is kept out of awareness and turned against himself, as self-reproach ("I have wasted their money"), an idea that goes back to Freud's "Mourning and Melancholia" (1917). His father's critical voice has become an internal critic that now attacks him from within. The same template, a judging authority and a self that falls short, has organised his relationships with teachers, coaching-centre instructors and his cousin, and his withdrawal protects him from a confrontation he both wants and fears. In Luborsky's terms: the wish, to be valued as he is; the expected response of the other, to be compared and found wanting; the response of the self, to withdraw, hide his anger and feel worthless.

Prognosis in treatment. Ravi will probably begin as an ideal patient: punctual, polite and eager to please. He is likely to hear the therapist's comments as grades, to hide setbacks, to feel that a cancelled session means he has disappointed, and to comply without changing. The therapist may feel pulled to push him, as his father does, or to rescue him, as his mother does. His conscientiousness, his capacity for a warm relationship and his ability to reflect suggest he could use a time-limited exploratory therapy focused on his conditional sense of worth and his unspoken anger.

Exam trap

Perry, Cooper and Michels (1987) gave the psychodynamic formulation four parts: a summary statement, the nondynamic factors, the psychodynamic explanation of central conflicts and the prognostic assessment (how the conflicts will show up in treatment as transference and resistance). A psychodynamic formulation does not ignore biology. The CCRT (wish, response of other, response of self) is Luborsky's; the triangles of conflict and of person are Malan's. Cabaniss's scheme is describe, review, link.

One client, four stories

You have now read four formulations of the same young man, built from exactly the same facts. Put them side by side, as Table 2.30 does, and notice what changes and what does not.

Table 2.30 Ravi seen through four lenses.
LensCentral questionRavi's story in one sentenceTreatment that follows
5 PsWhy this person, why now, why still, what helps?A vulnerable perfectionist, triggered by a humiliating failure, kept down by withdrawal and criticism, held up by his mother and his faithTarget the perpetuating factors; build on the protective ones
BiopsychosocialAt which levels do the causes act, and how do they interact?Genes, sleep, beliefs, family pressure and debt feeding one another in a circleInvestigations and psychiatric review; psychological therapy; family session and practical help
Cognitive-behaviouralWhich beliefs and behaviours produce and maintain the problem?"I am worth only what I achieve", activated by failure and confirmed every day by avoidanceBehavioural activation, thought records, experiments, then core belief work
PsychodynamicWhich unconscious conflict is being repeated?A son who cannot be angry with a father whose love seems conditional, turning the anger on himselfTime-limited exploratory therapy; attention to the transference

Three lessons follow. First, the same fact changes its meaning with the lens. The father's comparisons are a precipitating and perpetuating factor in the 5 Ps, a social stressor in the biopsychosocial grid, the activator of a core belief in the cognitive formulation and the current edition of a lifelong relational conflict in the psychodynamic one. None of these readings is false; each picks out a different aspect of the fact and connects it to a different mechanism.

Second, the lenses are not equally specific. The 5 Ps and the biopsychosocial grid are frameworks without a theory of their own: they organise the factors and leave open how they are connected. The cognitive-behavioural and psychodynamic formulations are theories: they say what the mechanism is. In practice clinicians often use a framework to collect and organise the information and a theory to explain it, and many write an integrative formulation that does both.

Third, a formulation is chosen, not discovered, and the choice should be justified by evidence and by the client. A theory with good evidence for this kind of problem (cognitive-behavioural therapy has a strong evidence base for depression, Section 5.11), a client who finds the account convincing and useful, and a treatment that the service can actually provide are all good reasons for preferring one formulation. The worst reason is that it is the only lens the clinician knows. Notice, finally, what all four formulations share: they explain, they are individual, they are tentative, and they lead to action. That is what distinguishes every one of them from a diagnosis.

Diagnosis versus formulation

Ravi's provisional diagnosis is a depressive episode, possibly recurrent. That single phrase does a great deal. It places him in a category that research has studied for a century, so it tells the clinician what course to expect, what treatments have been shown to work for people in that category, what risks to watch, and what to write on an insurance claim, a medical certificate or a research form. It lets a psychiatrist in Mysuru and a psychologist in Delhi know at once roughly what they are dealing with. These are the purposes of diagnosis, the assignment of a person's presenting problems to a category of a standard classification such as DSM-5-TR or ICD-11 (Section 2.10).

What the diagnosis cannot do is explain Ravi. Two people with the same diagnosis may have become depressed for entirely different reasons and stay depressed through entirely different processes; one may need help mostly with a grief, the other with a marriage, a third with a thyroid. A diagnosis describes what Ravi has in common with other people; a formulation explains what is particular to him. Table 2.31 sets out the contrast.

Table 2.31 Diagnosis and formulation compared.
FeatureDiagnosisFormulation
Main purposeTo classify the problem in a standard systemTo explain the problem in this person and guide treatment
ApproachNomothetic (what he shares with others)Idiographic (what is particular to him)
FormA label, usually categoricalA narrative or diagram, a hypothesis
ContentSymptoms, duration, exclusionsCauses, precipitants, maintaining and protective factors
Role of theoryLargely atheoretical about causes (DSM since 1980)Explicitly theory-informed
Who decidesThe clinician, against the manual's criteriaThe clinician and the client together
StabilityFixed until criteria changeRevised as therapy proceeds
StrengthsCommunication, research, epidemiology, servicesIndividual treatment planning; meaning for the client

The two are complementary rather than rivals in most clinical practice. The diagnosis tells you which body of evidence applies; the formulation tells you how to apply it to this person and what to do when the standard treatment does not fit. A clinician who has only a diagnosis treats a category; one who has only a formulation may miss what research knows about people like the client, including the risks.

Some psychologists go further. Kanfer and Saslow in 1965 offered behavioural analysis as an "alternative to diagnostic classification" (Section 2.8), and in Britain a vocal group of clinical psychologists has argued that formulation should replace psychiatric diagnosis altogether, on the grounds that diagnostic categories lack validity, encourage the belief that distress is a disease located inside the individual, and stigmatise. Its most developed statement is the Power Threat Meaning Framework (2018) of Lucy Johnstone and Mary Boyle, which asks not "What is wrong with you?" but "What has happened to you?", and organises explanations around the operation of power, the threats it poses and the meanings people make of them. The argument about labelling, and Rosenhan's pseudopatients, are taken up in Section 2.10.

Formulation has its own weakness, and an honest clinician should know it. Its reliability is modest. When several clinicians formulate the same case, they agree reasonably well on the descriptive level (what the problems are) but much less on the inferential level (which beliefs, conflicts or processes lie underneath). Peter Bieling and Willem Kuyken reviewed the evidence in 2003 under a pointed title, "Is cognitive case formulation science or science fiction?", and concluded that the descriptive level was on firm ground but the deeper inferences needed much more research. Evidence that formulation-driven treatment produces better outcomes than standard protocols is also limited. These are reasons to hold a formulation lightly, as a hypothesis, which is exactly what the definition asks.

Exam trap

"A formulation differs from a diagnosis in that it primarily aims to ..." Answer: provide a hypothesis about the cause, precipitants and maintaining factors of an individual's difficulties. The distractors each describe something else: classifying symptoms in a nosological system is diagnosis; determining prognosis and course is a use of diagnosis (and of formulation, but not its primary aim); ruling out organic causes is part of differential diagnosis. Diagnosis is nomothetic and categorical; formulation is idiographic, explanatory, collaborative and revisable. The Power Threat Meaning Framework (2018) is Johnstone and Boyle's alternative to diagnosis.

Differential diagnosis as a process

Before a formulation can be trusted, the clinician must be reasonably sure what he is formulating. Depressed mood, tiredness and weight loss can come from a depressive disorder, from bipolar disorder in its depressed phase, from an underactive thyroid, from a medicine, from alcohol, from early dementia in an older person, or from an ordinary and proportionate reaction to a hard year. Differential diagnosis is the systematic process of distinguishing among the conditions that could account for the same presenting features: the clinician lists the plausible explanations, then gathers evidence for and against each until the most likely one remains.

The word "process" matters. Differential diagnosis is not a list written at the end of the case record; it begins in the first minutes of the interview and drives everything that follows. Sullivan's "detailed inquiry" (Section 2.1) is the stage at which the interviewer tests the hypotheses he has formed. The negative history of Section 2.3 ("no elated periods, no head injury, no alcohol") is differential diagnosis written down: each negative is an alternative considered and set aside. A clinician who decides on the diagnosis in the first five minutes and then asks only questions that confirm it has committed premature closure (Section 2.2), and he is in the company of the admitting psychiatrists who met Rosenhan's pseudopatients.

Is there a right order in which to consider the possibilities? Broadly, yes: rule out the causes that are most dangerous to miss and most specifically treatable first. The psychiatrist Michael B. First, in the DSM-5 Handbook of Differential Diagnosis (2014), set out six steps that make the logic explicit.

  1. Rule out malingering and factitious disorder: is the person feigning or producing the symptoms (Section 2.3)?
  2. Rule out a substance as the cause: a drug of abuse, a prescribed medicine or a toxin.
  3. Rule out a general medical condition as the cause: thyroid disease, anaemia, a brain tumour, epilepsy, an infection.
  4. Determine the specific primary mental disorder or disorders.
  5. Distinguish an adjustment disorder, or a residual "other specified" or "unspecified" category, from these specific disorders.
  6. Establish the boundary with no mental disorder: are the symptoms severe and impairing enough to count as a disorder at all, or are they within the range of ordinary distress?

Indian case conferences follow the same logic in an older, hierarchical form: organic causes first, then substance use, then psychotic disorders, then mood disorders, then anxiety and related disorders, then personality disorders. A higher category, if present, usually explains the symptoms of a lower one, so it is considered first. This is the hierarchical principle of diagnosis usually traced to Karl Jaspers's General Psychopathology (1913; Section 2.4), and the same order is built into the blocks of the ICD-10 chapter on mental disorders that most Indian hospitals still use (F0 organic, F1 substance use, F2 psychotic, F3 mood, F4 neurotic and stress-related, F6 personality). It is not a law: modern classifications allow two diagnoses at once (comorbidity, Section 2.10) where each is independently present.

Push Ravi through the steps. There is no hint of feigning and no obvious external gain. He takes no alcohol, tobacco or drugs, and the only medicines he has had are the vitamins his general practitioner prescribed, so a substance cause is unlikely. A medical cause has not yet been excluded, which is why thyroid function and haemoglobin are being checked. Among the primary disorders, the history describes the picture of a depressive episode; the month of low mood at 17 raises the question of whether this is in fact a second, recurrent episode, which a more detailed history of that month will answer. Bipolar disorder must be kept in mind, because a first depression in a young man is sometimes the first episode of a bipolar illness; the history found no periods of elated mood, and the family will be asked again. Adjustment disorder fits the clear stressor, but it is a residual category, used only when the criteria for a specific disorder such as a depressive episode are not met. And the severity of his symptoms, the biological features, the impairment and the thoughts of death place him well beyond the boundary of ordinary disappointment. The case record therefore reads: provisional diagnosis, depressive episode, possibly recurrent (recurrent depressive disorder if the month at 17 proves to have been a depressive episode); differential diagnoses, depressive disorder due to a medical condition (pending investigations) and bipolar disorder (to be monitored). (The criteria themselves belong to the Psychiatric Disorders book; how the classifications are organised is the subject of Section 2.10.)

Clinicians' errors in differential diagnosis are mostly errors of reasoning rather than of knowledge: anchoring on the first impression, seeking only confirming evidence, judging by the vivid recent case, and diagnostic overshadowing, a term introduced by Steven Reiss and his colleagues in 1982 for the tendency to attribute a person's new symptoms to a condition already known (his intellectual disability, his schizophrenia) and so to miss a second, treatable problem. A person with schizophrenia who becomes withdrawn may be depressed, or hypothyroid, or in pain.

Exam trap

Differential diagnosis is a process of generating and testing alternative explanations, not a label. Organic (medical) and substance causes are ruled out first. First's (2014) six steps run: malingering and factitious disorder; substance; medical condition; primary disorder; adjustment and residual categories; boundary with no disorder. Adjustment disorder is diagnosed only when the criteria for a more specific disorder are not met. Diagnostic overshadowing (Reiss and colleagues, 1982) is attributing new symptoms to an existing diagnosis.

Writing the clinical report

Everything in this chapter ends up in writing. A clinical report (in psychology, often called the psychological or assessment report) is the written account of an assessment, its findings, the formulation and the recommendations, addressed to a particular reader in answer to a particular question. That last phrase is the key to writing a good one. A report is not a record of everything the clinician did; it is an answer. A psychiatrist who asks "Is there evidence of psychosis?", a school that asks "Why is this child failing?", a court that asks "Is this man fit to stand trial?" and a client who asks "What is wrong with me, and what can I do?" each need a different document from the same assessment.

The headings vary between settings, but most reports follow the order in Table 2.32.

Table 2.32 The usual headings of a clinical (psychological) report.
HeadingWhat it contains
Identifying informationName, age, sex, education, occupation, dates of assessment, referred by
Reason for referralThe referral question, stated precisely
Sources of information and proceduresInterviews, informants, records, tests and observations used, with dates
Relevant backgroundA summary of the history, selected for relevance to the question
Behavioural observationsAppearance, behaviour, rapport and test-taking attitude; the MSE
Results and interpretationFindings organised by theme, with their meaning
Summary and formulationThe integrated explanation
DiagnosisProvisional and differential diagnoses
RecommendationsSpecific, feasible next steps, each linked to the findings
SignatureName, qualification, registration number, date

A handful of principles separate a useful report from a long one.

  • Answer the referral question, early and plainly. If the question cannot be answered, say why.
  • Integrate rather than list. Organise the results by theme (mood, cognition, relationships, risk), drawing on every source, instead of reporting one test after another. A report that says "On test A he scored ... On test B he scored ..." has left the hardest part of the work to the reader.
  • Separate observation from inference. "He wept twice while describing his father" is an observation; "he is grieving the relationship he wished he had" is an inference, and should be marked as one ("this suggests ...").
  • Write for the reader. Use plain language, define the technical terms you cannot avoid, and keep it as short as the question allows.
  • Be specific to this person. Avoid statements that would be true of almost anyone.
  • Include strengths and limitations: what the client does well, and what limits confidence in the findings (language, fatigue, tests without Indian norms, an uncooperative informant).
  • Make recommendations specific and feasible: "weekly cognitive-behavioural therapy for 12 to 16 sessions, beginning with activity scheduling; thyroid function and haemoglobin; a family session" rather than "therapy is recommended".
  • Remember who may read it. Under the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 (Section 25), a person with mental illness has the right to access his basic medical records; the professional in charge may withhold specific information only if disclosing it would cause him serious mental harm or would be likely to harm others, and must then tell him that he can apply to the Mental Health Review Board for its release. Write every report as though the client will read it, because he may.

The fifth principle has a name. In a classroom demonstration published in 1949, the psychologist Bertram R. Forer (1914 to 2000) gave his students a personality test and, a week later, a "personalised" interpretation of their results. Every student in fact received the same paragraph, assembled largely from a newsstand astrology book ("You have a great need for other people to like and admire you ... At times you are extroverted, affable, sociable, while at other times you are introverted, wary, reserved"). Asked to rate how well it described them on a scale from 0 (poor) to 5 (perfect), the 39 students gave it an average of 4.26. In 1956 Paul Everett Meehl (1920 to 2003) named the tendency to accept vague, universally applicable descriptions as accurate personal ones the Barnum effect, after the American showman P. T. Barnum, whose circus was said to have "a little something for everybody". It is also called the Forer effect. Statements of this kind make a report feel accurate to its reader and tell him nothing; the cure is to say only what distinguishes this person from others.

From Ravi's report: summary and recommendations

Ravi, 24, came with his mother; the questions for assessment were the nature of his low mood and the treatment he needs. Assessment over two sessions (interviews with Ravi and his mother, mental status examination, risk assessment, a week of self-monitoring and a role play) indicates a depressive episode of three months' duration, without psychotic features, with daily passive death wishes and occasional thoughts of suicide but no intent, plan or preparation; the risk is raised but not imminent (Section 2.7). His difficulties are best understood as the activation, by his third examination failure and his cousin's success, of a long-standing belief that his worth depends on achievement; they are maintained by withdrawal, rumination, poor sleep and continuing family criticism. Strengths include his mother's support, his intelligence and conscientiousness, his faith, and his recovery from an earlier, briefer episode. Recommendations: (1) psychiatric review to consider antidepressant medication; (2) thyroid function and haemoglobin; (3) weekly cognitive-behavioural therapy, beginning with behavioural activation; (4) a family session with both parents; (5) a written safety plan, with the pesticide at the family farm locked away, and risk reviewed at every session.

Exam trap

The Barnum effect (named by Meehl, 1956; demonstrated by Forer, 1949, hence also the Forer effect) is the acceptance of vague, generally true statements as accurate descriptions of oneself. A report should answer the referral question, integrate findings by theme rather than test by test, separate data from inference and give specific recommendations. Under the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017, Section 25 gives the right of access to basic medical records.

Giving feedback to the client and the family

The last step of assessment is the one most often skimped: telling the client what was found. For much of the twentieth century psychological test results were treated as the property of the professional, written up for the referring doctor and never discussed with the person tested. That attitude is no longer defensible, ethically or legally. The Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 gives a person with mental illness, and his nominated representative, a right to information (Section 22) about the nature of his illness and the proposed treatment plan, in a language and form he can understand, and a right to confidentiality (Section 23) about his mental health and treatment. (The Act itself is taught in Chapter 1.) But the strongest argument for good feedback is clinical: done well, feedback is itself a treatment.

Key study: feedback as an intervention (Finn and Tonsager, 1992)

Does simply hearing the results of an assessment help? Stephen E. Finn and Mary Tonsager studied university students who were on the waiting list of a counselling centre. All completed the MMPI-2 personality inventory. One group then received a collaborative feedback session in which the results were discussed with them in relation to their own questions; the control group received equal attention from an examiner but no feedback on their results. Compared with the controls, the students who received feedback showed a decline in symptomatic distress and a rise in self-esteem, both immediately and at follow-up about two weeks later, and they felt more hopeful about their problems.

The study helped launch Finn's therapeutic assessment, a collaborative model in which the client helps frame the questions the assessment will try to answer and the findings are worked through together, so that the assessment becomes a brief intervention in its own right.

How should feedback be given? Finn suggested that findings be thought of in three levels and presented in order. Level 1 information confirms what the client already thinks about himself ("You are a very conscientious person who sets high standards"); it is easily accepted and builds trust. Level 2 information modifies or extends his self-view in ways he can accept with a little reflection ("Those high standards may also be making it hard for you to start anything you might not do perfectly"). Level 3 information is so discrepant from his self-view that he is likely to reject it or be distressed by it ("Part of you may be very angry with your father"); it is offered tentatively, only when trust is established, and sometimes not at all in the first session. Beginning with Level 3 information is the commonest way to lose a client.

Around that ordering, a few practical rules apply in any setting.

  • Prepare. Decide in advance on the three or four main messages, the words you will use and what you will leave for later.
  • Start with the client's questions. Ask what he understands so far and what he most wants to know.
  • Use plain language and his own words, in his own language. "Depression" may need explaining as an illness of mood, sleep, energy and thinking, not a weakness or "madness"; his own idiom ("tension", "my mind is not working") is a good bridge (Section 2.2).
  • Share the formulation, not just the label. Draw the vicious circle of Figure 2.9 or the five-part model on a sheet of paper and ask, "Does this fit your experience? What would you change?" A formulation the client has corrected is one he owns.
  • Name the diagnosis honestly, explain what it means and what it does not mean, and say what can be done. Hope is a clinical intervention, not a courtesy.
  • Check understanding by asking the client to explain the main points back in his own words (sometimes called the teach-back method), allow time for feeling, and give a short written summary to take home.

Feedback to the family. In India the family is usually waiting outside, and often expects to hear the findings before the patient does. The clinician's first duty is to the client: with an adult, what is shared with the family is decided with him beforehand, within the limits of confidentiality, except where there is a serious risk to his life or to others (Section 2.7). In practice most clients want their families involved, and a joint session is often the most useful hour of the whole assessment. Its aims are to give the family a shared, blame-free understanding of the problem, to correct harmful beliefs (that he is lazy, that he is possessed, that a marriage will cure him, that medicines for the mind cause addiction), to give each member something specific to do, and to agree on what to do in a crisis.

Ravi's family session shows how much skill this takes. His father arrived defensive, sure that he was about to be blamed. The clinician began with what everyone agreed on (Level 1): that Ravi is a hard-working son who has always tried to make his parents proud, and that the family has sacrificed a great deal for his education. She then drew the circle of failure, withdrawal, poor sleep and self-blame, and asked the father where he thought the comparisons with the cousin fitted into it. He was silent for a while, and then said that his own father used to compare him with his elder brother. No one had to tell him to stop; the family agreed instead on a plan: no talk of examinations at meals for a month, a weekly call in which Ravi's mother would also take part, help with activity scheduling from his sister, the pesticide at the farm kept locked away (as agreed in the risk assessment), and a list of the warning signs that should bring them back to the clinic at once. The temple vow was not discouraged. It was part of the family's hope, and the clinician treated it as an ally.

Clinical link

In a feedback session with a family, frame criticism as worry. "You are frightened for his future, and the comparisons come from that fear" is something a father can hear; "Your criticism is making him worse" usually is not. The goal is not to find the person to blame but to help every member of the family step out of the cycle that is keeping the problem going. Families who feel blamed stop coming; families who feel understood become the most powerful protective factor in the formulation.

Exam trap

Collaborative feedback can itself reduce distress and raise self-esteem: Finn and Tonsager (1992), the origin of therapeutic assessment. Present findings from Level 1 (consistent with the client's self-view) through Level 2 to Level 3 (discrepant), not the other way round. Under the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017, Section 22 is the right to information, Section 23 the right to confidentiality and Section 25 access to medical records. Family members hear what the adult client has agreed they should hear, except where safety requires otherwise.

Ravi's assessment is complete. A frightened mother's complaint that her son "barely leaves his room" has become a history, a picture of his present mental state, an estimate of risk, a functional analysis, a formulation seen through four lenses, a provisional diagnosis with its alternatives, a written report and a plan the family has helped to make. One question remains, the one with which this chapter began: what does it mean to put a person into a diagnostic category, and how far can such categories be trusted? That is the subject of the final section.

Scan Your Brain
