2.10 Classification as a Clinical ToolLower yield · 2 Qs

Section 2.9 drew a line between two ways of summing up a client. A formulation is a story about one person: why this problem, in this person, now. A diagnosis is a name taken from a shared list. This last section is about the shared list. It does not teach the disorders themselves; their symptoms, criteria and causes fill the Psychiatric Disorders book. It asks what the list is for, how its two great versions (the American DSM and the international ICD) are built, how anyone can tell whether two clinicians using the list really agree, and what a label does to the person who receives it. That last question brings us back to the eight pseudopatients who opened the chapter.

Why clinicians classify

Imagine a clinic with no shared names for anything. One psychologist writes that Ravi is "low and stuck", a second that he has "nervous weakness", a third that he is "going through a phase". None of them is exactly wrong, but their notes cannot be compared, counted, researched or acted on by anyone else. Classification is the sorting of things into groups according to the features they share. A classification of diseases or disorders is called a nosology (from the Greek nosos, disease), and the set of names it uses is its nomenclature. Diagnosis is the act of deciding which group in the nosology an individual's problems belong to. Biologists classify insects and geologists classify rocks for the same reason clinicians classify disorders: without agreed groups, knowledge cannot accumulate.

Clinicians classify for at least six reasons, and each reason is also a test that a good classification must pass.

  • Communication. A diagnosis compresses a great deal of information into a few words. "Bipolar I disorder, current episode manic" tells a colleague in Delhi or Toronto a lot before she has met the patient. DSM-5-TR names this as the "clear virtue" of its criteria: they create "a common language for communication between clinicians".
  • Prediction. A sound category tells you what usually happens next: the likely course, the risk of relapse and the outcome with and without treatment.
  • Treatment selection. Most treatments have been tested on diagnostic groups, so the diagnosis points the clinician to the relevant evidence: exposure for a specific phobia, a mood stabiliser for bipolar disorder.
  • Research. Studies of causes and treatments need groups of people who have the same condition. The US-UK Diagnostic Project in Section 2.1 showed what happens when two cities use the same word for different patients.
  • Administration. Hospitals count admissions, governments plan services, insurers pay claims, and courts and disability boards make decisions, and all of them work through diagnostic codes.
  • Understanding. Grouping people who share a pattern is the first step towards finding its cause. General paresis, a common cause of madness in nineteenth-century asylums, was first described as a clinical group and only later traced to syphilis.

All six jobs depend on a prior decision: what counts as a disorder at all? DSM-5-TR defines a mental disorder as a syndrome marked by clinically significant disturbance in a person's cognition, emotion regulation or behaviour that reflects a dysfunction in the psychological, biological or developmental processes underlying mental functioning, and that is usually associated with significant distress or disability. Two exclusions follow, and both matter for this section. An expectable or culturally approved response to a common stressor or loss, such as grief after a death, is not a mental disorder. And socially deviant behaviour (the manual's examples are political, religious and sexual) and conflicts between the individual and society are not mental disorders unless they arise from a dysfunction in the individual. The manual then adds a sentence that students often miss: a diagnosis is not the same thing as a need for treatment. Whether treatment is needed depends on severity, distress, disability, risk and the balance of benefits and harms.

Exam trap

A nosology is the classification system itself; its nomenclature is the list of names; taxonomy is the general science of classifying. A diagnosis classifies the problem, not the person, and it is descriptive: a DSM diagnosis says what is present, not why. The individual explanation, the hypothesis about cause, precipitants and maintaining factors, is the formulation (Section 2.9). Finally, under DSM-5-TR a diagnosis does not by itself mean that treatment is needed, and distress or disability is "usually", not always, present.

Categories, dimensions and prototypes

There are two basic ways to sort people's problems, and you use both every day. You can put them in boxes ("she has a fever or she does not") or place them on a scale ("her temperature is 38.4 degrees"). The same choice runs through psychiatric classification.

The categorical approach treats each disorder as a distinct class that a person either has or does not have. Its pure form, the classical categorical approach, goes back to Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926), the German psychiatrist who, in the closing years of the nineteenth century, separated dementia praecox (later renamed schizophrenia) from manic-depressive insanity by their course and outcome. Kraepelin believed that each disorder had its own underlying brain pathology, as an infection does. If that were true, every member of a category would share the same essential features, a single set of criteria would fit everyone, and knowing the diagnosis would tell you the cause. That is how a physician thinks about appendicitis. Few mental disorders behave this way. Most arise from interacting biological, psychological and social causes, the same symptom (poor sleep, poor concentration) turns up in many disorders, and the borders between categories are blurred.

The dimensional approach instead rates each person on one or more continuous attributes. Barlow and Durand's example is a patient rated, on scales of 1 to 10, as severely anxious (10), moderately depressed (5) and mildly manic (2): a profile of 10, 5, 2 rather than a single name. Dimensions suit problems that are distributed continuously in the population, as anxiety, low mood and most personality traits are. DSM-5-TR's own introduction sums up the trade-off fairly. Dimensional systems increase reliability and carry more information, because they record problems that fall below a categorical threshold. But numbers are less familiar and vivid than names, nobody yet agrees on which dimensions to use, and a clinician who has to decide whether to treat still needs a cut-off point, which quietly brings back a category.

Between the two lies the prototypical approach, which is what DSM and ICD actually use. A prototype lists the essential features of a category together with other features that are common but not required, and a person qualifies by matching enough of the list. Criteria of this "any five of nine" kind are called polythetic; criteria that every member must meet in full are called monothetic. The DSM-5-TR major depressive episode is the standard example: at least five of nine symptoms over the same two weeks, at least one of which must be depressed mood or loss of interest or pleasure. Two people can both meet the criteria while sharing only one symptom. The prototype is honest about the state of knowledge and easy to use, but it pays a price: fuzzy edges between categories, great variety within a single diagnosis (heterogeneity), many patients who fall just short of every category and end up with an "other specified" or "unspecified" diagnosis, and high comorbidity, the presence of two or more disorders in the same person at the same time.

Modern manuals have therefore moved towards hybrids. DSM-5 (2013) kept its categories but added dimensional severity ratings, cross-cutting symptom measures that rate problems such as anxiety, low mood and poor sleep across all diagnoses, and, in its Section III, an Alternative DSM-5 Model for Personality Disorders built on levels of personality functioning and pathological traits. The personality disorder criteria in the main body of the manual are still the categorical ones carried over from DSM-IV-TR. ICD-11 went further. It replaced the list of separate personality disorder types with a single diagnosis of personality disorder graded mild, moderate or severe, to which the clinician may add trait domains (negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition and anankastia) and a borderline pattern. Fully dimensional research systems, such as the Hierarchical Taxonomy of Psychopathology (HiTOP), have been proposed as replacements for categories altogether. Table 2.33 compares the three basic approaches.

Table 2.33 Three ways to classify psychological problems.
ApproachCore ideaStrengthWeakness
Classical categorical (Kraepelin)A disorder is present or absent; every member meets every criterionClear decisions; one cause implies one treatmentFits almost no mental disorder; forces borderline cases into boxes
DimensionalEach person is rated on continuous attributes (anxiety 10, depression 5, mania 2)More reliable; keeps subthreshold informationNo agreed set of dimensions; unfamiliar; still needs cut-offs for decisions
Prototypical (polythetic), used by DSM and ICDEnough features from a list, some of them essential ("five of nine, including one of two")Fits current knowledge; easy to useFuzzy boundaries, heterogeneity within diagnoses, high comorbidity
Exam trap

DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR are categorical systems with polythetic (prototypical) criteria and some dimensional additions (severity specifiers, cross-cutting measures, the Section III personality model). They are not dimensional systems, even though they contain dimensional ratings. The classical categorical model is Kraepelin's. Comorbidity means two or more disorders in one person, not two symptoms of one disorder. "Five of nine symptoms" is polythetic; "all of the following" is monothetic.

From DSM-I to DSM-5-TR: a short history

Classifications of madness are as old as medicine, but official, shared ones are young. The World Health Organization (WHO) added a section on mental disorders to the sixth revision of its International Classification of Diseases (ICD-6) in 1948. In 1952 the American Psychiatric Association published the first Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM-I), the first official manual of mental disorders to include a glossary describing each category. Its language came from Adolf Meyer, whom you met in Section 2.3: disorders were "reactions" of the personality to biological, psychological and social factors. DSM-II (1968) was based on the mental disorders section of ICD-8, and both went into effect in the same year (the World Health Assembly had approved ICD-8 in 1966). It dropped most of the "reactions" but was full of psychoanalytic terms, above all "neurosis". Neither manual gave rules for deciding when a patient had a disorder. The glossary sketched a typical case, and each clinician decided how close was close enough.

You already know what happened next. Section 2.1 described Beck's 1962 finding that pairs of psychiatrists agreed on the specific diagnosis only about half the time, the US-UK Diagnostic Project showing that New York and London used "schizophrenia" for different patients, and the analysis showing that criterion variance (different rules for turning information into a diagnosis) was the largest source of disagreement. Rosenhan's 1973 paper landed in the middle of this crisis of confidence. The remedy came largely from Washington University in St. Louis, where Eli Robins (1921 to 1994) and Samuel B. Guze (1923 to 2000) argued that a psychiatric diagnosis is a scientific claim and should be tested like one. In 1972 John Feighner, Robins, Guze and their colleagues published explicit diagnostic criteria for research use (the Feighner criteria), and Spitzer, Jean Endicott and Robins developed them into the Research Diagnostic Criteria (RDC) a few years later.

DSM-III (1980), produced under Spitzer's direction, carried these ideas into everyday practice, and it is the most important edition in the series. Three changes stand out. First, it was descriptive and tried to be atheoretical: disorders were defined by what could be observed and reported, not by presumed psychoanalytic or biological causes, so "neurosis" disappeared as an organising idea and the phobias, for example, moved into a new group of anxiety disorders. Second, every disorder had explicit diagnostic criteria, specific enough for their reliability and validity to be studied. Field trials of reliability, a DSM-III innovation, became a fixture of every later edition. Third, it introduced the multiaxial system, which recorded each patient on five axes so that the clinician considered the whole person and not only the disorder. In their DSM-IV form the axes were:

  • Axis I: clinical disorders (the main psychiatric syndromes).
  • Axis II: personality disorders and mental retardation (now called intellectual disability).
  • Axis III: general medical conditions.
  • Axis IV: psychosocial and environmental problems.
  • Axis V: the Global Assessment of Functioning (GAF), a single 100-point rating of overall functioning.

DSM-III-R (1987) corrected inconsistencies found in DSM-III. DSM-IV (1994) was the product of six years of work by more than 1,000 people, built on systematic literature reviews, reanalyses of existing data and field trials, and coordinated closely with ICD-10. (Sources give different years for ICD-10 because several dates attach to it. The World Health Assembly approved ICD-10 in 1990; its mental disorders chapter appeared with clinical guidelines in 1992, the year DSM-5-TR gives, and with research criteria in 1993; member countries began using it from 1994.) It also abandoned the old split between "organic" and "functional" disorders. DSM-IV-TR (2000) was a text revision: it updated the descriptive text and left the criteria virtually unchanged. DSM-5 (2013), whose task force was chaired by David Kupfer with Darrel Regier as vice-chair, made three structural changes worth knowing. It switched from Roman to Arabic numerals so that later updates could be issued as 5.1, 5.2 and so on. It abolished the multiaxial system: the former Axis I, II and III diagnoses are now listed together, psychosocial problems are recorded with codes from a chapter of "other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention", and the GAF was replaced by the WHO Disability Assessment Schedule (WHODAS). And it ordered its chapters along the life span, from neurodevelopmental disorders at the start to neurocognitive disorders near the end. DSM-5-TR, published in March 2022, is the first published revision of DSM-5. It updated the text of nearly every disorder, clarified the criteria of more than 70, and added one new diagnosis, prolonged grief disorder, together with symptom codes for recording suicidal behaviour and non-suicidal self-injury. Figure 2.12 lays out the sequence.

The history of the manuals is also a history of what counts as a disorder, and nothing shows this more sharply than the case of homosexuality. Classifying a behaviour or identity as a mental disorder is called pathologisation, and removing it from the classification is depathologisation. DSM-I placed homosexuality among the "sociopathic personality disturbances", and DSM-II listed it as a sexual deviation. In December 1973, after sustained protest by gay activists and research such as the 1957 finding of the American psychologist Evelyn Hooker (1907 to 1996) that expert judges could not tell the projective test records of gay and heterosexual men apart, the American Psychiatric Association's Board of Trustees voted to remove homosexuality as such from the manual. A narrower category for people distressed by their orientation survived, as "sexual orientation disturbance" and then "ego-dystonic homosexuality" in DSM-III, until DSM-III-R dropped it in 1987. The WHO removed homosexuality from the ICD with ICD-10, which the World Health Assembly approved in 1990. (In India, the Supreme Court decriminalised consensual same-sex relations between adults in Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India in 2018.) The same reasoning has since reached gender identity. DSM-5 replaced "gender identity disorder" with gender dysphoria, which places the clinical problem in the distress rather than in the identity, and ICD-11 moved gender incongruence out of the mental disorders chapter altogether. Notice the DSM-5-TR definition of disorder at work here: deviance from social norms is not, by itself, a disorder.

Exam trap

Learn the DSM years as one string: 1952, 1968, 1980, 1987, 1994, 2000, 2013, 2022 (I, II, III, III-R, IV, IV-TR, 5, 5-TR). Explicit criteria and the multiaxial system arrived with DSM-III (1980), not DSM-IV; DSM-5 removed the axes. DSM comes from the American Psychiatric Association, not the American Psychological Association. "Pathologisation and depathologisation of homosexuality" means its classification as a mental disorder and the removal of that classification; it does not mean changing orientation (that is so-called conversion therapy) or coming out. The APA vote was in 1973, but a residual category lasted until DSM-III-R (1987).

DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 side by side

Two classifications are in use today, and a clinical psychologist in India needs to know both. The Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM) is published by one national professional body, the American Psychiatric Association, and its current version is DSM-5-TR (2022). The International Classification of Diseases (ICD) is published by the WHO, covers every disease and health condition from cholera to fractures, and is the international standard that member countries use to record and report health statistics. Its eleventh revision, ICD-11, was adopted by the 72nd World Health Assembly in May 2019 and came into effect on 1 January 2022, although each country decides when to make the switch from ICD-10. Even the United States still codes with ICD-10-CM, its clinical modification of ICD-10.

The two systems were developed in close contact, and DSM-5-TR reports that they are now closer than at any time since DSM-II and ICD-8: their chapters are organised in much the same way, and most disorders carry the same names. The differences that remain come mostly from their different jobs. DSM is written for clinicians and researchers and gives explicit polythetic criteria with symptom counts and minimum durations. ICD has to work in every member country, often in busy general clinics without a specialist, so its mental disorders chapter is accompanied by the WHO's Clinical Descriptions and Diagnostic Requirements, which set out the essential features of each disorder in prose, avoid precise counts and cut-offs where the evidence does not support them, and leave more room for clinical judgement and cultural variation. ICD-11 also has a few categories of its own, among them complex post-traumatic stress disorder, gaming disorder and body integrity dysphoria, and it treats an acute stress reaction as a normal response to an extreme event rather than a disorder, coding it (QE84) outside the mental disorders chapter, in chapter 24, factors influencing health status or contact with health services. Finally, the codes. A DSM-5-TR code is not DSM's own: the manual borrows ICD-10-CM codes (F32.1, for example), because that is the official coding system in the United States. ICD-11 has its own alphanumeric codes, and those in its mental disorders chapter (chapter 06) begin with the digit 6. Table 2.34 sets the two side by side.

Table 2.34 DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 compared.
FeatureDSM-5-TRICD-11
PublisherAmerican Psychiatric AssociationWorld Health Organization
DateMarch 2022 (DSM-5 in 2013)Adopted May 2019 by the World Health Assembly; in effect 1 January 2022
ScopeMental disorders onlyAll diseases and health conditions; mental disorders are one chapter
StructureSection I, Basics; Section II, Diagnostic Criteria and Codes (20 disorder chapters and 2 chapters of other conditions); Section III, Emerging Measures and ModelsChapter 06, Mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders; sleep-wake disorders (chapter 07) and gender incongruence (chapter 17) sit outside it
Diagnostic styleExplicit criteria with counts and durationsEssential features in prose; fewer fixed cut-offs; more clinical judgement
CodesBorrowed ICD-10-CM codes, for example F32.1Own codes; chapter 06 codes begin with 6, for example 6A70.1
AccessSold as a book by the publisherFree online, in many languages
Main usersUnited States clinicians; research journals worldwideHealth records and statistics in WHO member countries
In IndiaTextbooks, research papers, entrance examinations, many private clinicsHospital case records, official statistics and the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (ICD-10 in most places, moving to ICD-11)
Coding Ravi

Suppose the clinician concludes that Ravi's present illness meets criteria for a moderate depressive episode and judges the brief sadness at 17 too mild to count as an earlier episode. In DSM-5-TR he would be recorded as having major depressive disorder, single episode, moderate (F32.1), perhaps with Z codes for unemployment (Z56.0) and a high expressed emotion level within the family (Z63.8). In ICD-11 the same judgement becomes single episode depressive disorder, moderate, without psychotic symptoms (6A70.1). If she decides that the episode at 17 was a genuine depressive episode, both codes change to their recurrent forms (F33.1 and 6A71.1). Until that question is settled, usually with the help of his mother's account of the episode at 17, the record keeps it open, which is why Section 2.3 called the provisional diagnosis "a depressive episode, possibly recurrent". Notice what none of these codes contains: the third failure, the cousin, the father's comparisons, the pledged gold. That is the work of the formulation in Section 2.9.

Indian context

Indian practice runs on both manuals at once. Government hospitals and national health statistics code with the ICD, still mostly ICD-10, with the move to ICD-11 gradual; many Indian case-record formats and psychiatry teaching programmes grew up around the ICD-10 "blue book" (Clinical Descriptions and Diagnostic Guidelines, 1992). Research journals, most textbooks and entrance examinations lean on DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR. The law points to the ICD: under Section 3 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (Chapter 1), mental illness is to be determined according to nationally or internationally accepted medical standards, including the latest edition of the WHO's International Classification of Diseases, as notified by the central government. The same diagnosis also opens the door to disability certification (Section 9.9).

Exam trap

ICD-11 has two dates: adopted in May 2019 by the 72nd World Health Assembly, in effect from 1 January 2022. If an option says "ICD-11, 2022", read it as the date it came into force. DSM-5-TR is also 2022 (March), and DSM-5 is 2013. ICD-10 (1992) put mental disorders in chapter V with codes beginning with F; ICD-11 puts them in chapter 06 with codes beginning with 6. DSM-5-TR has no codes of its own: it uses ICD-10-CM codes. And the ICD covers all of medicine; the DSM covers only mental disorders.

Reliability of diagnosis and Cohen's kappa

A classification is useful only if different clinicians, applying it to the same person, reach the same answer. This is the reliability of diagnosis, and in practice it means one of two things. Inter-rater reliability is agreement between two or more clinicians who judge the same information: two psychologists watch the same recorded interview, or one interviews while the other observes, and each records a diagnosis without consulting the other. Test-retest reliability is agreement between two assessments of the same person made at different times, usually by two clinicians who interview separately within a short interval. The second is the harder test. On top of the raters' own disagreements it adds the other sources of variance named in Section 2.1: the patient may tell the second interviewer a different story, or may genuinely have changed.

Test-retest designs rest on an assumption that examiners like to probe. They make sense only when the thing being measured ought to stay the same over the interval. A stable trait, such as intelligence or a personality characteristic, should give similar results a few weeks apart, so a low test-retest figure means a poor measure. A rapidly changing state, such as today's mood or the anxiety someone feels in the waiting room, is expected to change, so low test-retest agreement there says nothing against the measure. Diagnoses are meant to describe conditions that last for weeks or months, which is why diagnostic field trials can use two interviews up to two weeks apart.

How should agreement be measured? The obvious answer, the percentage of cases on which two clinicians agree, is misleading, because some agreement happens by pure chance. Two clinicians who each tossed a coin to decide "depressed" or "not depressed" would agree about half the time while knowing nothing about the patients. In 1960 the American psychologist and statistician Jacob Cohen (1923 to 1998) published a solution in the journal Educational and Psychological Measurement: a coefficient that measures how far the observed agreement exceeds the agreement chance alone would produce. Cohen's kappa (κ) is calculated as

κ = (po - pe) / (1 - pe)

where po is the observed proportion of agreement and pe is the proportion of agreement expected by chance, worked out from how often each rater uses each category. The top of the fraction is the agreement achieved beyond chance; the bottom is the largest amount of agreement beyond chance that was possible. Kappa is therefore 1 when agreement is perfect, 0 when agreement is no better than chance, and negative when the raters agree less often than chance would predict.

Kappa only clicks when you calculate one, so push a case through it. Two trainee clinical psychologists at a Mysuru outpatient clinic independently watch the same 100 recorded intake interviews, and for each client each decides whether a depressive disorder is present. Figure 2.13 shows their decisions and the three steps of the calculation.

Walk through the steps. The clinicians agreed on 40 "yes" and 40 "no" cases, so po = 80 / 100 = 0.80. Each said "yes" to 50 of the 100 clients. If their decisions were unconnected, they would both say "yes" by chance 0.50 × 0.50 = 0.25 of the time and both say "no" another 0.25 of the time, so pe = 0.50. Then κ = (0.80 - 0.50) / (1 - 0.50) = 0.30 / 0.50 = 0.60. Of all the agreement beyond chance that was possible, these two clinicians achieved 60 percent.

Now change one thing: the base rate. Suppose the same pair rate another 100 clients for a much rarer diagnosis. They agree that 5 clients have it and 85 do not, and they disagree on 10 (5 each way). Raw agreement has risen to 90 percent. But each clinician now says "yes" only 10 times in 100, so chance agreement is (0.10 × 0.10) + (0.90 × 0.90) = 0.82, and κ = (0.90 - 0.82) / (1 - 0.82) = 0.08 / 0.18 = 0.44. Higher raw agreement, lower kappa. When almost everyone is a "no", agreeing on "no" is easy and earns little credit, while the few disagreements on the rare "yes" cases weigh heavily. Kappa therefore depends on the base rate (the prevalence) of the condition in the sample. A diagnosis can look unreliable in a general clinic, where it is rare, and reliable in a specialist clinic, where it is common, without anything about the clinicians having changed.

Three relatives of Cohen's kappa are worth recognising. Weighted kappa (Cohen, 1968) is used for ordered categories such as mild, moderate and severe, and gives partial credit for near misses. Fleiss' kappa, after the statistician Joseph Fleiss (1971), extends the idea to more than two raters. And the intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC) measures agreement on continuous ratings such as severity scores; the DSM-5 field trials used an intraclass form of kappa for diagnoses and the ICC for severity ratings.

What counts as a "good" kappa? There is no law of nature here, only conventions, and Table 2.35 gives the two you are most likely to meet. The bands proposed by J. Richard Landis and Gary Koch (1977) are the most widely quoted. Joseph Fleiss offered a simpler set (above 0.75 excellent, 0.40 to 0.75 fair to good, below 0.40 poor). The DSM-5 field trials adopted their own, more lenient, bands.

Table 2.35 Two conventions for interpreting kappa. The cut-offs differ slightly, so read each pair of columns on its own.
Landis and Koch rangeLabelDSM-5 field-trial rangeLabel
Below 0Poor (worse than chance)Not separatedNot separated
0 to 0.20SlightBelow 0.20Unacceptable
0.21 to 0.40Fair0.20 to 0.39Questionable
0.41 to 0.60Moderate0.40 to 0.59Good
0.61 to 0.80Substantial0.60 to 0.79Very good
0.81 to 1.00Almost perfect0.80 and aboveExcellent

Those DSM-5 bands were needed, because the results were sobering. The DSM-5 field trials ran at 11 North American academic medical centres between December 2010 and October 2011 and used a deliberately demanding test-retest design: each patient was interviewed by two clinicians, separately and within two weeks, each blind to the other's diagnosis, using an ordinary clinical interview rather than a structured protocol. Post-traumatic stress disorder reached a kappa of 0.67 and autism spectrum disorder 0.69, and schizophrenia reached 0.46. But two of the commonest diagnoses in everyday practice did poorly: major depressive disorder 0.28 and generalised anxiety disorder 0.20 (Regier and colleagues, 2013). The task force argued that earlier field trials had used easier designs and had produced inflated figures; critics replied that relabelling a kappa of 0.28 as "questionable" rather than "fair" or "poor" lowered the bar. Either way, the lesson for practice is the one Section 2.1 drew: unstructured interviews let disagreement in, and research studies keep kappa high by using semi-structured interviews such as the SCID.

Reliability is not the whole story, however. For decades the first-rank symptoms described by Kurt Schneider (thought insertion, withdrawal and broadcasting, voices commenting on the patient, and other passivity experiences, taught in Section 2.5) were valued because clinicians could agree on whether they were present. But they also occur in mania and other conditions, so agreeing that they were present did not make them specific to schizophrenia, and DSM-5 dropped the special diagnostic weight DSM-IV had given them. Agreement, in other words, does not guarantee truth. That is the question of validity.

Exam trap

Kappa is a measure of agreement corrected for chance on categorical judgements, introduced by Jacob Cohen in 1960 (the same Cohen as Cohen's d). It runs from below 0 to +1; 0 means chance-level agreement, not "no diagnoses". Percentage agreement overstates reliability, especially for rare conditions. Do not confuse kappa with Cronbach's alpha (internal consistency of a test's items) or with Pearson's r (association: two raters who always differ by the same amount correlate perfectly yet never agree). Cohen's kappa is for two raters; Fleiss' kappa for more. Test-retest reliability is the right check for stable traits, not for rapidly changing states. And remember the pattern from the DSM-5 field trials: PTSD and autism high, schizophrenia middling, major depression and generalised anxiety low.

Validity and utility of a diagnosis

A diagnosis is reliable if clinicians agree on it. It has diagnostic validity if it is true, in the sense that the category picks out a real and distinct condition with its own features, causes, course and response to treatment. The two are linked in one direction only. A diagnosis that clinicians cannot agree on cannot be valid, because it is not picking out anything consistently, so reliability is necessary for validity. But it is not sufficient. Barlow and Durand give the cleanest example: if the only criterion for depression were hearing the patient say "I feel depressed" at some point in the interview, clinicians could agree perfectly, yet the category would be close to useless, because many people with other disorders, or with none, say that now and then. A system that chases reliability by narrowing its criteria to whatever is easy to agree on can lose validity in the process. This is the classification-level version of the trade-off between structured and unstructured interviews that you met in Section 2.1.

How would you show that a diagnosis is valid? In 1970 Robins and Guze answered with a five-phase method, using schizophrenia as their example: clinical description, laboratory studies, delimitation from other disorders, follow-up studies showing a characteristic course, and family studies showing that the disorder runs in families. A decade later Kenneth S. Kendler (1980) sorted such evidence by its timing into antecedent validators (familial aggregation, premorbid personality, precipitating factors), concurrent validators (psychological test findings, biological markers) and predictive validators (stability of the diagnosis over time, rates of relapse and recovery, response to treatment). When DSM-5 was prepared, each proposed change had to be supported by evidence organised around exactly these three kinds of validator. Textbooks also speak of construct validity (the criteria hang together and differ from those of other categories), predictive or criterion validity (the diagnosis forecasts course and treatment response) and content validity (the criteria capture what experts mean by the disorder).

By these standards most psychiatric diagnoses are only partly validated. Few have a laboratory test; the heterogeneity and comorbidity described earlier suggest that some boundaries are not where nature put them; and genetic studies find many of the same risk factors shared across several disorders. That is why the British psychiatrist Robert Evan Kendell (1935 to 2002) and Assen Jablensky (2003) urged clinicians to separate validity from clinical utility, the usefulness of a category for communication, prediction and choosing treatment. Most DSM and ICD categories, they argued, have not been shown to be discrete entities separated by natural boundaries, yet many of them are highly useful. The danger lies in forgetting the difference. Reification is treating a convenient grouping of observations as if it were a thing: saying that a man hears voices "because he has schizophrenia", when "schizophrenia" was only the name given to his hearing voices and the rest. One research response is to step outside the categories altogether: the Research Domain Criteria (RDoC), a framework of the United States National Institute of Mental Health, studies dimensions of functioning such as threat, reward and cognitive control from genes and circuits up to behaviour, without starting from DSM diagnoses. It is a research framework, not a manual for the clinic.

Exam trap

Reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity: a diagnosis can be reliable and invalid, but it cannot be valid and unreliable. Reliability asks "do we agree?"; validity asks "is what we agree on real and meaningful?". The five-phase validation method (clinical description, laboratory studies, delimitation, follow-up, family studies) is Robins and Guze (1970); antecedent, concurrent and predictive validators are Kendler (1980); utility versus validity is Kendell and Jablensky (2003). RDoC belongs to the US National Institute of Mental Health and is not a diagnostic manual.

The costs of labelling: Rosenhan and after

A diagnosis does more than describe. Once written, it travels: into the case file, the insurance form, the family's conversations and the client's picture of himself. It shapes what the next clinician expects to see and what the neighbours say. This is the problem of labelling, the process by which a diagnostic name, once applied, changes how others perceive and treat a person and how the person sees himself. The study that made labelling famous is the one that opened this chapter.

Key study: Rosenhan, "On being sane in insane places" (Science, 1973)

The question. Can psychiatric staff tell the sane from the insane, and what does a label do once it has been applied? The method. As the chapter opening described, eight sane people, Rosenhan among them, sought admission to 12 hospitals in five states, each reporting the same unclear voice saying "empty", "hollow" and "thud", and then behaved normally. Methodologically it was a field study using disguised participant observation. The results. All were admitted, all but one with a diagnosis of schizophrenia; they stayed 7 to 52 days (an average of 19) and were discharged with schizophrenia "in remission". Staff read ordinary behaviour through the label. Contact was minimal: when a pseudopatient courteously asked a staff member a question, psychiatrists moved on with head averted 71 percent of the time, and nurses and attendants 88 percent of the time, and daily contact with psychiatrists, psychologists and physicians averaged under seven minutes. The pseudopatients were handed nearly 2,100 pills, almost all of which they pocketed or flushed away.

The follow-up. The staff of a research and teaching hospital who had heard of the findings doubted that such errors could happen in their hospital. Rosenhan told them that during the next three months one or more pseudopatients would try to gain admission, and every staff member rated each new patient on the likelihood of being an impostor. Of 193 patients judged, 41 were confidently identified as pseudopatients by at least one staff member, 23 were suspected by at least one psychiatrist, and 19 by a psychiatrist and one other staff member. Rosenhan had sent no one. The conclusion he drew was that diagnoses depend on the setting and on the label more than on the patient, and that a label, once applied, is "sticky" and colours everything the person does afterwards.

The paper caused an uproar, and the most influential reply came from Robert Spitzer, who by then was chairing the task force preparing DSM-III. In a 1975 critique in the Journal of Abnormal Psychology, Spitzer argued that the study showed something quite different from what Rosenhan claimed. A diagnosis rests largely on what the patient reports, and a patient who lies convincingly can fool any diagnostician in any branch of medicine. He quoted an analogy from the psychiatrist Seymour S. Kety (1915 to 2000): if Kety drank a quart of blood, hid the fact and arrived at an emergency room vomiting blood, the staff would diagnose and treat a bleeding peptic ulcer, and nobody would conclude from this that medicine cannot diagnose ulcers. A person who asks to be admitted because he is hearing voices is reasonably treated as psychotic until there is evidence otherwise. Spitzer also turned the discharge diagnosis around. In the hospital records he checked, "schizophrenia, in remission" was a rare discharge diagnosis, so its use for the pseudopatients suggested that the staff had in fact noticed that they showed no symptoms. And the follow-up, he argued, showed only that staff warned to expect impostors became readier to suspect one; since no impostor came, nobody could say how accurate they were.

For decades the argument rested there: a famous finding and a famous rebuttal. Then the journalist Susannah Cahalan, whose own autoimmune brain inflammation had at first been mistaken for a psychiatric illness (she told that story in Brain on Fire, 2012), went looking for the pseudopatients. Her book The Great Pretender (2019) reported serious problems. Working through Rosenhan's private papers, she could trace only one of the other seven pseudopatients in the paper, a graduate student, plus one further volunteer, Harry Lando, who had found his hospital stay a positive experience and whose data had been left out. Rosenhan's own hospital record (he was admitted to Haverford State Hospital in Pennsylvania under the name "David Lurie") described a man who said he had been putting copper over his ears to keep the voices out and wanted admission because the hospital would be "better insulated": far more than the single unclear voice reported in Science. Cahalan raised the possibility that some of the pseudopatients never existed. In 2023 the historian of psychiatry Andrew Scull, writing in History of Psychiatry, called the study a "successful scientific fraud". Rosenhan died in 2012 and cannot answer, but a classic study whose data cannot be checked can no longer carry the weight it once did.

Does the collapse of Rosenhan's study mean that labels are harmless? No. The effect he claimed has been shown in better-controlled work. In 1974 Ellen J. Langer (born 1947) and Robert Abelson showed clinicians the same videotape of a young man talking about his past jobs. Half were told he was a "job applicant" and half that he was a "patient". Psychoanalytically oriented clinicians described the "patient" as considerably more disturbed than the identical "job applicant", whereas behaviourally trained clinicians, whose training directed them to the observable behaviour, rated him much the same under both labels. The label changed what some trained observers saw. A related bias is diagnostic overshadowing, a term introduced by Steven Reiss and his colleagues in 1982 after they found that clinicians were less likely to recognise emotional problems in a person labelled as having intellectual disability: the existing label explained everything away. Today the term is used most often for physical illness missed in people who carry a psychiatric diagnosis, whose chest pain or breathlessness is put down to "anxiety" or "his illness".

Sociologists had argued for some time that labels do more than bias observers: they help to create the behaviour they name. Edwin M. Lemert (1912 to 1996), in Social Pathology (1951), distinguished primary deviance, the original rule-breaking, which may be brief and have many causes, from secondary deviance, which develops after a person has been publicly labelled and begins to organise his life and identity around the label. Thomas J. Scheff (1929 to 2025) applied the idea to mental illness in Being Mentally Ill: A Sociological Theory (1966), the classic statement of labelling theory. Scheff called the symptoms of mental illness residual rule-breaking: violations of rules so taken for granted that they have no other name, such as talking to no one or staring fixedly into space. Most residual rule-breaking, he argued, is common, passes unnoticed and fades. But when it is noticed and officially labelled, typically in a crisis, the person is cast in the stereotyped role of "mental patient" that everyone learns in childhood; he is rewarded for playing the role and punished for trying to leave it, and the label becomes the main cause of a lasting career of mental illness. Erving Goffman (1922 to 1982) described that career from the inside in Asylums (1961), based on fieldwork at a large mental hospital in Washington, D.C. A total institution, in which every part of life is lived in one place under one authority, strips the newcomer of his former identity through a series of "mortifications of the self". The psychiatrist Thomas Stephen Szasz (1920 to 2012) went furthest in The Myth of Mental Illness (1961), arguing that "mental illness" is a metaphor for problems in living and that psychiatric labels serve social control.

Labelling theory was a valuable corrective, but it overstated its case. The sociologist Walter Gove (1970) reviewed the evidence and concluded that most people are labelled because they are already seriously disturbed, that families usually resist the label for a long time before seeking help, and that treatment more often helps than entrenches illness; labelling could not explain why the rule-breaking began. Bruce Link and his colleagues (1989) proposed a more defensible modified labelling theory. Everyone grows up absorbing the culture's beliefs that psychiatric patients are dangerous and incompetent and will be rejected. When a person is later diagnosed, those beliefs suddenly apply to him. Expecting rejection, he may keep his treatment secret, withdraw from friends or stop applying for jobs, and these ways of coping themselves narrow his life. Labelling, on this view, does not cause the illness; it adds a second burden to it.

That second burden is stigma. Goffman, in Stigma (1963), defined it as an attribute that is deeply discrediting, one that reduces a person in others' eyes "from a whole and usual person to a tainted, discounted one". Barlow and Durand, following Stephen Hinshaw, describe it as a combination of negative stereotypes, prejudices and attitudes that reduces the life chances of the devalued group. Patrick Corrigan's research distinguishes public stigma, the prejudice and discrimination of the community, from self-stigma, in which the person comes to agree with the stereotype and applies it to himself, losing self-esteem and showing what Corrigan called the "why try" effect: "Why try for that job? Someone like me won't get it." History also shows labels used deliberately as weapons. In 1851 the American physician Samuel Cartwright described "drapetomania", a supposed disease that made enslaved people run away, and in the Soviet Union political dissidents were confined in psychiatric hospitals under diagnoses such as "sluggish schizophrenia". The DSM-5-TR rule that political, religious or sexual deviance is not in itself a disorder is meant to guard against exactly such abuses.

None of this means that clinicians should stop diagnosing. A label can also bring relief ("so this has a name, and I am not simply weak"), entry to treatment, insurance cover, leave from work, disability benefits and legal protection, and it connects the clinician to the evidence on what helps. Reviewing the labelling research, John Ruscio (2004) concluded that harm is not an automatic consequence of diagnosis; it depends a great deal on how the diagnosis is used and conveyed. Three habits reduce the cost. Use person-first language ("a man with schizophrenia", not "a schizophrenic"), because a disorder is something a person has, not what he is. Treat every diagnosis as a working hypothesis, open to revision when the evidence changes, which is exactly the habit the pseudopatients' clinicians lacked. And pair the diagnosis with a formulation (Section 2.9) that describes the person's story, context and strengths, not only his category.

Clinical link

Giving a diagnosis is a skill in its own right. When the clinician tells Ravi and his mother that he has depression, she explains what the word means and what it does not mean (common, treatable, not "madness" and not weakness of character), links it to their own words ("what doctors call depression is the heaviness and the 3 a.m. waking you described"), asks what they now understand and what they fear (a "mental case" label, gossip in the village, his sister's marriage), and moves straight to the formulation and the plan. A label delivered this way opens a door; the same label written on a form and handed over in silence can close one.

Indian context

In India the cost of a psychiatric label often falls on the whole family. Families conceal illness to protect the marriage prospects of the patient and of his siblings, and a diagnosis disclosed at the wrong moment can end an engagement. Indian law tries to limit the damage. Section 3 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (Chapter 1) provides that mental illness must not be determined on the basis of a person's political, economic or social status, membership of a cultural, racial or religious group, or non-conformity with the moral, social, cultural, work or political values or religious beliefs of the community; that past treatment or hospitalisation does not by itself justify any present or future determination of mental illness; and that a determination of mental illness does not by itself mean that the person is of unsound mind unless a competent court has declared so. Read the clause on past treatment (section 3(4)) against Rosenhan's "schizophrenia, in remission": the Act rules out exactly the sticky label he described.

Exam trap

Rosenhan, "On being sane in insane places", Science, 1973: eight pseudopatients, 12 hospitals, a disguised participant observation design; in the follow-up, 41 of 193 genuine patients were confidently judged impostors although none had been sent. The best-known critique is Spitzer (1975); the "quart of blood" analogy he quoted is Kety's. The later doubts are Cahalan's The Great Pretender (2019) and Scull's 2023 charge of fraud. Labelling theory and "residual rule-breaking" are Scheff (1966); primary and secondary deviance are Lemert (1951); total institutions are Goffman's Asylums (1961) and "spoiled identity" his Stigma (1963); The Myth of Mental Illness is Szasz (1961). Modified labelling theory is Link (1989), and diagnostic overshadowing comes from Reiss and colleagues (1982).

The case of Rosenhan's pseudopatients, revisited

Go back to the admissions office in Pennsylvania where this chapter began, and to the question it left open: was the problem the label, or the way the information behind the label was gathered and interpreted? Section 2.10.7 showed that the study itself can no longer settle the matter. Robert Spitzer replied in 1975 that a patient who lies convincingly can fool a diagnostician in any branch of medicine, and that the rare discharge diagnosis "schizophrenia, in remission" suggests the staff had in fact noticed that the pseudopatients showed no symptoms. In The Great Pretender (2019) Susannah Cahalan could trace only one of the other seven pseudopatients, found a volunteer whose data had been left out, and found that Rosenhan's own hospital record described far more than the single unclear voice reported in Science. A classic study whose data cannot be checked cannot carry the weight it once did. Its warning survives anyway, because a label can change what trained observers see (Langer and Abelson, 1974), and because every tool in this chapter exists to stop a clinician from hearing one symptom, reaching for one label and reading everything afterwards through it.

Run the chapter's tools over a man who says a voice is saying "thud" and the difference is plain. The case history (Section 2.3) would ask when he was last completely well and find a lone symptom with no course and no previous episode behind it, and a premorbid personality that matched the man in the chair. The MSE (Sections 2.4 to 2.6) would describe rather than diagnose: ordinary appearance, speech, affect and cognition, with one isolated perceptual complaint recorded in his own words. Explicit criteria and a semi-structured interview such as the SCID would narrow criterion and information variance (Section 2.1), and kappa would measure how far two clinicians agreed beyond chance (Section 2.10). First's steps of differential diagnosis (Section 2.9) begin by asking whether the person is feigning his symptoms, which is exactly what the pseudopatients were doing. The psychiatrist who saw "oral-acquisitive" illness in a queue outside the cafeteria broke the report writer's rule of separating observation from inference, and Kent and colleagues (1974) showed that expectation distorts global impressions more than specific behaviour codes (Section 2.8). Above all, a formulation treats every conclusion, a diagnosis included, as a hypothesis to be revised. Ravi's record says "a depressive episode, possibly recurrent" and keeps the question of the month at 17 open on purpose; the pseudopatients' clinicians closed theirs at the first symptom.

Chapter summary

2.1 The Clinical Interview: Purposes, Types and Structure. The interview is the core tool of clinical assessment. Interviews differ first by purpose (intake, case history, diagnostic, mental status, crisis and termination), and Harry Stack Sullivan (1954), who saw the clinician as a participant observer, divided them into formal inception, reconnaissance, detailed inquiry and termination. They differ second by structure. Unstructured interviews are flexible but unreliable: in 1962 Aaron Beck's group found psychiatrists agreeing on the specific diagnosis only about half the time, and Spitzer, Endicott and Robins (1975) named five sources of disagreement, of which criterion variance was the largest. The SCID-5 (First, Williams, Karg and Spitzer), Wing's Present State Examination, the WHO's SCAN and the K-SADS are semi-structured; the DIS and CIDI are fully structured. Structure buys reliability but not validity, so practice blends an open start with a semi-structured check.

2.2 The Craft of Interviewing. Allen Ivey's microcounselling (from 1968) broke interviewing into teachable microskills, and Carl Rogers's person-centred tradition runs through them. Rapport rests on attending behaviour, which Gerard Egan summarised as SOLER (1975). Open questions ("Tell me about ...") generate the story and closed questions pin down details, combined in the funnel technique. Minimal encouragers (furthering) keep the client talking; paraphrasing restates content in new words; reflection of feeling, the signature technique of client-centred therapy, names the emotion; summarising draws the threads together. Clarification, gentle confrontation of discrepancies, sparing interpretation and silence follow, and Egan separated primary from advanced empathy. Common errors include leading, double-barrelled and "why" questions and Thorndike's halo effect (1920). Children, older adults, resistant and suicidal clients need adapted methods, and culture enters through Kleinman's explanatory model (1980), idioms of distress and trained interpreters.

2.3 The Case History. The case history is the longitudinal story of the person and the illness, an approach owed most to Adolf Meyer's psychobiology. It follows a standard order: identifying data; the informants and the reliability of their accounts; chief complaints in the patient's own words, with duration; the history of present illness, with onset (abrupt, acute, subacute or insidious), course, progress, precipitating factors and treatment; past psychiatric and medical history; family history, drawn as a genogram of at least three generations (popularised by Murray Bowen) and distinct from Hartman's ecomap and Moreno's sociogram; personal history, from birth and development through education, occupation and sexual and marital life to substance use and forensic history; and premorbid personality, the person before the illness. The history of Ravi, a 24-year-old in a shared rented room in Mysuru whose mother is staying with him, already suggests a depressive episode, possibly recurrent.

2.4 The Mental Status Examination I: Appearance, Behaviour, Speech, Mood and Affect. The mental status examination is a cross-sectional photograph beside the history's film: a description, not a diagnosis, in the vocabulary of Karl Jaspers' General Psychopathology (1913), which taught that form matters more than content. Appearance and behaviour come first, hallucinatory behaviour included and everything read against culture, then attitude to the examiner and eye contact. Psychomotor activity covers agitation, retardation and catatonia, which Kahlbaum described in 1874 and DSM-5-TR treats as twelve signs, three needed, no longer tied to schizophrenia. Speech is recorded for rate, quantity, volume, reaction time, articulation and coherence; pressure of speech is not flight of ideas. Mood is what the patient reports and affect what the clinician observes, restricted, blunted or flat in increasing severity, labile or inappropriate. Anhedonia (Ribot, 1896) and alexithymia (Sifneos, 1973) complete the vocabulary.

2.5 The Mental Status Examination II: Thought and Perception. Thought is known only through speech, as Nancy Andreasen stressed. British texts use four headings: stream (flight of ideas, retardation, thought block, perseveration), form (Bleuler's loosening of associations, derailment, tangentiality, circumstantiality, which reaches its goal, word salad, neologisms), content and possession; American texts merge stream and form as thought process. Delusions, Jaspers' "basic characteristic of madness", are held with extraordinary conviction, impervious to argument and false; they may be primary or secondary and are named by theme, from persecution and reference to Capgras and Cotard. Wernicke's overvalued ideas are understandable, and obsessions are recognised as one's own. Thought insertion, withdrawal, broadcasting and the passivity experiences belong to Kurt Schneider's eleven first-rank symptoms. Perception covers illusions (first separated from hallucinations by Esquirol), hallucinations by modality, special varieties, pseudohallucinations, and depersonalisation and derealisation.

2.6 The Mental Status Examination III: Cognition, Judgement, Insight and the Written MSE. Cognition is examined in a fixed order because each function rests on those below it: consciousness (Glasgow Coma Scale 3 to 15); orientation, lost in the order time, place, person, which points to organic causes; attention and concentration (digit span, serial sevens or serial threes); memory, immediate, recent and remote, with Ribot's law, Korsakoff's confabulation and depressive pseudodementia; intelligence; abstraction (proverbs and the fairer similarities); and judgement, personal, social and test. Insight, "a correct attitude to a morbid change in oneself" (Aubrey Lewis, 1934), runs in six grades from complete denial to true emotional insight, and its lack was the commonest finding in the WHO's International Pilot Study of Schizophrenia. The MMSE (Folstein, Folstein and McHugh, 1975) is a 30-point screen, not the MSE, which is written by describing first and labelling afterwards.

2.7 Risk Assessment: Suicide and Violence. Asking directly about suicide does not plant the idea (Gould and colleagues, 2005); the questions climb a ladder from passive ideation through intent, plan, means and preparation, and past attempts are always explored. A previous attempt is the strongest single predictor and hopelessness the strongest psychological one. Durkheim (1897) described four types of suicide, Joiner's interpersonal theory (2005) explains who acts on the wish to die, and Linehan's Reasons for Living Inventory (1983) measures protection. The SAD PERSONS mnemonic (Patterson and colleagues, 1983) is a checklist, not a score: its weights are equal, its items mostly static, and Pokorny (1983) showed that the low base rate defeats prediction. Modern practice formulates risk, targets dynamic factors and writes a safety plan. For violence, past violence predicts best, most people with mental illness are not violent, and structured professional judgement (the HCR-20) joins actuarial tools.

2.8 Behavioural Assessment. Behavioural assessment treats a response as a sample of behaviour, not a sign of an underlying trait (Goldfried and Kent, 1972), backed by Walter Mischel's (1968) evidence that behaviour varies with the situation. Its core is functional analysis, Skinner's term, built on operant conditioning: antecedent, behaviour and consequence, extended by Kanfer and Saslow (1965) into excesses, deficits and assets and later into SORKC. Ravi's withdrawal is maintained by negative reinforcement, and Mowrer's neurotic paradox explains why short-term relief outweighs long-term cost. The behavioural interview defines target behaviours that pass Lindsley's dead man test. Self-monitoring is reactive, usually therapeutically. Observation is naturalistic, analogue (Lang and Lazovik's behavioural avoidance test, 1963) or participant; it is recorded by event, duration, latency, interval or momentary time sampling, and checked by inter-observer agreement against reactivity, observer drift and expectancy bias. Role play and Lang's three response systems complete it.

2.9 Case Formulation. A case formulation is, in Tracy Eells's (1997) definition, a hypothesis about the causes, precipitants and maintaining influences of a person's problems, and treatment follows from it. The 5 Ps ask what the problem is (presenting), why this person (predisposing), why now (precipitating), why it continues (perpetuating, the main target of treatment) and what helps (protective). George Engel's biopsychosocial model (1977) crosses these with biological, psychological and social levels. The cognitive-behavioural formulation, pioneered by Jacqueline Persons (1989), uses Padesky and Mooney's five-part model and Judith Beck's cognitive conceptualisation diagram (1995); the psychodynamic formulation follows the four parts of Perry, Cooper and Michels (1987). Diagnosis is nomothetic and categorical, formulation idiographic and explanatory. Differential diagnosis rules out malingering, substances and medical causes first. Reports answer the referral question and avoid the Barnum effect (Meehl, 1956), and feedback can itself help (Finn and Tonsager, 1992).

2.10 Classification as a Clinical Tool. Clinicians classify to communicate, predict, choose treatment, do research, administer services and understand causes. DSM and ICD are categorical systems with polythetic, prototypical criteria and some dimensional additions. DSM-III (1980), produced under Spitzer and growing out of the Feighner criteria, brought explicit criteria and the multiaxial system, which DSM-5 (2013) removed; homosexuality was voted out in 1973. DSM-5-TR (2022, American Psychiatric Association) and ICD-11 (adopted May 2019, in effect 1 January 2022, chapter 06) are now closely aligned. Cohen's kappa (1960) corrects agreement for chance; in the DSM-5 field trials major depression reached only 0.28. Reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity, and utility differs from validity (Kendell and Jablensky, 2003). Rosenhan's 1973 study, challenged by Spitzer and Cahalan, proves less than once thought, but labelling theory (Lemert, Scheff, Goffman) and stigma still argue for person-first language.

From the past papers

  1. A structured interview commonly used in clinical psychology is: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) MMSE (b) SCID (c) WAIS (d) TAT

    Answer: (b) SCID. The Structured Clinical Interview for DSM walks the clinician through the diagnostic criteria module by module (strictly it is semi-structured, because it needs clinical judgement). The MMSE is a 30-point cognitive screening test, not a diagnostic interview and not the MSE; the WAIS is an intelligence scale and the TAT a projective technique.

  2. The Vineland-3, K-SADS, ADI-R, and ADOS-2 are best classified as: (practice mock)

    (a) EEG paradigms (b) Personality projective techniques (c) Self-report cognitive tests (d) Structured / semi-structured interviews and observation instruments used in developmental and psychiatric assessment (Vineland-3 adaptive, K-SADS childhood psychiatric, ADI-R + ADOS-2 autism)

    Answer: (d). The K-SADS is a semi-structured interview of parent and child, the ADI-R a caregiver interview for autism, the ADOS-2 a standardised play-based observation and the Vineland-3 an adaptive-behaviour interview with a caregiver. None records brain activity, none is projective, and none is a self-report test.

  3. Which of the following techniques is a client centred therapist most likely to use? (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Analysis (b) Giving advice (c) Reflection (d) Aversive conditioning

    Answer: (c) Reflection. Reflection of feeling is the signature technique of client-centred therapy: Rogers held that accurately reflecting the client's inner experience let the client hear himself and grow. Analysis belongs to psychoanalysis, giving advice is a directive move Rogers deliberately avoided, and aversive conditioning belongs to behaviour therapy.

  4. Leaning forward, saying umm, nodding, are examples of which one of the following interview techniques? (RML 2020)

    (a) Paraphrasing (b) Prompting (c) Furthering (d) Checking

    Answer: (c) Furthering. These minimal encouragers, called furthering responses in social-work interviewing texts, keep the client talking without changing the subject. Paraphrasing restates content in new words, prompting is a specific cue that steers the client to a topic, and checking asks the client to confirm the interviewer's understanding.

  5. 'Tell me about' is an example of: (RML 2020)

    (a) an open-ended question (b) a close-ended question (c) an ethnographic interviewing technique (d) theory-guided inquiry

    Answer: (a) An open-ended question. It leaves the content and length of the answer entirely to the client. Spradley's ethnographic "grand tour" questions ("Tell me about a typical day") use the same move, but the question is still open; theory-guided inquiry describes where questions come from, not whether they are open or closed.

  6. When a counsellor repeats what the client says, but uses words that are different than what the client used, the counsellor is using what skill? (RML 2021)

    (a) Paraphrasing (b) Questioning (c) Empathy (d) Nonverbal communication

    Answer: (a) Paraphrasing. Paraphrasing (restatement, reflection of content) feeds back the essence of what was said in the interviewer's own words; repeating it word for word is parroting. Questioning asks for new information, empathy is the broader capacity that a paraphrase is one way of showing, and nonverbal communication is posture, gesture and tone.

  7. Anhedonia, a core feature of major depression, is defined as: (practice mock)

    (a) Excessive guilt (b) Markedly diminished interest or pleasure in nearly all activities (c) Psychomotor agitation (d) Difficulty concentrating

    Answer: (b). Ribot's term (1896) means the loss of the capacity to experience pleasure. Excessive guilt is a disturbance of thought content, psychomotor agitation a motor sign and difficulty concentrating a cognitive symptom; all four can occur in the same depressive episode, which is why they make tempting distractors.

  8. Circumstantiality is a disorder of: (Recall set)

    (a) Possession of thought (b) Content of thought (c) Form of thought (thought process) (d) Perception

    Answer: (c) Form of thought. The circumstantial patient takes a long detour through needless detail but reaches the point, a disturbance of how ideas are organised. It says nothing about what he believes (content), his thoughts feel his own (possession), and nothing is misperceived. British texts following Fish file it under stream; choose stream only when form is not offered.

  9. First-rank symptoms of schizophrenia (Schneider) most prominently include: (practice mock)

    (a) Avolition and flat affect (b) Memory deficits and disorientation to person (c) Thought insertion, thought withdrawal, thought broadcasting, made feelings/impulses, somatic passivity, third-person auditory hallucinations (d) Catatonic excitement

    Answer: (c). Schneider's list centres on thought alienation, passivity experiences, delusional perception and three kinds of auditory hallucination. Avolition and flat affect are negative symptoms, memory deficits and disorientation are cognitive impairments that point to organic disease (which Schneider's rule excluded), and catatonic excitement is a motor sign.

  10. In the Mental Status Examination (MSE), 'insight' refers to: (practice mock)

    (a) The client's orientation to time, place, and person (b) The client's ability to do serial subtraction (c) The client's awareness and understanding of their own illness (d) The presence of hallucinations

    Answer: (c). Insight is "a correct attitude to a morbid change in oneself" (Aubrey Lewis, 1934), graded from complete denial to true emotional insight. Orientation is a separate cognitive heading, serial subtraction tests concentration, and hallucinations are recorded under perception.

  11. What is assessed in the Insight category of the cognitive assessment? (practice mock)

    (a) Orientation to time, place, and person (b) Level of consciousness (c) Degree of awareness regarding one's illness (d) Immediate retention and recall

    Answer: (c). The distractors are the other cognitive headings, in the order they are examined: consciousness comes first because every later test assumes an alert patient, orientation next, and immediate retention and recall is registration within seconds, tested with the digit span. Insight closes the examination.

  12. Functional analysis is a therapy based on: (RML 2022)

    (a) Classical conditioning (b) Humanistic principles (c) Operant conditioning (d) Psychodynamic principles

    Answer: (c) Operant conditioning. Functional analysis examines the antecedents, the behaviour and above all the consequences that maintain it, Skinner's three-term contingency. Classical conditioning can explain how a fear was acquired but not what keeps a behaviour going; humanistic and psychodynamic principles have nothing to do with the ABC.

  13. In the context of psychopathology, a "formulation" differs from a "diagnosis" in that a formulation primarily aims to: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Classify the client's symptoms according to a standardized nosological system like DSM-5. (b) Provide a hypothesis about the cause, precipitants, and maintaining factors of an individual's difficulties. (c) Determine the prognosis and likely course of the disorder over time. (d) Rule out organic causes for the presenting psychological symptoms.

    Answer: (b). This is Eells's (1997) definition of a case formulation. Classifying symptoms in a nosological system is diagnosis; prognosis and course are a use of diagnosis (and of formulation, but not its primary aim); ruling out organic causes is part of differential diagnosis.

  14. Test-retest reliability is most appropriate for: (practice mock)

    (a) Measures of rapidly changing state (b) Stable trait measures (c) Single-item measures only (d) Open-ended interviews only

    Answer: (b) Stable trait measures. A test-retest design makes sense only when the thing measured ought to stay the same over the interval, as intelligence or a personality characteristic should. A state such as today's mood is expected to change, so low agreement there says nothing against the measure; the other two options are arbitrary restrictions.

  15. What do researchers mean by pathologization and depathologization of homosexuality? (practice mock)

    (a) None of the above (b) The classification of homosexuality as a mental disorder and the removal of this classification (c) The changing of sexual orientation from homosexual to heterosexual (d) The process of coming out as a gay or lesbian individual

    Answer: (b). The American Psychiatric Association's Board of Trustees voted in December 1973 to remove homosexuality as such from DSM, a residual category lasted until DSM-III-R (1987), and the WHO removed it with ICD-10. Changing orientation is so-called conversion therapy, and coming out has nothing to do with classification.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Diagnostic interviews run from unstructured through semi-structured (SCID-5, PSE and SCAN, K-SADS, ADIS-5: standard questions, but the clinician probes and judges) to fully structured (DIS, CIDI: fixed wording, usable by lay interviewers); structure buys reliability, not validity. asked twice
  2. In the MSE, insight is the patient's awareness and understanding of his own illness, "a correct attitude to a morbid change in oneself" (Aubrey Lewis, 1934), graded from 1 (complete denial) to 6 (true emotional insight); it is not orientation, consciousness, immediate recall, serial subtraction or hallucinations. asked twice
  3. The SCID (SCID-5: First, Williams, Karg and Spitzer, 2015 to 2016) is the structured diagnostic interview of clinical psychology; strictly it is semi-structured, and the DIS and CIDI are the truly fully structured ones. asked once
  4. The MMSE (Folstein, Folstein and McHugh, 1975) is a 30-point cognitive screening test, not a diagnostic interview and not the MSE; the WAIS is an intelligence scale and the TAT a projective technique. asked once
  5. Circumstantiality is a disorder of the form of thought (thought process): a detour through needless detail that reaches the goal, whereas tangentiality never does; it is not content, possession or perception. asked once
  6. A case formulation is a hypothesis about the causes, precipitants and maintaining influences of a person's difficulties (Tracy Eells, 1997): idiographic, explanatory and revisable. asked once
  7. Classifying symptoms in a nosological system such as DSM-5 is diagnosis (nomothetic, categorical); ruling out organic causes is part of differential diagnosis; prognosis is a use of diagnosis, not the primary aim of a formulation. asked once
  8. Functional analysis is based on operant conditioning: antecedent, behaviour and the consequences that maintain it, Skinner's three-term contingency; not classical conditioning, humanistic or psychodynamic principles. asked once
  9. Reflection of feeling is the signature technique of client-centred therapy (Carl Rogers); analysis is psychoanalytic, giving advice is directive, and aversive conditioning is behaviour therapy. asked once
  10. Leaning forward, nodding and "umm" are furthering (minimal encouragers); prompting is a cue that steers to a topic, and checking asks the client to confirm the interviewer's understanding. asked once
  11. Paraphrasing (restatement, reflection of content) repeats the essence of what the client said in different words; word-for-word repetition is parroting, and empathy is the broader capacity a paraphrase shows. asked once
  12. "Tell me about ..." is an open-ended question; Spradley's "grand tour" questions (The Ethnographic Interview, 1979) use the same form, and theory-guided inquiry describes where a question comes from, not its type. asked once
  13. Kurt Schneider's first-rank symptoms: thought insertion, withdrawal and broadcasting; made feelings, impulses and acts; somatic passivity; delusional perception; audible thoughts, voices arguing or discussing, and running commentary. asked once
  14. Not first-rank symptoms: avolition and flat affect (negative symptoms), memory deficits and disorientation (organic), catatonic excitement (motor), depersonalisation, second-person voices and delusional mood. asked once
  15. Anhedonia (Ribot, 1896) is markedly diminished interest or pleasure in nearly all activities, one of the two core symptoms of a major depressive episode; not guilt (thought content), agitation (motor) or poor concentration (cognitive). asked once
  16. Test-retest reliability suits stable traits such as intelligence; low test-retest agreement for a rapidly changing state such as today's mood says nothing against the measure. asked once
  17. The K-SADS (semi-structured, parent and child; K-SADS-PL), ADI-R (caregiver autism interview), ADOS-2 (play-based observation) and Vineland-3 (caregiver adaptive-behaviour interview) are structured or semi-structured interview and observation instruments. asked once
  18. Pathologisation is classifying homosexuality as a mental disorder and depathologisation removing it: the American Psychiatric Association voted in December 1973, a residual category lasted until DSM-III-R (1987), and ICD-10 removed it. asked once
  19. Serial sevens (100, 93, 86, 79, 72, 65) tests concentration, not insight; for a person with little schooling use serial threes, counting backwards, or days or months backwards. asked once
  20. The cognitive MSE starts with consciousness and then orientation; immediate memory (registration within seconds, the digit span) differs from recent memory (three words after five minutes) and remote memory (years). asked once
